### [1] [Domain-decomposed Evolutional Deep Neural Network with Random Features for Transient Pressure Diffusion with Discontinuous and High-Contrast Coefficients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22349)
*Peiqi Li,Jie Chen,Hui Zhang,Simon Hands*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: DD RF-EDNN separates spatial approximation from temporal evolution for transient pressure diffusion in heterogeneous porous media, achieving 9.54e-4 final-time relative L2 error in the principal benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transient pressure diffusion in heterogeneous porous media is hard to resolve efficiently when permeability is discontinuous and spans orders of magnitude.

Method: Domain-decomposed random-feature evolutional deep neural network (DD RF-EDNN). Permeability-informed random features are compressed into an orthonormal pressure space; a conservative finite-volume operator is projected onto this space so only reduced coordinates evolve in time. Retains dissipative structure without repeated NN optimization. Includes error analysis of dictionary and singular-value truncation, proof of contractivity under an energy-consistent dissipativity correction, and a conditional grid-level estimate.

Result: Final-time relative L2 error of 9.54e-4 in the principal benchmark; errors on the order of 1e-2 at permeability contrast of 1e3. Consistent accuracy across low-permeability inclusions, high-conductivity channels/blocks, and 3D grids. Numerical diagnostics match the analysis.

Conclusion: DD RF-EDNN is a structure-preserving and interpretable reduced formulation for transient simulations on prescribed heterogeneous media.

Abstract: Transient pressure diffusion in heterogeneous porous media becomes difficult to resolve efficiently when permeability is discontinuous and spans several orders of magnitude. We develop a domain-decomposed random-feature evolutional deep neural network (DD RF-EDNN) that separates spatial approximation from temporal evolution. Permeability-informed random features are compressed into an orthonormal pressure space, and a conservative finite-volume operator is projected onto this space so that only the reduced coordinates are advanced in time. This formulation retains the dissipative structure of the discrete flow problem without repeated neural-network optimization. The analysis quantifies dictionary and singular-value truncation errors, proves contractivity under an energy-consistent dissipativity correction, and derives a conditional grid-level estimate for homogeneous dynamics and time-independent data admitting a steady lifting. Experiments on low-permeability inclusions, high-conductivity channels and blocks, and three-dimensional grids demonstrate consistent accuracy across distinct coefficient structures. The method reaches a final-time relative $L^2$ error of $9.54\times10^{-4}$ in the principal benchmark and maintains errors on the order of $10^{-2}$ at a permeability contrast of $10^3$. Numerical diagnostics further show that the observed error balance and temporal convergence are consistent with the analysis. These results support DD RF-EDNN as a structure-preserving and interpretable reduced formulation for transient simulations on prescribed heterogeneous media.

</details>


### [2] [Bernstein Constraint Complexes for Multivariate Splines on Triangulated Surfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22355)
*Shelvean Kapita*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Bernstein constraint complexes represent finite element differential complexes by per-triangle Bernstein-Bezier coefficients with continuity imposed via edge/vertex smoothness functionals, enabling a coefficient-level commuting identity and exact constraints without constructing a global basis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard finite element differential complexes require constructing global bases and enforcing continuity globally, which is cumbersome and obscures the local structure of smoothness constraints. The paper seeks a representation where continuity is encoded purely through edge/vertex functionals, letting each triangle keep its own coefficients.

Method: Each triangle retains its own Bernstein-Bezier coefficients; global continuity is imposed only through smoothness functionals on edges and vertices, with different complexes differing only in edge functionals. The key structural result is a coefficient-level commuting identity: edge smoothness functionals of a Bernstein derivative are explicit combinations of the argument's functionals via univariate Bernstein difference matrices. The constrained Galerkin problem is solved in broken coefficients with exact smoothness equations, using a pseudoinverse interface for semidefinite operators. Surface Piola maps transfer reference functionals to curved triangulations.

Result: Local proof that kernels of smoothness matrices form a subcomplex for de Rham traces and componentwise C^r profiles, plus a computable row-space test for candidate smooth profiles. The lowest-order Powell-Sabin exact sequence is realized and its exactness confirmed by ranks. Equivalence between the constrained broken-coefficient method and the conforming method is proven; a singular pencil whose finite spectrum is the constrained Maxwell spectrum is given. Transfer of functionals under covariant/contravariant Piola maps to curved triangulations is shown. Experiments compare null-space, augmented Lagrangian, and direct saddle solution on planar source/eigenvalue problems, a C^1 biharmonic solve, the exact sphere, and an embedded hyperboloid.

Conclusion: Bernstein constraint complexes provide a local, basis-free framework for finite element differential complexes, with exact smoothness equations, equivalence to conforming methods, and validated numerical performance on planar and curved-surface problems.

Abstract: We introduce Bernstein constraint complexes, a representation of finite element differential complexes in which every triangle of a triangulation keeps its own Bernstein--Bézier coefficients and global continuity is imposed only through smoothness functionals attached to edges and vertices. Continuity of scalar splines, tangential continuity of spline vector fields, normal continuity of spline vector fields, and higher smoothness differ only in the edge functionals. No global basis is constructed. The structural result is a coefficient-level commuting identity: on every edge the smoothness functionals of a Bernstein derivative are explicit combinations of the smoothness functionals of its argument, through univariate Bernstein difference matrices. This gives a local proof that the kernels of the smoothness matrices form a subcomplex, for the de Rham traces and for componentwise $C^r$ spline profiles, and a computable row-space test for candidate smooth profiles. We realize the lowest-order Powell--Sabin exact sequence in this form and confirm its exactness by ranks. The constrained Galerkin problem is solved in the broken coefficients with exact smoothness equations; we prove equivalence with the conforming method, treat semidefinite element operators through a pseudoinverse interface system, and give a singular pencil whose finite spectrum is the constrained Maxwell spectrum. Under the covariant and contravariant surface Piola maps the reference functionals of the de Rham sequence transfer unchanged to conforming curved triangulations. Experiments compare the null-space method, the augmented Lagrangian iteration of Awanou, Lai and Wenston, and direct solution of the saddle system on the same problems, and cover planar source and eigenvalue problems, a $C^1$ biharmonic solve, the exact sphere, and an embedded hyperboloid.

</details>


### [3] [Conformal Spherical Splines for the Laplace--Beltrami Operator on Genus-Zero Surfaces: Construction, Algorithm, and Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22356)
*Shelvean Kapita*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper solves the Poisson equation and Laplace–Beltrami eigenvalue problem on genus-zero conformal metrics using smooth spherical splines, where the conformal factor is the only geometry input. The stiffness matrix equals the round-sphere matrix for all conformal metrics, enabling fast, accurate computations on parametric and mesh surfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Genus-zero closed Riemannian surfaces admit a conformal representation (round metric times a positive factor), so geometry can be encoded by a single conformal factor. Exploiting conformal invariance could yield efficient, accurate discretizations for PDEs and eigenvalue problems on such surfaces.

Method: Use smooth spherical splines in broken Bernstein–Bézier form on a fixed spherical triangulation, with Cr smoothness imposed algebraically via edge functionals and the conforming space realized by a null-space matrix computed once. Because Dirichlet energy is conformally invariant in 2D, the stiffness matrix is the round-sphere matrix for every conformal metric; the factor enters only the load vector, mean-value constraint, and weighted mass matrix. The conformal factor is checked against an area identity before discretization.

Result: Experiments on the round metric, prolate spheroid, dumbbell of revolution, Evans–Fung red-blood-cell profile, and a prescribed factor without embedding show convergence rates d+1 in L2 and d in energy for spline degree d, rate 2d for eigenvalues (even-order round-metric eigenvalues reproduced to roundoff), and smaller errors than parametric surface finite elements at comparable unknowns for degrees four and six.

Conclusion: The conformal-factor approach with spherical splines provides an efficient and accurate method for Poisson and eigenvalue problems on genus-zero surfaces, reducing geometry dependence to a single factor and achieving high-order convergence with a metric-independent stiffness matrix.

Abstract: Every smooth closed Riemannian surface of genus zero is isometric to the unit sphere carrying a conformal metric, the round metric multiplied by a positive factor. This paper solves the Poisson equation and the eigenvalue problem of the Laplace--Beltrami operator of such a metric with smooth spherical splines, taking the conformal factor as the only description of the geometry. Because the Dirichlet energy is conformally invariant in two dimensions, the stiffness matrix is the round-sphere matrix for every conformal metric, and the factor enters only the load vector, the mean-value constraint and the weighted mass matrix. A surface embedded in space with a conformal parametrization, a surface of revolution, and a surface given only as a triangle mesh are instances of the same problem that differ in how the factor is obtained; the first two are treated here, and the factor is checked against an area identity before any equation is discretized. The unknown is a spline on a fixed spherical triangulation in broken Bernstein--Bézier form, with $C^r$ smoothness imposed algebraically through edge functionals and the conforming space realized by a null-space matrix computed once and reused for every metric. Each step of the computation is stated with the formulas it needs. Experiments on the round metric, a prolate spheroid, a dumbbell of revolution, an Evans--Fung red-blood-cell profile and a prescribed factor with no embedding show the rates $d+1$ in $L^2$ and $d$ in energy for spline degree $d$, the rate $2d$ for eigenvalues with reproduction of the even-order eigenvalues of the round metric up to degree $d$ to roundoff, and, against parametric surface finite elements on the same triangulations, smaller errors at comparable numbers of unknowns for degrees four and six.

</details>


### [4] [Update to: Spectral sets: Numerical range and beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22460)
*Michel Crouzeix,Anne Greenbaum*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Using Lorist and Schwenninger's solution to Crouzeix's conjecture, this note improves the spectral set bounds for all regions from a 2019 Crouzeix–Greenbaum paper, showing annular regions are 2-spectral sets and a general convex region with a circular hole/cutout is a 4-spectral set.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The 2019 Crouzeix–Greenbaum paper gave K-spectral set bounds for various regions. The recent solution of Crouzeix's conjecture enables tighter bounds, motivating an update.

Method: Apply the results of Lorist and Schwenninger (arXiv:2608.03841v2) to each region considered in the 2019 paper to derive improved spectral set constants.

Result: Every region from the 2019 paper is shown to be a K-spectral set with a smaller K. Annular regions are 2-spectral sets, and a general convex region with a circular hole or cutout is a 4-spectral set.

Conclusion: The new conjecture solution yields strictly better spectral set bounds, refining the earlier results.

Abstract: We use the results of Lorist and Schwenninger, {\em A solution to Crouzeix's conjecture}, arXiv:2608.03841v2, https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03841, to update the 2019 paper of Crouzeix and Greenbaum, {\em Spectral sets: Numerical range and beyond}, SIAM J.~Matrix Anal.~Appl., 40(3):1087-1101. For all regions considered in the 2019 paper, we are able to show that the region is a $K$-spectral set with a bound on $K$ that is smaller than that established in the 2019 paper. In particular, we show that various annular regions are $2$-spectral sets and that a more general convex region with a circular hole or cutout is a $4$-spectral set.

</details>


### [5] [Efficient Rigorous Continuation via Chebyshev Series Expansion I](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22689)
*Maxime Breden,Olivier Hénot*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A rigorous Chebyshev series-based continuation method for solution manifolds in dynamical systems, with explicit error bounds via quasi-Newton operator contraction on weighted ℓ¹ spaces, applicable to pseudo-arclength and multi-parameter continuation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard continuation methods rely on C⁰-error bounds from the uniform contraction theorem, which provide coarse control. A finer, more rigorous control of solution manifolds (including cases where the continuation parameter is not a valid local coordinate) is needed.

Method: The solution branch is first approximated by a high-order Chebyshev interpolation polynomial. An explicit error bound is obtained by verifying contraction of a quasi-Newton operator near this approximation, formulated on a weighted ℓ¹ space rather than the usual C⁰ setting.

Result: The weighted ℓ¹ contraction yields finer error control than the standard C⁰ bound, which follows as a direct corollary. The method extends naturally to pseudo-arclength continuation and multi-parameter continuation. Two applications are demonstrated: a two-parameter family of steady-states for the Cahn--Hilliard equation, and a one-parameter family of steady-states undergoing saddle-node bifurcations for the Shigesada--Kawasaki--Teramoto system.

Conclusion: The proposed Chebyshev-based continuation framework provides rigorous, finer-grained error bounds and generalizes to challenging continuation settings, as validated by nontrivial applications.

Abstract: We study the global continuation of solution manifolds arising in dynamical systems. We present a rigorous continuation method based on a Chebyshev series expansion of the solution manifold. The branch is first approximated by a high-order Chebyshev interpolation polynomial, and an explicit error bound is then obtained by verifying the contraction of a quasi-Newton operator near this approximation. The contraction is formulated on a weighted $\ell^1$ space, giving a finer control than the typical $C^0$-error bound obtained from the uniform contraction theorem. In fact, the latter follows directly from our contraction operator. Furthermore, we discuss how our strategy applies naturally to pseudo-arclength continuation, where the continuation parameter fails to provide a valid local coordinate, and extends to multi-parameter continuation. Lastly, we detail two applications in which we compute a two-parameter family of steady-states for the Cahn--Hilliard equation, and a one-parameter family of steady-states undergoing saddle-node bifurcations for the Shigesada--Kawasaki--Teramoto system.

</details>


### [6] [Asymptotic-Preserving Exponential Integrators applied to the Hyperbolic Korteweg-de Vries System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22864)
*Abhijit Biswas,Sebastiano Boscarino,David I. Ketcheson,Giovanni Russo*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies exponential time integration with Fourier pseudospectral discretization for the hyperbolic KdV (KdVH) system, analyzing asymptotic preserving (AP) properties and numerical efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding whether time integration schemes preserve the asymptotic limit behavior of the KdVH system is important for reliable simulation, and existing schemes like ImEx Runge-Kutta may not be optimal. The authors aim to determine which exponential methods are AP and how they compare.

Method: They couple exponential time integration methods with Fourier pseudospectral space discretization. They analyze AP properties of Lawson and ETD methods, and develop an efficient implementation using an exact formula for the matrix exponential of the system, comparing it against ImEx Runge-Kutta.

Result: Lawson methods are generally not asymptotic preserving because auxiliary derivative-approximating variables fail to satisfy the limit equilibrium manifold, whereas ETD methods are AP for all components (solution and auxiliary variables). Exponential methods are shown to be competitive with ImEx Runge-Kutta in this context.

Conclusion: ETD methods, with an exact matrix exponential formula, provide an AP and competitive approach for the KdVH system compared to ImEx Runge-Kutta.

Abstract: We study the application of exponential time integration methods, coupled with Fourier pseudospectral space discretization, to the hyperbolic Korteweg-de Vries (KdVH) system. We investigate the asymptotic preserving (AP) properties of such discretizations, showing that, in general, Lawson methods are not asymptotic preserving, because the auxiliary (derivative-approximating) variables do not satisfy the limit equilibrium manifold, whereas exponential time differencing (ETD) methods are AP for all components, including both the solution variable and the auxiliary variables. We also present an efficient numerical implementation based on an exact formula for the matrix exponential of this system, and compare it with previously-proposed ImEx Runge-Kutta time integration, showing that exponential methods can be competitive in this context.

</details>


### [7] [The Zipped Finite Element Method on polyhedral meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22955)
*Stefano Berrone,Gioana Teora*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Extension of high-order Zipped FEM to 3D polyhedral elements, with a systematic QR-based construction and optimal convergence for diffusion-reaction problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The high-order Zipped FEM had only been developed for 2D polygonal elements using a heuristic procedure; a 3D extension with a more systematic and robust construction was needed.

Method: Local sub-tetrahedralization via triangulating polygonal faces and connecting to an interior point; high-order shape functions built as linear combinations of standard FE basis functions ensuring exact polynomial reproduction and C0-conformity; a dimension-independent QR-based strategy replaces the heuristic one, avoiding geometric alignment tests and problem-dependent tolerances.

Result: Three-dimensional convergence tests for a diffusion-reaction problem confirm the expected optimal polynomial order of accuracy.

Conclusion: The method provides a systematic, robust, dimension-independent high-order approach for 3D polyhedral elements, validated numerically.

Abstract: We extend the high-order Zipped Finite Element Method to three-dimensional polyhedral elements. The construction relies on a local sub-tetrahedralization obtained by triangulating the polygonal faces and connecting the resulting vertices to a suitable interior point. High-order shape functions are constructed as linear combinations of standard finite element basis functions, with coefficients chosen to ensure exact polynomial reproduction on the faces and in the element interior while preserving global C0-conformity. We further introduce a QR-based strategy as a systematic alternative to the heuristic procedure that was adopted for the two-dimensional case. The QR-strategy is more systematic and robust than the heuristic strategy, while being independent of the spatial dimension and avoiding geometric alignment tests and problem-dependent tolerances. The resulting method is formulated for a diffusion-reaction problem and numerically assessed through three-dimensional convergence tests, confirming the expected optimal polynomial order of accuracy.

</details>


### [8] [NeSTR: Neural S-Transform Reconstruction for Gaussian SPDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23001)
*Nacira Agram,Fred Espen Benth,Jan Rems*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: NeSTR reconstructs Gaussian SPDE solutions by learning the restricted S-Transform on a finite-dimensional white noise subspace, recovering Wiener chaos coefficients from Taylor derivatives of an analytic generating function.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning stochastic solutions directly is hard; the paper aims to tie neural approximation to the Wiener chaos expansion via the S-Transform, enabling deterministic learning and coefficient recovery.

Method: For Wick-type SPDEs, NeSTR derives a deterministic parametric PDE for the restricted S-Transform and uses an inverse S-Transform reconstruction principle with finite mode learning. It proves consistency of recovered chaos coefficients and separates noise mode, chaos truncation, solver, and neural approximation errors. Polynomial spectral representations in transform variables access higher-order chaos coefficients.

Result: On an additive stochastic heat equation and a multiplicative Wick heat equation, polynomial spectral representations provide stable access to higher-order chaos coefficients; reconstruction separates equation-dependent deterministic coefficient fields from a reusable Gaussian-Hermite basis.

Conclusion: NeSTR learns an analytic generating function whose derivatives recover Wiener chaos coefficients instead of learning stochastic trajectories, offering error-separated, deterministic learning for Gaussian SPDEs.

Abstract: We introduce Neural $S$-Transform Reconstruction (NeSTR) for Gaussian stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs). The learned object is not the random solution itself, but its $S$-Transform on a finite dimensional subspace of the white noise test space. For Wick type SPDEs, NeSTR yields a deterministic parametric PDE for the restricted $S$-Transform, and the stochastic solution is recovered from the Taylor coefficients of this transform at the origin. Thus the neural approximation is tied directly to the Wiener chaos expansion of the solution. The main novelty of NeSTR is an inverse $S$-Transform reconstruction principle combined with finite mode learning: we prove consistency of the recovered chaos coefficients, separate noise mode, chaos truncation, deterministic solver, and neural approximation errors, and give a deterministic learning formulation for the restricted transform. Numerical tests on an additive stochastic heat equation and a multiplicative Wick heat equation show that polynomial spectral representations in the transform variables provide stable access to higher order chaos coefficients. The resulting reconstruction separates the equation dependent deterministic coefficient fields from a reusable Gaussian--Hermite basis. Consequently, NeSTR learns an analytic generating function whose derivatives recover the Wiener chaos coefficients, rather than learning stochastic trajectories directly.

</details>


### [9] [Asymptotic preservation of an exponential Euler method under Hamiltonian fast advection with multiple critical points](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23045)
*Jianbo Cui,Guozhen Li,Derui Sheng*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proves that the exponential Euler method is asymptotic-preserving for stochastic reaction-diffusion-advection equations in R^2 under fast Hamiltonian advection with multiple critical points, reducing to an SPDE on a noncompact metric graph.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fast Hamiltonian advection with multiple critical points complicates limiting dynamics, and existing convergence analysis lacks handling for noncompact graphs with multiple edges/vertices and degenerate operators.

Method: Uses a weighted L^2 framework and analytic-semigroup smoothing estimates to analyze strong convergence of the limiting exponential Euler scheme, combined with fast-advection limits.

Result: Temporal convergence order arbitrarily close to 1/2 for weighted L^2 initial data, and exactly 1/2 under a half-order fractional-domain condition; numerical experiments confirm predictions.

Conclusion: The exponential Euler method is asymptotic-preserving in the multiple-critical-point setting, with convergence rates established despite graph noncompactness and operator degeneracy.

Abstract: We establish the asymptotic-preserving property of the exponential Euler method for stochastic reaction-diffusion-advection equations in $\mathbb R^2$ under fast Hamiltonian advection with multiple critical points. In the fast-advection limit, the dynamics reduces to a stochastic partial differential equation on a noncompact metric graph. A key ingredient is the strong convergence analysis of the limiting exponential Euler scheme, which is complicated by the graph's noncompactness, its multiple edges and vertices, and the nonuniform ellipticity and vertex degeneracy of the graph operator. Using a weighted $L^2$ framework and analytic-semigroup smoothing estimates, we prove a temporal convergence order arbitrarily close to $1/2$ for weighted $L^2$ initial data and exactly $1/2$ under a half-order fractional-domain condition. Combining these estimates with the fast-advection limits yields the asymptotic-preserving property in the multiple-critical-point setting. Numerical experiments illustrate the asymptotic behavior and confirm the predicted temporal convergence rate.

</details>


### [10] [TASE-Stabilized Time-Evolving Natural Gradient Methods for Diffusion-Dominated PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23054)
*Zihao Shi,Dongling Wang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: TASE-TENG stabilizes Time-Evolving Natural Gradient neural PDE solvers for stiff diffusion problems by applying a highly stable exponential-operator factor to each Runge-Kutta stage, using a learned mesh-free surrogate of the stiff diffusion operator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TENG evolves neural approximations of time-dependent PDEs via natural-gradient projection, but explicit Runge-Kutta methods suffer severe stability limits on stiff, diffusion-dominated problems. High-frequency diffusive modes push stage targets outside the neural manifold's locally reachable region, worsening least-squares conditioning and destabilizing updates.

Method: TASE-TENG applies the stable explicit operator T_p(hW_0) to each Runge-Kutta stage increment before projection, where W_0 approximates the dominant stiff diffusion operator. The surrogate is a learnable mesh-free operator whose local interaction coefficients come from a shared geometry-dependent neural rule, with a structured factorization enforcing discrete self-adjointness, dissipativity, and constant preservation. It is trained offline, fixed during online integration, and used only inside the TASE stabilization operator.

Result: On stiff diffusion and reaction-diffusion benchmarks, TASE-TENG improves stability and accuracy at the same time-step size, reducing errors from high-frequency diffusive modes. The learned rule permits the stabilization operator to be reconstructed at different spatial point resolutions without retraining.

Conclusion: TASE-TENG overcomes Runge-Kutta stability restrictions in natural-gradient neural PDE solvers by embedding a learned, mesh-free stiff-diffusion surrogate in a stable exponential stabilization operator, yielding better stability and accuracy and resolution-transferable stabilization.

Abstract: The Time-Evolving Natural Gradient (TENG) method evolves neural approximations of time-dependent partial differential equations by projecting time-discrete target states onto the neural-network manifold through local natural-gradient iterations. For stiff diffusion-dominated problems, however, explicit Runge--Kutta methods face severe stability restrictions. High-frequency diffusive components can drive stage targets beyond the locally reachable region of the neural manifold, worsening the conditioning of local least-squares projections and destabilizing parameter updates.
  We propose TASE--TENG, which applies the highly stable explicit operator $T_p(hW_0)$ to each Runge--Kutta stage increment before projection, where $W_0$ approximates the dominant stiff diffusion operator. To construct this surrogate in a mesh-free setting, we develop a learnable operator whose local interaction coefficients are generated by a shared geometry-dependent neural rule. A structured factorization enforces discrete self-adjointness, dissipativity, and constant preservation. The operator is trained offline and remains fixed during online integration, where it is used only within the TASE stabilization operator. Numerical experiments on stiff diffusion and reaction--diffusion problems demonstrate improved stability and accuracy at the same time-step size, with reduced errors from high-frequency diffusive modes. The learned rule also allows the stabilization operator to be reconstructed at different spatial point resolutions without retraining.

</details>


### [11] [A fast spectral particle method for the Landau equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23086)
*Giacomo Borghi,Lorenzo Pareschi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A fast deterministic particle method for the spatially homogeneous Landau equation, combining particle representation with Fourier approximation of the collision flux, achieving O(N+M^d log M) per step and validated in 2D.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving the spatially homogeneous Landau equation efficiently and deterministically is challenging; existing particle methods can be slow, and direct blob implementations are costly. The paper aims to provide a fast, accurate alternative.

Method: The method couples a particle representation of the solution with a Fourier approximation of the nonlinear collision flux. Nonuniform FFTs reconstruct density from particles and evaluate flux/density at particle locations, while the flux's convolutional structure allows efficient FFT computation.

Result: Per-step cost of O(N+M^d log M) for fixed transform tolerance. A consistency estimate for the reconstructed velocity field is established where the reference density is bounded away from zero, separating spectral truncation error from particle-density reconstruction error (the latter can dominate for smooth densities). 2D Maxwellian experiments show accuracy/efficiency, sensitivity to particle and spectral resolution, and filtering effects. Compared to a direct blob implementation, it achieves better accuracy at much lower cost.

Conclusion: The proposed method is fast, accurate, and efficient for the homogeneous Landau equation, with favorable scaling and clear advantages over direct blob approaches.

Abstract: We propose a fast deterministic particle method for the spatially homogeneous Landau equation. The method combines a particle representation of the solution with a Fourier approximation of the nonlinear collision flux. Nonuniform fast Fourier transforms are used to reconstruct the density from the particles and to evaluate the flux and density at the particle locations, while the convolutional structure of the flux enables its efficient computation by FFTs. For a fixed transform tolerance, the cost per time step is $\mathcal{O}(N+M^d\log M)$, where $N$ is the number of particles and $M$ the number of Fourier modes per velocity dimension. We establish a consistency estimate for the reconstructed velocity field on regions where the reference density is bounded away from zero. The estimate separates the spectral truncation error from the particle-density reconstruction error, showing how the latter can dominate for sufficiently smooth densities. Two-dimensional numerical experiments with Maxwellian interactions illustrate the accuracy and efficiency of the method, its sensitivity to particle and spectral resolution, and the effects of filtering. Comparisons with a direct blob implementation demonstrate improved accuracy at a strongly reduced computational cost.

</details>


### [12] [Sparsely connected neural network representation of Lagrange finite element function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23299)
*Jiaxiong Hao,Yunqing Huang,Nianyu Yi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes a mesh-induced sparsely connected neural network that exactly reproduces arbitrary-order Lagrange finite element spaces on simplicial meshes, embedding full FE approximation theory and enabling AD-based gradient extraction, non-matching mesh interpolation, and adaptive FEM for parabolic PDEs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional black-box neural surrogates lack the rigorous approximation guarantees and structure of classical finite element methods, and standard FEM implementations require separate gradient calculation subroutines. The authors aim to unify neural network representations with exact FE discretization rules.

Method: A mesh-induced sparsely connected network fully dictated by FE rules: local computations from simplex geometry and barycentric transformations, global consistency via shared degrees of freedom. Linear Lagrange bases use affine barycentric layers; high-order polynomial bases are decomposed into barycentric product compositions via ReLU^p modules. Element indicator branches and multiplication units globally assemble local components, with customized backward differentiation rules for piecewise activations to extract values and gradients in one graph.

Result: Numerical experiments verify the accuracy of the network representation of Lagrange finite elements. The scheme interpolates FE functions between non-matching meshes and applies to adaptive FEM for solving parabolic PDEs.

Conclusion: The framework's function space exactly coincides with the target FE space, inheriting classical FE approximation theory. Its mesh-free nature enables non-matching mesh interpolation and adaptive parabolic PDE solving, with an open-source implementation released.

Abstract: We construct a mesh-induced sparsely connected neural network framework that exactly reproduces arbitrary-order Lagrange finite element spaces over simplicial meshes. Unlike conventional black-box neural surrogates, the proposed network architecture is fully dictated by finite element discretization rules: local computations stem from simplex geometry and barycentric coordinate transformations, while global consistency is enforced through shared degrees of freedom. For linear Lagrange elements, local basis functions are directly implemented via affine barycentric layers, and high-order polynomial bases are explicitly decomposed into barycentric product compositions realized by specially designed $\mathrm{ReLU}^p$ modules. Equipped with element indicator branches and multiplication units, these modular local components are globally assembled into a sparsely connected neural network whose function space coincides exactly with the target finite element space, thereby inheriting the complete classical finite element approximation theory. By prescribing customized backward differentiation rules for piecewise activations, function values and their spatial gradients can be simultaneously extracted via automatic differentiation within a unified computational graph, eliminating the separate gradient calculation subroutines required in standard finite element implementations. Numerical experiments verify the accuracy of the neural network representation of Lagrange finite elements. Furthermore, by virtue of the intrinsic mesh-free nature of this neural network representation, finite element functions can be interpolated between non-matching meshes, and the proposed scheme can be applied to adaptive finite element methods for solving parabolic partial differential equations. An open-source code implementation of the proposed architecture is made publicly available.

</details>


### [13] [Super-approximation and optimal $L^2$ convergence of the stabilized BGN method with piecewise linear parametric finite elements for curve-shortening flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23326)
*Genming Bai*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces a bihomotopy argument combined with summation-by-parts to derive super-convergence estimates for a stabilized BGN method for curve-shortening flow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Establish stronger (super-)convergence results for the projection and trajectory errors of a stabilized BGN method for curve-shortening flow using piecewise linear parametric finite elements.

Method: A bihomotopy argument to estimate discrete material time derivatives of consistency functionals, combined with summation-by-parts and super-approximation of a reversely weighted discrete normal.

Result: A directional super-convergence estimate for the consistency contribution when tested with the normal error velocity, yielding L∞_t H¹_x super-convergence of the projection error and optimal L∞_t L²_x convergence of the trajectory error.

Conclusion: The proposed techniques successfully establish super-convergence and optimal convergence of the stabilized BGN method for curve-shortening flow.

Abstract: We develop a bihomotopy argument to estimate the discrete material time derivatives of consistency functionals. Combined with summation-by-parts, this argument yields a directional super-convergence estimate for the consistency contribution when tested with the normal error velocity. Together with the super-approximation of the reversely weighted discrete normal, these techniques establish $L_t^\infty H_x^1$ super-convergence of the projection error and optimal $L_t^\infty L_x^2$ convergence of the trajectory error of a stabilized BGN method for curve-shortening flow with piecewise linear parametric finite elements.

</details>


### [14] [High-order mass-, energy- and momentum-conserving methods for the nonlinear Schrödinger equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23344)
*Georgios Akrivis,Buyang Li,Rong Tang,Hui Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A space-time finite element method for the nonlinear Schrödinger equation that preserves mass, energy, and momentum at the discrete level, with an efficient Newton-based iterative scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Numerical methods for the nonlinear Schrödinger equation typically do not conserve key physical invariants (mass, energy, momentum) at the discrete level, which causes large errors in long-time simulations.

Method: A novel formulation with an associated space-time finite element method using a fully discrete implicit scheme. Conservation of mass, energy, and momentum is proven. An iterative scheme uses an equivalent formulation that applies Newton's iteration for the solution while leaving Lagrange multipliers un-iterated.

Result: Numerical examples show high-order convergence and effective conservation of mass, energy, and momentum for 1D Ma-solitons and bi-solitons and 2D solitons. The conserving method significantly reduces solution errors in long-time simulations compared with non-conserving methods.

Conclusion: The proposed space-time finite element method achieves provable discrete conservation of mass, energy, and momentum, is computationally effective, and improves accuracy in long-time simulations of the nonlinear Schrödinger equation.

Abstract: This paper introduces a novel formulation and an associated space-time finite element method for simulating solutions to the nonlinear Schrödinger equation. A major advantage of the proposed algorithm is its intrinsic ability to preserve the conservation of mass, energy, and momentum at the discrete level. This is proved for the numerical solutions determined by the fully discrete implicit scheme. An effective iterative scheme is proposed for solving the nonlinear system based on an equivalent formulation which suggests using Newton's iteration for the solution and no iteration for the Lagrange multipliers in the nonlinear system. Extensive numerical examples are provided to demonstrate the high-order convergence and effectiveness of the proposed algorithm in conserving mass, energy, and momentum in the simulation of one-dimensional Ma-solitons and bi-solitons, as well as of two-dimensional solitons governed by the nonlinear Schrödinger equation. The numerical results show that the mass-, energy- and momentum-conserving method designed in this paper also significantly reduces the errors of the numerical solutions in long-time simulations compared with methods which do not conserve these quantities.

</details>


### [15] [Dynamic Ritz projection of mean curvature flow and optimal ${\bf L^2}$ convergence of parametric FEM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23361)
*Buyang Li,Rong Tang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces a dynamic Ritz projection to analyze convergence of parametric finite element approximations for mean curvature flow of closed surfaces, achieving optimal-order error estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing error analyses for parametric finite element methods for mean curvature flow typically compare numerical solutions to an interpolation of the exact flow, which is limiting. A better comparison object—closer to the discrete solution but still accurate—is needed for sharper convergence results.

Method: A dynamic Ritz projection of the mean curvature flow is introduced as the comparison target. Errors of this projection in approximating mean curvature flow are first established in L^2 and W^{1,p} norms, then leveraged to conduct the convergence analysis of parametric finite element approximations.

Result: Optimal-order convergence of parametric finite element methods for mean curvature flow of closed surfaces is proved in the L^∞(0,T;L^2) norm, including the case of piecewise linear finite elements.

Conclusion: The dynamic Ritz projection approach provides a rigorous and effective framework for optimal-order convergence analysis, applicable even to low-order (piecewise linear) parametric finite element schemes.

Abstract: A new approach is developed to study the convergence of parametric finite element approximations to the mean curvature flow of closed surfaces in three-dimensional space. In this approach, the error analysis is conducted by comparing the numerical solution to a dynamic Ritz projection of the mean curvature flow introduced in this paper, rather than an interpolation of the mean curvature flow, as commonly used in the literature. The errors associated with the dynamic Ritz projection in approximating the mean curvature flow are established in the $L^2$ and $W^{1,p}$ norms. Leveraging these results, optimal-order convergence of parametric finite element methods for mean curvature flow of closed surfaces in the $L^\infty(0,T;L^2)$ norm is proved, including the convergence of parametric finite element methods with piecewise linear finite elements.

</details>


### [16] [A novel monotonous finite volume element scheme for convection dominant diffusion problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23546)
*Cunyun Nie,Xiaoling Chen,Zhujun wang,Zhikun Tian,Chengjie Xia*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A novel monotonous finite volume element (MFVE) scheme is proposed for convection-dominant diffusion problems, featuring an upwind volume, high-order nonlinear convection operators, and positivity-preserving diffusion, with second-order accuracy and error analysis validated numerically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Convection-dominant diffusion problems are challenging to discretize accurately; classical finite volume methods have limitations in achieving monotonicity and high-order accuracy for the convection term.

Method: Introduces an upwind sub-control volume (upwind volume) for convection discretization; derives a first-order linear discrete convection operator via numerical integration; builds a second-order nonlinear convection operator using asymptotic gradient expansion with perturbed coefficients as high-order correction; designs a two-order MFVE scheme (plus a one-order version) combined with a positivity-preserving finite volume element (PFVE) diffusive operator.

Result: L2 norm error of the approximate solution is derived, and numerical results validate the theoretical conclusions.

Conclusion: The proposed MFVE scheme achieves monotonicity and high-order accuracy for convection-dominated diffusion problems, with theoretical error bounds confirmed by numerical experiments.

Abstract: One novel monotonous finite volume element (MFVE) scheme is put forward for convection dominant diffusion problem. The main contributions of this paper include four aspects. Firstly, one upwind sub-control volume, called upwind volume, is introduced for the discretization of the convection term similar to the upwind element, which leads to the upwind property. Secondly, one first-order linear discrete operator for the convection term in the balance equation is directly obtained by numerical integration in upwind volume, which is different from the classical finite volume methods.Thirdly, one asymptotic expansion of gradient functions in the dual element of each node is derived by one extension interpolation function. Then, one second-order nonlinear discrete operator for the convection term is constructed by the asymptotic expansion and error estimation of its FVE solution, where some perturbed coefficients are skillfully put into the expansion, which serves as a high-order correction. Fourthly, one novel two-order MFVE scheme, also accompanied by another one-order MFVE, is designed for convection-dominated diffusion problems together with one positivity-preserving finite volume element (PFVE) discrete diffusive operator. The L2 norm of the error of the approximate solution is derived. Finally, numerical results validate theoretical conclusions.

</details>


### [17] [Numerical analysis of parabolic equations with Prandtl--Ishlinskii hysteresis of play type](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23676)
*Shu Xu,Liqun Cao*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper establishes an O(h+τ) error bound for an implicit Euler P1 finite element discretization of parabolic equations with Prandtl-Ishlinskii play-type hysteresis, without requiring higher-order temporal or extra spatial regularity of hysteresis variables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rigorous error analysis for numerical approximations of parabolic equations with hysteresis remains limited, even for the widely used Prandtl-Ishlinskii play-type hysteresis.

Method: Temporal discretization exploits a convex subgradient-flow structure in a weighted Hilbert space with dissipation and coercive subgradient remainder for first-order convergence. Spatial discretization restricts only the diffusive field to the finite element space, using a constraint-preserving comparison for an O(h) semidiscrete estimate. The analysis covers play-type Prandtl-Ishlinskii operators on a spatial Hilbert space, including pointwise and spatially structured constraints.

Result: An O(h+τ) error bound is obtained for the implicit Euler P1 finite element discretization, requiring neither higher-order temporal regularity nor additional spatial regularity of the hysteresis variables.

Conclusion: The proposed analysis provides rigorous first-order convergence for parabolic equations with play-type Prandtl-Ishlinskii hysteresis, generalizing to spatially structured constraints.

Abstract: Rigorous error analysis for numerical approximations of parabolic equations with hysteresis remains limited, even for the widely used Prandtl--Ishlinskii hysteresis of play type. In this work, we establish an $O(h+τ)$ error bound for an implicit Euler $P_1$ finite element discretization. The analysis requires neither higher-order temporal regularity of the hysteresis variables, which cannot in general be expected in hysteretic evolutions, nor additional spatial regularity of these variables. For the temporal discretization, we exploit a convex subgradient-flow structure in a weighted Hilbert space together with the associated dissipation and coercive subgradient remainder to obtain first-order convergence. For the spatial discretization, only the diffusive field is restricted to the finite element space, and a constraint-preserving comparison yields an $O(h)$ semidiscrete estimate. The analysis is developed for play-type Prandtl--Ishlinskii operators formulated directly on a spatial Hilbert space, encompassing the canonical pointwise model as well as more general spatially structured constraints.

</details>


### [18] [ELIPPS: Exact Learning for Inverse Problems from Partial Self-supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23683)
*Benjamin Walder Markus Haltmeier,Lukas Neumann,Nadja Gruber,Gyeongha Hwang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: ELIPPS is a self-supervised training paradigm for undersampled inverse problems that learns only from incomplete measurements on a fixed supervision set, with theory showing masked empirical risk equals full self-supervised risk under transformation invariance, and strong CT results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In undersampled inverse problems like sparse-view CT, collecting full measurements and ground-truth images for supervision is costly or infeasible. Existing learning-based methods require full supervision, so a method is needed that trains without ground-truth images or fully sampled measurements.

Method: ELIPPS uses incomplete self-supervised training with only incomplete forward measurements on a fixed supervision set. It minimizes a masked empirical risk, exploiting data invariance under transformations. Theory proves equivalence to full self-supervised risk for equivariant hypothesis classes. Implemented for CT using rotation and reflection invariance.

Result: Theory shows exact equivalence for arbitrary equivariant hypothesis classes, validity for signal-dependent noise (pre-log Poisson), and up to quantifiable bias for log-transformed count model. A stability estimate is given for approximate coverage on a discrete grid. Experiments show ELIPPS greatly outperforms naive masked supervision and reaches accuracy comparable to a fully supervised reference model.

Conclusion: ELIPPS enables effective training of undersampled inverse problems without ground-truth images or full measurements, with theoretical guarantees and strong practical CT performance matching fully supervised accuracy.

Abstract: In undersampled inverse problems (such as sparse-view computed tomography), only a small number of measurements are collected, which reduces radiation exposure and acquisition time and cost, and can also address the inaccessibility of certain acquisition arrangements. Most learning-based methods for such problems require supervision in the form of fully sampled measurements and ground-truth images, which are costly or even infeasible to acquire. To overcome this issue, we propose \emph{Exact Learning for Inverse Problems from Partial Self-supervision} (ELIPPS), an incomplete self-supervised training paradigm for undersampled inverse problems that requires neither ground-truth images nor fully sampled measurements. ELIPPS learns solely from incomplete forward measurements on a fixed incomplete supervision set. Our theory shows that when the data distribution is invariant under certain transformations, minimizing a masked empirical risk is equivalent to minimizing the full self-supervised risk, i.e. the risk against the complete, noise-free measurement. The equivalence is exact for arbitrary equivariant hypothesis classes and holds for signal-dependent noise such as the pre-log Poisson statistics of low-dose tomography, and for the log-transformed count model up to a quantifiable bias. When the underlying coverage condition is only approximately satisfied on a discrete grid, we give a stability estimate in terms of the associated frame constants and the irreducible error of the inverse problem. We realize ELIPPS for computed tomography by exploiting rotation and reflection invariance. In our experiments, ELIPPS substantially outperforms naive masked supervision and reaches the same order of accuracy as a reference model trained with full clean measurements.

</details>


### [19] [Blind error estimation for CUR approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23810)
*Lorenzo Lazzarino,Katherine J. Pearce,Nathaniel Pritchard*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper derives a "blind" estimator for the Frobenius error of a CUR decomposition when only a subset of matrix entries can be observed, with worst-case lower bounds on required column queries and uncertainty quantification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Randomized low-rank methods like CUR decomposition operate with limited matrix access, which suits settings (e.g., spectro-microscopy experiments) where even matrix-vector products are unavailable. However, their inherent randomness makes it hard to assess approximation accuracy under the constraint that only a subset of matrix entries can be observed.

Method: The authors derive a "blind" estimator of the Frobenius error of a CUR decomposition. They establish a worst-case lower bound on the number of required column queries and provide techniques for quantifying the estimator's uncertainty.

Result: The estimator and its associated uncertainty sets are evaluated on a mix of synthetic problems and real spectro-microscopy examples, demonstrating performance.

Conclusion: A blind Frobenius-error estimator for CUR decompositions is feasible under entry-limited observation, supported by query lower bounds and uncertainty quantification, with practical validation.

Abstract: Low-rank approximation is a fundamental tool for scalable matrix computations. While such approximations have classically been formed via the truncated SVD, recent advances in randomized numerical linear algebra have produced methods of comparable accuracy at a fraction of the cost. A key advantage of these approaches is their ability to operate with limited access to the full matrix. A prominent example is the CUR decomposition, which builds an approximation from only a subset of columns and rows, making it particularly well-suited to settings where global matrix access is unavailable, such as in the design of spectro-microscopy experiments where even matrix-vector products cannot be computed. However, the randomness inherent to these methods introduces a key challenge: efficiently assessing approximation accuracy under the stringent constraint that only a subset of matrix entries can be observed. We derive a "blind" estimator of the Frobenius error of a CUR decomposition. For this estimator, we derive a worst-case lower bound on the number of required column queries, and provide techniques for quantifying its uncertainty. Finally, we demonstrate the performance of the estimator and the uncertainty sets on a mix of synthetic problems and real spectro-microscopy examples.

</details>


### [20] [Numerical differentiation of functions on the half-line in a weighted uniform metric](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23833)
*S. G. Solodky,Y. A. Volynets*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper studies numerical differentiation of functions on the half-line from perturbed Fourier–Laguerre coefficients, recovering $f^{(r)}$ with error measured in a weighted uniform metric and coefficient error in $\ell_p$, and constructs optimal-order methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Numerical differentiation from noisy Fourier–Laguerre coefficients on the half-line is a practically relevant ill-posed problem, and the authors aim to find a proper coefficient-error/uniform-error setting and optimal methods.

Method: The derivative $f^{(r)}$ is recovered from a finite set of perturbed Fourier–Laguerre coefficients; the Wiener class $W^\mu_s$ is shown to admit this setting for $\mu>r+1-1/s$, and a class of methods $\mathcal{S}^\theta$ is constructed.

Result: The Wiener class $W^\mu_s$ admits the setting for $\mu>r+1-1/s$, and the constructed methods $\mathcal{S}^\theta$ achieve the optimal order of accuracy for numerical differentiation while using the smallest amount of input information in order.

Conclusion: An optimal-order, information-minimal approach for numerical differentiation on the half-line is established within the Fourier–Laguerre framework.

Abstract: We consider the problem of numerical differentiation of functions defined on the half-line: the derivative $f^{(r)}$, $ r=1,2,\ldots$, is recovered from a finite set of perturbed Fourier--Laguerre coefficients of a function $f$, whose error is measured in $\ell_p$, while the accuracy of approximation is measured in the uniform metric with the weight $t^αe^{-t}$. It is established that the Wiener class $W^μ_{s}$ admits such a setting for $μ>r+1-1/s$. A class of methods $\mathcal{S}^θ$ is constructed that realize the optimal order of accuracy of numerical differentiation on $W^μ_{s}$ and use the smallest amount of input information in order.

</details>


### [21] [Time-uniform accuracy of ensemble Kalman filters with localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23927)
*Xiaoou Cheng,Daniel Sanz-Alonso,Nathan Waniorek*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper proves time-uniform accuracy guarantees for deterministic square-root ensemble Kalman filters in linear-Gaussian systems, showing ensemble size depends on effective rank and unstable subspace dimension rather than ambient dimension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ensemble Kalman filters are widely used but lack rigorous time-uniform accuracy guarantees, particularly for deterministic square-root variants without inflation or resampling. Understanding how ensemble size requirements scale, especially for high-dimensional and spatially localized systems, is critical for practical deployment.

Method: The analysis combines stability and forgetting properties for possibly singular Riccati recursions, nonasymptotic covariance concentration bounds, and perturbation estimates for localized covariance dynamics.

Result: For hyperbolic, detectable systems, ensemble means and covariances approximate Kalman filter counterparts uniformly in time with high probability. Required ensemble size depends on effective rank of initial covariance and unstable subspace dimension, not ambient state dimension. For a localized filter on weakly coupled spatial systems, ensemble size depends on local intrinsic dimension and only logarithmically on the number of spatial blocks, while localization bias scales with interaction strength.

Conclusion: Deterministic square-root ensemble Kalman filters achieve time-uniform accuracy without inflation or resampling, with scalability governed by intrinsic (effective/ local) dimensions rather than ambient dimension, and localization bias separated from sampling error.

Abstract: This paper establishes time-uniform accuracy guarantees for deterministic square-root ensemble Kalman filters in linear--Gaussian state-space models under perfect-model dynamics. For hyperbolic, detectable systems, we prove that the ensemble means and covariances approximate their Kalman filter counterparts uniformly in time, with high probability and without inflation or resampling. The required ensemble size depends on the effective rank of the initial covariance and the dimension of the unstable subspace, rather than on the ambient state dimension. We also analyze a localized square-root ensemble Kalman filter for weakly coupled spatial systems. We show that the stabilizing Kalman covariance inherits spatial decay from the dynamics and derive accuracy bounds that separate sampling error from localization bias. For the localized filter, the required ensemble size depends on the local intrinsic dimension and only logarithmically on the number of spatial blocks, while the localization bias scales with the interaction strength. Our analysis combines stability and forgetting for possibly singular Riccati recursions, nonasymptotic covariance concentration, and perturbation estimates for localized covariance dynamics.

</details>


### [22] [Cost-Accuracy Trade-offs: Neural Operator vs Classical Numerical Solver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24021)
*Daniel Zhengyu Huang,Andrew M. Stuart*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Neural operator surrogates beat classical PDE solvers mainly at low-to-moderate accuracy and when they bypass iterative solves or predict reduced quantities; at high accuracy, classical solvers win.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify when and why neural operator surrogates outperform classical numerical solvers in cost-for-accuracy terms. Prior work rarely addresses this rigorously, especially in the amortized many-query regime favorable to neural operators.

Method: Reproducible benchmark comparing neural operators against problem-matched classical solvers on representative computational science and engineering problems. Evaluation in the post-training, many-query limit (training/data costs amortized). Metrics: prediction error, per-query floating-point cost, wall-clock runtime.

Result: Neural operators are most competitive at low-to-moderate accuracy. Floating-point advantages arise when they avoid temporal/nonlinear iterations or predict reduced quantities of interest instead of full fields. Extra wall-clock speedups come from dense tensor operations suited to modern hardware. As target accuracy tightens, neural operators struggle and classical solvers become more cost-effective.

Conclusion: Neural operators are not universally superior; their advantage is regime-dependent and diminishes at high accuracy. Classical solvers remain essential for verification and high-accuracy computation.

Abstract: Neural operators are data-driven models that learn mappings from inputs that parameterize partial differential equations, such as spatially varying coefficients, initial conditions, forcing terms, boundary conditions, or geometries, to solution fields or quantities of interest. Once trained, they can serve as surrogates for classical numerical solvers in many-query settings that require repeated evaluations for varying inputs. We address the question of when, and then why, neural operator surrogates outperform classical numerical solvers, in terms of cost for a given accuracy. We focus on the post-training, many-query limit, in which data-acquisition and training costs are treated as fixed and fully amortized. Even in this deliberately favorable regime for neural operators, there are regimes in which classical solvers outperform the surrogate models. We compare the cost-accuracy performance of neural operator surrogates and classical numerical solvers through a reproducible benchmark study comparing neural operators with problem-matched classical solvers on representative problems in computational science and engineering, focusing on prediction error, per-query floating-point cost, and wall-clock runtime. Neural operators are most competitive at low-to-moderate accuracy requirements. Their floating-point cost advantage depends strongly on the problem structure, arising when they avoid temporal or nonlinear iterations or predict a reduced quantity of interest rather than a full solution field. Additional wall-clock speedups result from dense tensor operations that are well suited to modern hardware. As the target accuracy is tightened, achieving the required accuracy with neural operators becomes increasingly challenging, and classical solvers outperform surrogates in this regime; thus classical solvers will remain important for verification and high-accuracy computation.

</details>


### [23] [Accurate wall shear stress in immersed flow analysis with application to point cloud-based CFD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24069)
*Monu Jaiswal,Ming-Chen Hsu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A new method for accurate wall shear stress (WSS) in point cloud-based CFD using non-symmetric Nitsche formulation with near-wall modeling and patch-based stress recovery with traction compatibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Point cloud-based CFD bypasses surface reconstruction and mesh generation by enforcing no-slip conditions via Nitsche-based weak BC. While it yields accurate velocity, pressure, and loads, local WSS prediction is challenging due to cut elements lacking regularity for gradient evaluation and the stabilization term's cut-configuration-dependent parameter in symmetric Nitsche, affecting traction definition.

Method: Non-symmetric Nitsche's formulation combined with near-wall modeling and a patch-based stress recovery approach with traction compatibility.

Result: Validated on canonical benchmarks and applied to turbulent flow past a sphere and a patient-specific aorta, showing excellent agreement with reference results.

Conclusion: The proposed method achieves accurate WSS in immersed flow analysis for point cloud-based CFD.

Abstract: Point cloud-based CFD enables flow analysis directly on discrete points obtained from 3D scanning and medical imaging, bypassing surface reconstruction, geometry cleanup, and boundary-fitted mesh generation. Derived from immersogeometric analysis, the method immerses the point cloud in a background mesh and enforces no-slip conditions on discrete points through a Nitsche-based weak boundary condition (BC). The framework delivers accurate velocity fields, pressure distribution, and integrated loads; however, accurate prediction of the local wall shear stress (WSS) has remained a critical challenge. The geometry intersects the background mesh arbitrarily, producing cut elements that lack the regularity required for consistent gradient evaluation. The issue is compounded by the stabilization term of the weak BC, whose parameter estimation in the symmetric Nitsche formulation is dependent on the cut configuration and affects the variationally consistent definition of traction from which the WSS is computed. In this work, we propose a new method to obtain accurate wall shear stress in immersed flow analysis with application to point cloud-based CFD, using a non-symmetric Nitsche's formulation with near-wall modeling and a patch-based stress recovery approach with traction compatibility. The method is validated on canonical benchmarks and applied to turbulent flow past a sphere and to a patient-specific aorta, showcasing excellent agreement with reference results.

</details>


### [24] [A mesh- and horizon-robust preconditioner for finite element discretizations of volume-constrained nonlocal diffusion problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24143)
*Jiashu Lu,Yufeng Nie,Haolun Zhang,Pingrui Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper develops a robust preconditioner B_h = G_h + βδ²D_h⁻¹ for finite element discretizations of volume-constrained nonlocal diffusion problems, with spectrum uniformly bounded in mesh size and horizon, supported by rigorous analysis and numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite element discretizations of volume-constrained nonlocal diffusion problems suffer from ill-conditioning that depends on both the mesh size and the interaction horizon. Existing solvers are typically not robust with respect to both parameters simultaneously, motivating a preconditioner that handles both uniformly.

Method: Motivated by the Fourier multiplier of the nonlocal operator, the authors construct preconditioner B_h = G_h + βδ²D_h⁻¹, where G_h is a symmetric local Poisson solver, D_h is the lumped mass matrix, and β is a kernel-dependent constant. They prove the preconditioned spectrum is bounded above and away from zero uniformly in mesh size and horizon, develop a pair-cell assembly preserving symmetry and positive definiteness for PCG, establish an L²-error estimate, and use a single symmetric geometric multigrid V-cycle for G_h.

Result: The spectrum of the B_h-preconditioned system is uniformly bounded with respect to mesh size and horizon; the L²-error estimate is established; and numerical experiments confirm the predicted convergence rate with bounded condition numbers and PCG iteration counts across tested meshes, horizons, and kernels.

Conclusion: The proposed preconditioner is robust with respect to both mesh size and interaction horizon for volume-constrained nonlocal diffusion, delivering uniform spectral bounds and stable PCG performance validated by theory and numerics.

Abstract: In this paper, we develop a preconditioner that is robust with respect to both the mesh size and the interaction horizon for finite element discretizations of volume-constrained nonlocal diffusion problems. Motivated by the Fourier multiplier of the nonlocal operator, we construct the preconditioner $B_h=G_h+βδ^2D_h^{-1}$, where $G_h$ is a symmetric local Poisson solver, $D_h$ is the lumped mass matrix, and $β$ is a kernel-dependent constant. We rigorously prove that the spectrum of the $B_h$-preconditioned system is bounded above and away from zero uniformly with respect to the mesh size and horizon. Moreover, we develop a pair-cell assembly for the finite element matrix that preserves the symmetry and positive definiteness required by the preconditioner and PCG, and establish an $L^2$-error estimate for the finite element discretization. Finally, we use a single symmetric geometric multigrid V-cycle for $G_h$ in the numerical experiments, and the corresponding numerical results confirm the predicted convergence rate and show bounded condition numbers and PCG iteration counts over the tested meshes, horizons, and kernels.

</details>


### [25] [An Implementation-Friendly SDG Scheme based on Cartesian Grids for Stokes Equations with Pressure Robustness and Superconvergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24232)
*Bohan Yang,Eric Chung*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A staggered discontinuous Galerkin (SDG) scheme on Cartesian grids for Stokes equations that is simple, pressure-robust, and second-order superconvergent, extended to Navier-Stokes with an energy-stable CN-SAV scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard SDG uses composite meshes that are complex to implement; a simpler scheme with intrinsic pressure robustness and superconvergence on Cartesian grids is desired.

Method: Constructs staggered quadrilateral meshes from Cartesian grids, using piecewise-constant spaces with staggered continuity for velocity, pressure, and velocity gradient. Gradient unknowns are locally eliminated via static condensation and mass lumping. An explicit pointwise formulation is derived. Extended to Navier-Stokes via a novel discrete convection term with second-order consistency, combined with SAV and Crank-Nicolson.

Result: Proves pressure robustness and second-order superconvergence on general non-uniform Cartesian grids. Navier-Stokes extension yields an unconditionally energy-stable, second-order accurate scheme. Numerical experiments validate the theory and demonstrate accuracy and robustness.

Conclusion: The scheme is simple to implement, pressure-robust, and superconvergent, with a practical extension to Navier-Stokes that is unconditionally energy-stable and second-order accurate.

Abstract: This paper develops a staggered discontinuous Galerkin (SDG) scheme based on Cartesian grids for Stokes equations that is simple to implement, intrinsically pressure-robust, and superconvergent for all variables. Instead of the composite meshes used in standard SDG, we construct staggered quadrilateral meshes from Cartesian grids. The scheme takes velocity, pressure, and velocity gradient as unknowns, and employs piecewise-constant spaces with carefully designed staggered continuity. The additional gradient unknowns are locally eliminated via static condensation and can be further removed by mass lumping without loss of accuracy. An explicit pointwise formulation of the scheme is derived, which facilitates implementation and enables a detailed pointwise analysis. We rigorously prove pressure robustness and second-order superconvergence, which holds on general non-uniform Cartesian grids. The scheme is further extended to Navier-Stokes equations by introducing a novel discrete convection term with second-order consistency. Combined with the scalar auxiliary variable (SAV) approach and the Crank-Nicolson (CN) scheme, this yields an unconditionally energy-stable and second-order accurate scheme. Numerical experiments validate the theory and demonstrate accuracy and robustness.

</details>


### [26] [A Discretely Entropy Conserving/Stable Flux Reconstruction Scheme via Hybridized Split Form Differential Operator and Generalized Entropy Projection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24257)
*Geng Liang,Junjie Wang,Hui Xu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A novel discretely entropy conserving/stable flux reconstruction scheme for nonlinear conservation laws, combining a hybridized split-form operator with generalized entropy projection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing entropy-conserving FR schemes typically require modifying quadrature weights or are limited to specific nodal distributions and correction functions, restricting flexibility.

Method: Integrates a hybridized split-form differential operator with a generalized entropy projection, built directly on the classical FR framework, to achieve discrete entropy conservation without altering original quadrature weights.

Result: The scheme preserves conservation properties and achieves entropy conservation in the semi-discrete sense, works for arbitrary nodal distributions and correction functions, and yields predicted accuracy with robust entropy behavior (isentropic vortex tests).

Conclusion: The proposed FR scheme enables entropy conservation/ stability for general nodal and correction setups while maintaining conservation, offering a robust framework for nonlinear conservation laws.

Abstract: This paper proposes a novel discretely entropy conserving/stable flux reconstruction (FR) scheme for nonlinear conservation laws. The scheme combines a hybridized split-form differential operator with a generalized entropy projection, enabling discrete entropy conservation for arbitrary nodal distributions and arbitrary correction functions without modifying the original quadrature weights. The formulation is built directly upon the classical FR framework and is shown to preserve conservation properties while achieving entropy conservation in the semi-discrete sense. Numerical experiments using the isentropic vortex problem confirm the theoretically predicted accuracy and demonstrate robust entropy behavior with entropy-dissipative interface fluxes.

</details>


### [27] [A Nonlinear Physics-based Reduced Order Model with Convolutional-based Operator Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24300)
*Anna Ivagnes,Giovanni Stabile,Gianluigi Rozza*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper proposes nonlinear ROMs by learning nonlinear low-dimensional representations of the governing operators rather than the solution field, using convolutional architectures and continuous convolutions, with RBF interpolation for online prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Projection-based ROMs rely on linear representations limited by the slow decay of the Kolmogorov N-width of the solution manifold, requiring many linear modes for accuracy. Existing nonlinear ROMs typically only compress the solution field, leaving operator representation linear.

Method: Introduces nonlinearity directly into the representation of governing operators, learning nonlinear low-dimensional representations of full-order operators while retaining an explicit reduced system. Uses convolutional architectures for locality/spatial structure, continuous convolutions for large sparse operators, a training strategy accounting for both reconstruction accuracy and reduced-order solution error, and RBF interpolation for online prediction at unseen parameters.

Result: Numerical experiments on three parametrized problems demonstrate the potential of nonlinear operator compression as an alternative strategy for equation-based ROMs.

Conclusion: Nonlinear operator compression offers a viable alternative to solution-field compression for equation-based ROMs, overcoming linear representation limitations while keeping an explicit reduced system.

Abstract: Reduced-order models (ROMs) are widely used to accelerate the solution of parametrized partial differential equations, with projection-based methods relying on a linear representation of the governing equations in a low-dimensional space. Their efficiency, however, is fundamentally limited by the Kolmogorov N-width of the solution manifold: when the N-width decays slowly, accurate approximations require a large number of linear modes. This limitation has motivated nonlinear ROMs, which typically replace the linear representation of the solution field with nonlinear latent representations learned through autoencoder architectures. In this work, we propose a different perspective by introducing nonlinearity directly into the representation of the governing operators. Rather than compressing only the solution field, we learn nonlinear low-dimensional representations of the full-order operators while retaining an explicit reduced system of equations. Convolutional architectures are employed to exploit the locality and spatial structure of differential operators, and the resulting compressed operators are coupled with a nonlinear representation of the solution through a training strategy that accounts for both reconstruction accuracy and the error of the reduced-order solution. To address large sparse operators arising from fine and unstructured discretizations, continuous convolutions are used to operate directly on their non-zero entries. Finally, radial basis function interpolation enables the prediction of the compressed operators at unseen parameter configurations, providing an efficient online stage without assembling the corresponding full-order system. Numerical experiments on three parametrized problems demonstrate the potential of nonlinear operator compression as an alternative strategy for equation-based ROMs.

</details>


### [28] [Autoencoders vs. Numerical Analysis--Informed Manifold Learning for Navier--Stokes Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24332)
*Alessandro Della Pia,Lucia Russo,Ioannis Kevrekidis,Constantinos Siettos*

Main category: math.NA
