# physics.comp-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [113] [Stochastic consensus dynamics for decentralized decision systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22454)
*André L. M. Vilela,Caio B. L. Silva,Kenric P. Nelson,Emilio Cobanera,Gaogao Dong*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies consensus formation in a stochastic consensus model on random networks, showing how initial conditions, connectivity, and size shape the emergence of unanimous states, with weak initial majorities amplified into predictable, reliable collective outcomes in large decentralized networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decentralized decision-making underlies collective phenomena in social, technological, and economic systems. Understanding consensus formation is important for blockchain networks, distributed AI, autonomous multi-agent systems, distributed control, and collective decision networks. The authors aim to clarify how initial conditions, network connectivity, and system size affect the emergence of unanimous states.

Method: The authors use a stochastic consensus model on random networks, examining the roles of initial conditions, network connectivity, and system size through stochastic dynamics and finite-size analysis. A key tool is finite-size scaling, which they use to characterize how the range of initial conditions leading to uncertain outcomes narrows with system size.

Result: Stochastic dynamics strongly amplify small initial majorities and suppress the competing state, driving the system toward a predictable collective outcome. Increased connectivity accelerates local agreement propagation and reduces the chance that fluctuations reverse the dominant state, though gains saturate at high connectivity. Larger networks need more updates for unanimity but are more reliable in selecting the initially favored state. The uncertain-outcome range narrows approximately as the inverse square root of system size.

Conclusion: The results reveal a collective amplification mechanism whereby weak initial asymmetries become increasingly decisive in large decentralized networks. The work offers a simple framework for understanding the efficiency, predictability, and reliability of consensus formation in distributed decision systems.

Abstract: A fundamental mechanism underlying collective phenomena in social, technological, and economic systems is decentralized decision-making, in which the behavior of individual agents follows from local interactions in the absence of central coordination. Consensus formation is a central feature of such systems, with relevance to blockchain networks, distributed artificial intelligence, autonomous multi-agent systems, distributed control, and collective decision networks. We investigate consensus formation using a stochastic consensus model on random networks and examine how initial conditions, network connectivity, and system size shape the emergence of unanimous states. We show that the stochastic dynamics strongly amplify small initial majorities, progressively suppress the competing state, and drive the system toward a predictable collective outcome. Network connectivity primarily controls the efficiency of this process: increasing connectivity accelerates the propagation of local agreement and reduces the likelihood that fluctuations reverse the initially dominant state, although these gains gradually saturate in highly connected networks. System size produces a complementary effect. Larger networks require more individual updates to reach unanimity, but they are also increasingly reliable in selecting the state favored by the initial majority. Finite-size analysis shows that the range of initial conditions associated with uncertain outcomes becomes progressively narrower as the network grows, decreasing approximately with the inverse square root of the system size. These results reveal a collective amplification mechanism by which weak initial asymmetries become increasingly decisive in large decentralized networks, and they provide a simple framework for understanding the efficiency, predictability, and reliability of consensus formation in distributed decision systems.

</details>


### [114] [TubeLab: Interactive inverse design of wind instrument bores with hard spectral constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22468)
*Carlo Andrea Rozzi,Andrea Ferroni*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: TubeLab: a browser-based Transfer Matrix Method acoustic simulator with adjoint-based inverse design for wind instrument bores, validated against FEM and experiments on didgeridoos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing wind instrument bores to hit specific musical resonance targets is challenging; existing tools lack accessible, fast inverse design with effective constraint handling.

Method: Direct problem solved via Transfer Matrix Method. Inverse design as Tikhonov-regularized least-squares with Jacobian from a single adjoint backward pass per mode. Preserved modes enforced via saddle-point formulation (hard equality constraints).

Result: Resonances match FEM within 1.21 cent (11 geometries, 132 mode pairs). Tap-tone validation on 16 CNC-machined didgeridoos (6 designs): 9.6 cent RMS over 189 mode pairs; replicate variance 4.5 cent RMS, ~36% of residual variance is fabrication scatter.

Conclusion: TubeLab offers an accurate, validated browser tool for bore inverse design, with performance near FEM and experimentally confirmed, largely limited by manufacturing tolerances.

Abstract: We present TubeLab, a browser-based acoustic simulator and inverse design tool for wind instrument bores without tone holes. The direct problem is solved by a Transfer Matrix Method. Inverse design - finding a bore correction that shifts selected resonance frequencies toward specified musical targets - is formulated as a Tikhonov-regularized least-squares problem whose Jacobian is computed by a single adjoint backward pass per mode. Modes whose frequencies must be preserved are handled by a saddle-point formulation that enforces hard equality constraints regardless of the regularization strength. Resonance frequencies agree with state-of-the-art finite-element calculations to within 1.21 cent over eleven geometries and 132 mode pairs. Experimental validation against tap-tone measurements of 16 numerical control-machined didgeridoos spanning six distinct bore designs yields 9.6 cent RMS deviation over 189 matched mode pairs. Replicate specimens of the same nominal bore disagree with each other by 4.5 cent RMS, so about 36% of the residual variance is fabrication scatter.

</details>


### [115] [Exact ballistic energy transport and emergent XXZ dynamics in an integrable three-state chain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22817)
*Hanbing Liang,Fujun Liu*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies ballistic energy transport and emergent spin dynamics in an integrable Hermitian three-state chain, connecting a clock interaction to a degenerate flag limit, and derives an exact effective XXZ description.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how coupling strength controls ballistic transport and spin dynamics in an integrable three-state chain bridging a clock model and a highly degenerate flag limit.

Method: Constructs a regular R-matrix to define a globally conserved energy current, analytically computes its full variance for the exact thermal Drude weight, and analyzes the strong-coupling effective theory via virtual spectator transitions.

Result: Obtains the exact positive leading high-temperature Drude weight and ballistic growth rate; strong coupling yields an effective spin-1/2 XXZ Hamiltonian with anisotropy Δ=-1/2, selecting the all-active two-color sector; periodic boundaries give a length-changing XXZ supercharge squaring to zero on a finite interval above the ground state.

Conclusion: The periodic effective theory rigorously reproduces the exact low-energy XXZ spectrum, all state multiplicities, and the macroscopic bulk free-energy density.

Abstract: We investigate the coupling dependence of ballistic energy transport and the emergent spin dynamics in an integrable Hermitian three-state chain that connects a clock interaction to a highly degenerate flag limit. By constructing a regular $R$-matrix to establish a globally conserved energy current, we analytically evaluate its full variance to obtain the exact, strictly positive leading high-temperature coefficient of the thermal Drude weight and the ballistic growth rate of the energy-correlation second moment. In the strong-coupling limit, the degeneracy is lifted by virtual transitions of a delocalized third-color spectator state, which generates an effective spin-$1/2$ XXZ Hamiltonian with anisotropy $Δ= -1/2$ and fundamentally selects the all-active two-color sector as the true ground state. For periodic boundaries, this virtual spectator motion introduces a positive length-changing XXZ supercharge squared that, for $L\ge4$, strictly annihilates all states within a finite, length-independent energy interval above the ground state. Consequently, we rigorously prove that the full periodic effective theory perfectly replicates the exact low-energy XXZ spectrum, including all state multiplicities, as well as its macroscopic bulk free-energy density.

</details>


### [116] [Diff-NekRS: A Scalable Differentiable Framework for Multi-Timestep Solver-in-the-Loop Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23208)
*Junoh Jung,Riccardo Balin,Bethany Lusch,Emil Constantinescu*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Diff-NekRS is a scalable differentiable framework embedding neural corrections into the GPU-accelerated NekRS incompressible-flow solver, enabling verified, multi-timestep solver-in-the-loop training that improves coarse-grid accuracy while preserving speedups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hybrid physics-ML solvers improve under-resolved simulations by embedding trainable corrections into time integration. But autoregressive inference causes repeated solver-model interactions that amplify small errors, motivating multi-timestep solver-in-the-loop training. Yet production solvers rarely expose the derivatives needed to backpropagate through such rollouts.

Method: Diff-NekRS embeds neural corrections directly in the GPU-accelerated NekRS solver. NekRS computes the authoritative forward trajectory; a manually implemented exact discrete adjoint differentiates the supported fully discrete timestep; LibTorch supplies neural vector-Jacobian products and parameter gradients.

Result: End-to-end Taylor and centered finite-difference tests verify gradients for 2D cylinder flow (2Dcyl) and 3D Taylor-Green vortex (3DTGV) across five horizons and 12-1,020 MPI ranks. At 1,020 ranks, training retains 54.5%-78.0% (2Dcyl) and 80.7%-81.9% (3DTGV) weak-scaling efficiency. In 200-step autoregressive inference, the M=50 model reduces median terminal relative L2 velocity error by 59.2% (2Dcyl) and 12.1% (3DTGV) versus the uncorrected coarse-grid P=2 baseline, with wall-clock speedups of 5.38x and 2.49x versus P=7.

Conclusion: These results establish a verified and scalable path for multi-timestep solver-in-the-loop training that improves coarse-grid trajectory accuracy while retaining a speed advantage over the high-order reference.

Abstract: Hybrid physics-machine-learning solvers improve under-resolved simulations by embedding trainable corrections into the time integration. During autoregressive inference, repeated solver-model interactions can amplify small errors, motivating multi-timestep solver-in-the-loop training. However, production solvers rarely expose the derivatives needed to backpropagate through such rollouts. We introduce Diff-NekRS, a scalable differentiable framework that embeds neural corrections directly in the GPU-accelerated NekRS incompressible-flow solver. NekRS computes the authoritative forward trajectory, a manually implemented exact discrete adjoint differentiates the supported fully discrete timestep, and LibTorch supplies neural vector-Jacobian products and parameter gradients. End-to-end Taylor and centered finite-difference tests verify the assembled gradient for two-dimensional cylinder flow (2Dcyl) and the three-dimensional Taylor-Green vortex (3DTGV) across five horizons and 12-1,020 MPI ranks. At 1,020 ranks, optimizer-enabled post-setup training updates retain 54.5%-78.0% and 80.7%-81.9% weak-scaling efficiency for 2Dcyl and 3DTGV, respectively. In 200-step autoregressive inference, the M = 50 model reduces the three-seed median terminal relative L2 velocity error by 59.2% for 2Dcyl and 12.1% for 3DTGV relative to the uncorrected coarse-grid P = 2 baseline, and retains wall-clock speedups of 5.38x and 2.49x, respectively, relative to the corresponding P = 7 configurations for equal simulated-time intervals. These results establish a verified and scalable path for multi-timestep solver-in-the-loop training that improves coarse-grid trajectory accuracy while retaining a speed advantage over the high-order reference

</details>


### [117] [MadVfold: accelerating NLO event generation and reducing negative weights with SIMD vectorization and GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23500)
*Andrea Valassi*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes "vectorized folding" using SIMD and GPUs to speed up NLO simulations in MadVfold, achieving 6x-9x speedups with folding and 3x without.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NLO simulations are essential for LHC physics analyses but expensive, being slow and producing negative weights that require much larger event samples. Folding reduces negative weights but is itself costly, creating a need for faster calculations.

Method: Proposes "vectorized folding" using SIMD and GPUs, and presents a CUDACPP-based implementation for MG5aMC within MadVfold, including an extension for unfolded NLO event generation. The work relies on a test-centric, LLM-assisted software development process.

Result: Preliminary results show overall speedups around 6x to 9x with folding and 3x without folding.

Conclusion: Vectorized folding accelerates expensive NLO simulations, offering significant performance gains and demonstrating the value of LLM-assisted, test-driven development.

Abstract: NLO simulations are essential for LHC physics analyses but are expensive, as they are not only slow but also lead to negative weights, which imply the need to simulate much larger samples of events. Folding is a powerful technique to reduce negative weights but is itself expensive. In this paper I propose ``vectorized folding'' as a new idea to speed up these calculations using SIMD and GPUs, and I present its CUDACPP-based implementation for MG5aMC in MadVfold, including its extension for unfolded NLO event generation. Preliminary results show overall speedups around 6x to 9x with folding and 3x without it. This work is based on a test-centric, LLM-assisted software development process.

</details>


### [118] [A subcell-refined entropy-residual-driven limiting strategy for high-order discontinuous Galerkin methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24268)
*Geng Liang,Rui Wang,Junjie Wang,Feng Wang,Xinlong Feng,Hui Xu*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Proposes a subcell-refined, entropy-residual-driven limiting strategy for high-order DG on Legendre-Gauss-Lobatto nodes that applies only nearest-neighbor pairwise dissipation with closed-form minimal coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust high-order DG simulations of nonlinear hyperbolic systems in under-resolved regimes need fine-grained, subcell-level dissipation control that preserves accuracy.

Method: A limiter adding nearest-neighbor pairwise dissipation within each element, with closed-form coefficients restoring the element entropy inequality. Framed as a diagonal locally stable approximation of entropy-stable methods via a generalized subcell framework. For Euler equations, a jump operator separately models thermal and shear entropy production while preserving velocity/pressure equilibrium, plus subcell refinement of the Zhang-Shu positivity limiter.

Result: Numerical tests confirm optimal high-order accuracy, strict entropy dissipation, and significantly reduced difficulty of a posteriori positivity-preserving procedures.

Conclusion: The subcell framework unifies split-form DG and residual-distribution entropy correction as special coefficient choices, yielding an accurate, entropy-stable, positivity-preserving DG limiter.

Abstract: Fine-grained, subcell-level dissipation control is essential for achieving robust high-order discontinuous Galerkin (DG) simulations of nonlinear hyperbolic systems in under-resolved regimes while preserving accuracy. This paper proposes a subcell-refined entropy-residual-driven limiting strategy for DG on Legendre-Gauss-Lobatto nodes. The limiter introduces only nearest-neighbor pairwise dissipation within each element, with closed-form coefficients that supply the minimal dissipation required to restore the element entropy inequality. The strategy is a diagonal, locally stable approximation of classical entropy-stable methods, and a generalized subcell framework reveals split-form DG and residual-distribution-based entropy correction schemes as particular choices of the limiting coefficients. For the Euler equations, a physically consistent jump operator separately models thermal and shear entropy production while preserving velocity and pressure equilibrium; a subcell refinement of the Zhang-Shu positivity limiter ensures pointwise positivity. Extensive numerical tests confirm that the scheme maintains optimal high-order accuracy, strictly enforces entropy dissipation, and significantly reduces the difficulty of a posteriori positivity-preserving procedures.

</details>


### [119] [VNS Tokamak for Medical Isotope Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24371)
*Christopher Ehrich,Pavel Pereslavtsev,Christian Bachmann,Christian Reiter*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The VNS tokamak is neutronically modeled in Serpent 2 and OpenMC, comparing coupled neutron-photon results; radioisotope production potential is assessed across multiple isotopes, with 60Co optimized and outage sensitivity simulated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A Volumetric Neutron Source tokamak is proposed for testing fusion components under neutron irradiation and for radioisotope production, requiring validated neutronics modeling and feasibility assessment.

Method: VNS geometry modeled in Serpent 2.2.2 and OpenMC 0.15.2; coupled neutron-photon simulations compared flux, spectra, and reaction rates in blanket and vacuum vessel; radioisotope yields simulated in Serpent 2 for capsule and Cobalt plate facilities; target heating and outage sensitivity analyzed.

Result: Both codes agree well, with largest discrepancy in (n,2n) reactions. Serpent 2 is faster for coupled simulations, OpenMC for neutron-only. Large-volume production potential found for 99Mo, 131I, 225Ac, 177Lu, 192Ir, 64Cu, 67Cu, 161Tb, 153Sm, but lower for 203Pb. LEU target heating suggests mass or cooling adjustment needed. Optimized 60Co yielded 1.2 GBq/mg and 100,000 TBq after 3 years.

Conclusion: VNS shows promising potential for large-scale radioisotope production, with 60Co robust to plant dwell-time and feasible irradiation restart for several isotopes; LEU target heating requires optimization.

Abstract: The Volumetric Neutron Source (VNS) tokamak is a proposed fusion reactor for testing components under fusion neutron irradiation, and has potential use for radioisotope production. The VNS geometry is modeled in the Serpent 2.2.2 and OpenMC 0.15.2 neutronics codes. Coupled neutron-photon simulations compared fluxes, spectra, and selected reaction rates in the blanket and vacuum vessel. Good agreement was found overall, with the largest difference found in (n, 2n) reactions. On an HPC cluster, Serpent 2 was found to have shorter computation time in coupled simulations, while OpenMC was faster in neutron only simulations. Radioisotope production yields were simulated in Serpent 2.2.2 for capsule and Cobalt plate irradiation facilities. Results indicate potential for large volume production of 99Mo, 131I, 225Ac, 177Lu, 192Ir, 64Cu, 67Cu, 161Tb, and 153Sm while 203Pb indicates lower potential. 100Mo and LEU target heating was calculated, suggesting the LEU target mass or the cooling may need adjustment. Optimized 60Co production yielded 1.2 GBq/mg and 100,000 TBq after a 3-year irradiation period. Sensitivity to plant outage for 99Mo, 131I, 177Lu, and 60Co was simulated, suggesting irradiation can be restarted for the same isotope loading and demonstrated long-lived 60Co to be robust to long plant dwell-time.

</details>


<div id='physics.plasm-ph'></div>

# physics.plasm-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [120] [Proton Heating Rates in the Inner Heliosphere: Helios Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22552)
*Alaa Fayad,Anna Tenerani,Chadi Salem*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Using Helios 1/2 data, the authors examine the radial evolution of adiabatic invariants and anisotropic heating rates of fast solar wind protons (bulk, core, beam), accounting for heat fluxes and Parker spiral deviations, finding all populations need perpendicular heating while parallel behavior differs by population.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solar wind protons show a temperature anisotropy deviating from double-adiabatic prediction, implying an additional energy source/heating mechanism that needs to be identified.

Method: Analyze Helios 1 and 2 data to characterize radial evolution of adiabatic invariants and anisotropic heating rates for fast wind protons, treating bulk, core, and beam components separately, while including proton heat fluxes and deviations from the Parker spiral due to large-amplitude fluctuations.

Result: Including heat fluxes and Parker spiral deviations significantly changes parallel heating rates versus earlier work. Power-law heating estimates and adiabatic-invariant evolution yield consistent results on the same dataset. All populations require net perpendicular heating; core protons need parallel heating while beam protons undergo parallel cooling, and the total population shows net parallel cooling.

Conclusion: The results are consistent with combined turbulence and core-beam kinetic instabilities driving preferential perpendicular heating plus parallel cooling in fast solar wind; core proton parallel heating may indicate Alfvén wave decay.

Abstract: Solar wind protons exhibit a temperature anisotropy that deviates from the double-adiabatic prediction, suggesting an additional energy source. To investigate solar wind heating mechanisms, we analyze Helios 1 and 2 data and characterize the radial evolution of the adiabatic invariants and anisotropic heating rates of fast wind protons, considering both the bulk population and its core and beam components separately. Unlike previous studies, we account for proton heat fluxes and deviations from the Parker spiral caused by large-amplitude fluctuations, which causes significant differences in the parallel heating rates compared to earlier results. Although earlier work has questioned the reliability of power laws to estimate heating rates and proposed as a more robust approach the investigation of the radial evolution of adiabatic invariants, we show that the two approaches yield consistent results when applied to the same dataset. Our analysis shows that all proton populations considered require net perpendicular heating, consistent with earlier studies. In contrast, the parallel energy evolution differs between populations. Core protons require parallel heating, whereas beam protons undergo parallel cooling although the total proton population undergoes a net parallel cooling. This behavior is consistent with the combined action of turbulence and core-beam kinetic instabilities, which together can drive preferential perpendicular heating while simultaneously produce parallel cooling in the fast solar wind. Core proton parallel heating could instead be a signature of Alfvén wave decay.

</details>


### [121] [Interactions Between Two Magnetized Plasma Pressure Filaments: Drift-Alfvén Modes and Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22627)
*Thomas Simala-Grant,Scott Karbashewski,Richard Sydora,Bart Van Compernolle*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Experiments in a large linear magnetized plasma device study the merging dynamics of two closely spaced magnetized plasma filaments, showing that interaction and drift-Alfvén mode coupling depend on separation distance and symmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine the separation scale at which cross-field interaction occurs between localized magnetized plasma filaments, and to understand the physics of filament merging dynamics and mode coupling relevant to plasma transport.

Method: A two-filament configuration is used in a large linear magnetized plasma device. Fluctuation analysis of the ion saturation current is performed to identify drift-Alfvén modes in each filament, and the separation distance between filaments is systematically varied (including asymmetric heating conditions).

Result: Unstable drift-Alfvén modes exist on each filament, driven mainly by the steep electron temperature gradient. At separations ≥ ~5 filament sizes, no interaction occurs and modes remain uncoupled. At closer distances, strong interaction with charge and energy transfer produces inter-filament electric fields that rotate the filaments and influence merging. With asymmetric heating, the cooler filament wraps around the other and modes become fully coupled in a global structure. Transport is explained via E x B drift and local potential wells, with mode coupling occurring when filaments are within a collisionless skin depth.

Conclusion: Filament interaction and drift-Alfvén mode coupling are governed by filament separation relative to a critical scale (~5 filament sizes for onset, collisionless skin depth for full coupling), with E x B drift and potential wells explaining transport and merging behavior.

Abstract: A set of experiments on merging dynamics of localized magnetized plasma pressure filaments in close proximity has been carried out in a large linear magnetized plasma device. A two filament configuration is used to determine the separation scale of cross-field interaction. Fluctuation analysis of the ion saturation current confirms the presence of unstable drift-Alfvén modes on each filament which are predominantly driven by the steep electron temperature gradient. When the filaments are separated a distance of approximately five times the size of a single filament or greater, no interaction is observed and the drift-Alfvén modes remain uncoupled. When brought closer together, significantly stronger interaction occurs with transfer of charge and energy, leading to the generation of inter-filament electric fields. This has the effect of rotating the filaments and influencing the merging dynamics. When the filaments are asymmetrically heated, the cooler filament becomes wrapped around the other, and the drift-Alfvén modes become fully coupled in a global mode structure. Transport between the filaments is explained in terms of E x B drift and the local potential wells of the filaments, and drift-Alfvén mode coupling occurs when the filaments are brought within the distance of a collisionless skin depth.

</details>


### [122] [Interaction renormalization and the plasma staircase](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22915)
*Alexander V. Milovanov,Alexander Iomin,Jens Juul Rasmussen*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper resolves the question of dynamical and structural stability of E×B staircases in fusion plasma by combining the Hasegawa-Wakatani drift-wave turbulence model with quantum-field-theory interaction renormalization, showing the staircase is stable only when δ > δc = π.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The stability of E×B staircases in magnetically confined fusion plasma has attracted strong interest due to its combined scientific and practical importance, and an existing open question about their dynamical and structural stability needed resolution.

Method: A new approach combining the paradigmatic Hasegawa-Wakatani model of electrostatic drift-wave turbulence with an idea of interaction renormalization imported from quantum field theory, based on the Schrödinger-Newton equation with a renormalized interaction constant.

Result: The plasma staircase is robust and stable when the nonadiabaticity parameter δ exceeds a critical value and unstable otherwise. The critical value is an exact result of the interaction model: δc = π. For δ < δc, the unstable system expands radially with a second moment growing as t^(2/3).

Conclusion: The results have important implications for implementing arrays of semipermeable transport barriers in tokamaks, suggesting a threshold condition for their generation.

Abstract: We resolve an existing question concerning dynamical and structural stability of the E {\times} B staircases in magnetically confined fusion plasma. The problem has stirred considerable interest in the literature due to a unique combination of its scientific and practical aspects. Here, we report a new approach to the problem by combining the paradigmatic Hasegawa-Wakatani model of electrostatic drift-wave turbulence with an idea of interaction renormalization, imported from quantum field theory. It is shown based on the Schrödinger-Newton equation with renormalized interaction constant that the plasma staircase is robust and stable as soon as the nonadiabaticity parameter, δ, goes above a certain critical value, and is unstable otherwise. This critical value is an exact result of the interaction model and is found to be δc = π. For δ < δc , the unstable system expands in the radial direction, resulting in a radial second moment that grows with time t as t^(2/3). The results obtained bear important implications with regard to the implementation of arrays of semipermeable transport barriers in tokamaks, suggesting a threshold condition for their generation.

</details>


### [123] [Real-Time Plasma State Prediction via FPGA-Accelerated Quantized Recurrent Probabilistic Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23141)
*Daniel Gaytan-Villarreal,Aiken Xie,Tu Pham,Rohit Sonker,Chiara Amendola,Matteo Cremonesi,Cong Hao,Jeff Schneider*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents an end-to-end workflow for deploying a recurrent probabilistic neural network (RPNN) on FPGA hardware for real-time plasma state estimation in Tokamak devices, achieving deterministic sub-10μs latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time plasma state estimation for Tokamak control is challenging due to the stringent latency requirements of the plasma control system (PCS).

Method: The authors combine architecture size reduction with quantization-aware training via QKeras, then synthesize the model using hls4ml targeting a Xilinx Alveo U50 device.

Result: The design fits comfortably within all four resource budgets (DSP, LUT, FF, BRAM) at deterministic sub-10μs single-timestep latency.

Conclusion: The proposed deployment meets the requirements for real-time inference inside a model-predictive-control-style plasma control loop.

Abstract: Real time plasma state estimation for control of Tokamak devices are challenging due to the stringent latency requirements of the plasma control system (PCS). We present an end-to-end workflow for deploying a recurrent probabilistic neural network (RPNN) on FPGA hardware. We combine architecture size reduction with quantization-aware training via QKeras. The model is then synthesized using hls4ml, targeting a Xilinx Alveo U50 device. We report a design that fits comfortably within all four resource budgets (DSP, LUT, FF, BRAM) at deterministic sub-10~$μ$s single-timestep latency, meeting the requirements for real-time inference inside a model-predictive-control-style plasma control loop.

</details>


### [124] [Scalar Dissipation Criticality in Compressible Magnetized Turbulence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23177)
*Svitlana Mayboroda,David N. Spergel,Camillo De Lellis*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper extends Obukhov-Corrsin scalar turbulence theory to compressible flows with variable, anisotropic diffusivity by introducing a "transport landscape" (density-diffusivity matrix), proving that anomalous scalar dissipation is impossible when α+2β>1, independent of anisotropy and regularity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical Obukhov-Corrsin theory assumes incompressible flows with isotropic, uniform diffusivity. Extending it to compressible flows with spatially variable, anisotropic diffusivity is challenging because the commutator between filtering and diffusion is sign-indefinite and difficult to control. This matters for applications like passive-scalar transport in gases and magnetized plasmas.

Method: The authors combine density and diffusivity into a single positive matrix field called a "transport landscape." This construction yields an exact scale-by-scale balance in which the sign-indefinite commutator between filtering and diffusion is eliminated rather than estimated. They analyze scaling of density-weighted third-order velocity and scalar increments (ℓ^α and ℓ^β).

Result: They prove that anomalous scalar dissipation is impossible when α+2β>1. This threshold is independent of anisotropy and spatial regularity of the diffusivity, as long as it stays uniformly elliptic. Codimension-one shocks in velocity and scalar have α=β=1/3, placing them exactly at the critical threshold. For stationary turbulence, they obtain an exact density-weighted constant-flux relation, a compressible analogue of Yaglom's law.

Conclusion: The results apply to passive-scalar transport in gases and magnetized plasmas, offering testable diagnostics for compressible MHD turbulence simulations. Since the same operator governs anisotropic heat conduction, the results extend to electron temperature in magnetized plasmas, implying that gradient statistics must be contracted with the transport landscape rather than with the density.

Abstract: We extend the Obukhov-Corrsin theory of scalar turbulence to compressible flows with spatially variable, anisotropic diffusivity. Combining density and diffusivity into a single positive matrix field, a transport landscape, permits an exact scale-by-scale balance in which the sign-indefinite commutator between filtering and diffusion is eliminated rather than estimated. If the density-weighted third-order velocity and scalar increments scale as $\ell^α$ and $\ell^β$, respectively, we prove that anomalous scalar dissipation is impossible when $α+2β>1$. Remarkably, this threshold is independent of the anisotropy and spatial regularity of the diffusivity, provided it remains uniformly elliptic. Codimension-one shocks in both velocity and scalar have $α=β=1/3$ and therefore lie exactly at the critical threshold. For statistically stationary turbulence we further obtain an exact density-weighted constant-flux relation, providing a compressible analogue of the relation underlying Yaglom's law. The results apply directly to passive-scalar transport in both gases and magnetized plasmas and provide testable diagnostics for simulations of compressible magnetohydrodynamic turbulence. Because the same operator governs anisotropic heat conduction, the results carry over to the electron temperature of a magnetized plasma, and they imply that gradient statistics in such flows must be contracted with the transport landscape rather than with the density.

</details>


### [125] [VEQDB: A Compact and Reconstructible Multi-Device Tokamak Equilibrium Database](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23296)
*Huasheng Xie,Ruohan Zhang,Xingyu Li,Feng Zhang,Zhengxiong Wang*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: VEQDB is an open, compact, reconstructible fixed-boundary tokamak equilibrium database using continuous MXH–Chebyshev geometry and independent profile roots, decoupling physics from rectangular meshes. Its first release contains 13,291 validated equilibria across 267 devices, with low projection error and large size reduction versus G-EQDSK.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tokamak equilibria are typically exchanged as gridded G-EQDSK files, whose conventions, resolutions, and machine-specific formats hinder cross-device comparison and data-driven modeling. There is a need for a common, compact, reconstructible equilibrium representation.

Method: VEQDB builds on continuous MXH–Chebyshev geometry with independent physical-profile roots, decoupling authoritative equilibrium physics from rectangular meshes. This enables continuous evaluation and metric differentiation at arbitrary resolutions. It uses an automated numerical validation pipeline and is structured as an extensible repository.

Result: The inaugural release provides 13,291 accepted equilibria across 267 conventional and spherical tokamaks, including parameter-sampled Grad–Shafranov solutions, G-EQDSK projections for EAST, MAST-U, and ITER scales, and controlled variation families with explicit provenance. Benchmark projections reproduce normalized flux maps with RMS errors of 1.09e-3 to 1.45e-3, and compact JSON achieves 89–96-fold size reduction vs. 129×129 G-EQDSK files. The release is 41 MB raw JSON, 18 MB compressed, with all records passing reload and evaluation tests.

Conclusion: VEQDB establishes an extensible, provenance-preserving foundation for equilibrium studies, reduced-order surrogate modeling, and cross-machine workflows.

Abstract: Tokamak equilibria are commonly exchanged as gridded G-EQDSK files whose conventions, resolutions, and machine-specific formats impede cross-device comparisons and data-driven modeling. Here, we present VEQDB, an open, compact, and reconstructible fixed-boundary equilibrium database built on continuous MXH--Chebyshev geometry and independent physical-profile roots. By decoupling authoritative equilibrium physics from rectangular meshes, VEQDB enables continuous evaluation and metric differentiation at arbitrary application-demanded resolutions. Backed by an automated numerical validation pipeline, VEQDB is structured as an extensible repository for ongoing community expansion. Its inaugural release provides 13,291 accepted equilibria across 267 conventional and spherical tokamaks, encompassing parameter-sampled Grad--Shafranov solutions, G-EQDSK projections spanning EAST, MAST-U, and ITER scales, and controlled variation families with explicit provenance. Benchmark projections reproduce normalized flux maps with RMS errors between $1.09 \times 10^{-3}$ and $1.45 \times 10^{-3}$, while compact JSON representations achieve an 89--96-fold size reduction relative to standard $129 \times 129$ G-EQDSK files. The complete initial release occupies 41~MB in raw JSON and 18~MB in compressed archives, and all records successfully passed independent reload and evaluation tests. VEQDB establishes an extensible, provenance-preserving foundation for equilibrium studies, reduced-order surrogate modeling, and cross-machine workflows.

</details>


### [126] [Machine Learning Accelerated Plasma Simulation through Physics Guided Time Jumps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23358)
*Asif Iqbal,Peng Zhang*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A hybrid ML-physics scheme combines learned time jumps with a drift-diffusion-Poisson solver (PASCHEN-1D), achieving up to 4.70x CPU speedup for nanosecond pulsed nitrogen discharges while closely matching reference results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ordinary plasma simulations are computationally expensive, especially for capturing fast transients in pulsed discharges. A practical way to reduce cost is to accelerate an established physics solver with machine learning rather than replacing it entirely.

Method: A neural network corrects a low-cost physics forecast over multiple time steps, while the original solver reconstructs the state and advances it between jumps via short reanchoring intervals. Implemented in PASCHEN-1D, a 1D drift-diffusion-Poisson solver.

Result: Up to 4.70x CPU rollout speedup over the ordinary solver for ns pulsed nitrogen discharges. Bulk densities and E-fields agree closely with reference; sheath width, gap voltage, and discharge current reproduce main temporal features. Largest discrepancies occur in the cathode sheath during fast transients. A trade-off among accuracy, acceleration, and stability is found over 500 ns. A model trained on 1000 grid points transfers usefully to 500- and 1500-point meshes without retraining.

Conclusion: Augmenting an established physics solver with machine learning provides a practical route to accelerating plasma simulations while maintaining accuracy and showing reasonable mesh-transferability.

Abstract: We present a machine-learning-accelerated plasma simulation scheme that combines learned time jumps with ordinary physics evolution. A neural network corrects a low-cost physics forecast over multiple time steps, while the original solver reconstructs the state and advances it between jumps through short reanchoring intervals. The scheme is implemented in PASCHEN-1D, a one-dimensional drift-diffusion-Poisson solver, achieving up to $4.70 \times$ CPU rollout speedup relative to the ordinary solver for nanosecond pulsed nitrogen discharges. The simulations capture pre-breakdown evolution, rapid sheath formation, expansion and collapse under pulsed-voltage excitation, and post-pulse afterglow. Bulk densities and electric fields agree closely with the ordinary-solver reference, while cathode sheath width, gap voltage, and discharge current reproduce the principal temporal features. The largest spatial discrepancies occur in the cathode sheath during fast transients. A sweep of the physics-reanchoring duration reveals a trade-off between accuracy, acceleration, and numerical stability over the tested 500 ns simulation interval. A model trained on simulations with 1000 grid points also shows useful transfer to 500- and 1500-point meshes without retraining. These results demonstrate a practical route to accelerating plasma simulations by augmenting an established physics solver with machine learning.

</details>


### [127] [Plasma localization of charged debris](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23437)
*Bikramjit Joardar,Madhurjya P. Bora*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Charged debris in flowing plasma generates learnable disturbance structures that can be used to infer its position remotely via sparse regression, working in transonic and supersonic flows but not subsonic ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether plasma disturbances from a charged debris object can reveal its position without directly sampling the object or its sheath, enabling a proof-of-principle route toward remote debris localization.

Method: A 1D open-boundary electrostatic particle-in-cell (PIC) model simulates a flowing electron-ion plasma with an initially uncharged debris object that self-consistently charges and drives an ion-ion counter-streaming instability (IICSI) reaching a statistically stationary state. A control simulation without debris isolates debris-induced changes. Precursor and wake are characterized by spatial extent, fluctuation power, and frequency-wavenumber spectra. Weak-form sparse regression recovers reduced fluid and kinetic residuals and learns an empirical relation linking disturbance envelopes to equilibrium flow, debris charge, and distance. Leave-one-out validation infers debris position from plasma response.

Result: The procedure successfully localizes the debris in transonic and supersonic flows, while subsonic cases remain unresolved.

Conclusion: Debris-induced plasma disturbances possess a learnable spatial structure, offering a proof-of-principle route toward future remote localization with more realistic multidimensional models, though it does not constitute an operational debris-detection scheme.

Abstract: We investigate whether the plasma disturbances generated by a charged debris object can be used to infer its position without directly sampling the object or its immediate sheath. A one-dimensional open-boundary electrostatic particle-in-cell (PIC) framework is used to model a continuously flowing electron-ion plasma containing an initially uncharged debris object. The debris charges self-consistently and produces an ion-ion counter-streaming instability (IICSI), which is sustained and reaches a statistically stationary state. A matching simulation without debris provides a controlled background against which debris-induced changes in different plasma fields are identified. The precursor and wake are examined through their spatial extent, fluctuation power, and frequency--wavenumber spectra. Weak-form sparse regression is then used as a supporting tool to recover reduced fluid and kinetic residuals and to learn an empirical relation connecting the precursor and wake disturbance envelopes to the equilibrium plasma flow, debris charge, and distance from the source. The predictive capability is examined by successively treating each simulation as an independent case. For each evaluation, the model is constructed from the remaining simulations, while the excluded simulation is used only to infer the debris position from its plasma response. For the idealized cold-ion regime considered here, the procedure successfully localizes the debris in transonic and supersonic flows, whereas the subsonic cases remain unresolved. These results demonstrate that debris-induced plasma disturbances possess a learnable spatial structure and provide a proof-of-principle route toward future remote localization using more realistic multidimensional models; they do not constitute an operational debris-detection scheme.

</details>


### [128] [On the statistical theory of strong electrolytes and high-temperature plasmas: New applications of the work by Yukhnovskii and Kelbg II](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23621)
*W. Ebeling,M. Holovko*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Extends the theory of exponential potentials (from Kramers, Hellmann, Glaubermann, Yukhnovskii, Kelbg) with new quantum-statistical results, deriving a Kelbg quantum potential for exponential interactions and applying it to alkali and fusion plasmas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exponential potentials have been used since Kramers, Hellmann, Glaubermann, Yukhnovskii, and Kelbg to solve problems in quantum chemistry, ionic solutions, and plasmas, but the theory needed further development, particularly regarding quantum-statistical treatments.

Method: The theory of exponential potentials is developed further, deriving the Kelbg quantum potential for exponential interactions, and analyzing screening and thermodynamic stability. Qualitative analytical applications are made to alkali plasmas, fusion plasmas, and phase transitions in electrolytes and alkali plasmas.

Result: New quantum-statistical results are obtained, including the Kelbg quantum potential for exponential interactions, with analysis of screening and thermodynamic stability. The exponential potential combined with early analytical results of Yukhnovskii and Kelbg enables a qualitative analytical treatment of difficult problems such as phase transitions in electrolytes and alkali plasmas.

Conclusion: The exponential potential and early analytical work of Yukhnovskii and Kelbg permit a qualitative analytical treatment of complex problems including phase transitions in electrolytes and alkali plasmas, with new applications to alkali and fusion plasma theory.

Abstract: Exponential potentials were used since Kramers, Hellman, Glauberman, Yukhnovskii and Kelbg for solving problems of quantum chemistry, ionic solutions and plasmas. Here we develop the theory further and add new results, in particular with respect to the quantum-statistical theory. In particular, we derive the Kelbg quantum potential for the exponential interactions and discuss screening as well as the problem of thermodynamic stability. Further we give new applications to a qualitative theory of alkali plasmas and fusion plasmas. We show that the exponential potential and the early analytical results of Yukhnovskii and Kelbg allow a qualtative analytical treatment of such difficult problems as the phase transitions in electrolytes and alkali plasmas.

</details>


### [129] [Effects of particle-induced electron emission on transport properties in electrically-biased plasma sheaths under fusion-relevant conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23689)
*Lucio Murillo,Chirag Skolar,Kolter Bradshaw,Bhuvana Srinivasan*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper implements energy-dependent particle-induced electron emission (PIEE) in a continuum-kinetic sheath model to study how ion- and electron-induced emission affect plasma sheath scaling under wall bias. Electron-induced emission primarily drives the classical-to-space-charge-limited transition, and PIEE increases wall heat and particle loads mainly via collisional energy transfer in the presheath.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Particle-induced electron emission strongly influences plasma sheath behavior and wall loads in magnetic fusion devices, yet its energy dependence is often treated simplistically. A rigorous, energy-dependent treatment is needed to clarify how ion- versus electron-induced emission governs sheath structure and scaling with applied wall bias.

Method: A continuum-kinetic framework with a fully energy-dependent, dynamic boundary condition for PIEE. Novel PIEE modeling includes: (1) improved fitting functions for yield and spectra; (2) stopping powers from SRIM combined with a Lindhard formula plus modified Bethe formula; (3) a DFT-based binding energy correction to PIEE spectra and ion-induced yield. Tungsten and graphite walls are simulated for magnetic fusion relevance, with fluid-theory equations derived to predict the relative importance of ion- and electron-induced emission.

Result: The classical-to-space-charge-limited (SCL) sheath transition depends primarily on electron-induced emission, confirming theoretical predictions. Prior claims of increased heat and particle loads from electron emission are supported, but via different mechanisms: the increases are driven mainly by collisional energy transfer from emitted electrons in the presheath. Quantitative predictions match previous modeling and experimental measurements.

Conclusion: A rigorous energy-dependent PIEE boundary condition yields physically consistent sheath scaling and wall-load predictions, highlighting electron-induced emission as the key driver of SCL transition and collisional presheath energy transfer as the main cause of enhanced wall loads, with results validating prior work.

Abstract: A rigorous implementation of energy-dependent ion- and electron-induced electron emission in a continuum-kinetic framework is used to reveal their effects on the scaling of plasma properties in the sheath with an applied bias potential to the walls. The approach comes with a novel methodology for modeling particle-induced electron emission (PIEE) that includes 1) improved fitting functions for the yield and spectra, 2) the use of SRIM and a summation of the Lindhard formula and a modified Bethe formula for obtaining accurate stopping powers, and 3) a binding energy correction to the PIEE spectra and ion-induced yield based on density functional theory (DFT) calculations. The emission models are implemented as a fully energy dependent and dynamic boundary condition. For this investigation, tungsten and graphite walls are studied for their relevance in magnetic fusion experiments. Equations are derived from fluid theory that predict the relative importance of ion- and electron-induced emission on the structure of the sheath. The simulations provide evidence for the theoretical predictions, showing that a transition from a classical to space-charge limited (SCL) sheath depends primarily on electron-induced emission. Furthermore, claims in previous literature of increased heat and particle loads to the walls due to electron emission are supported, however differing mechanisms for the increase are observed. The increased thermal and particle fluxes due to PIEE are primarily driven by collisional transfer of energy from emitted electrons in the presheath. Finally, quantitative predictions for the device modeled in this study coincide with previous modeling efforts and experimental measurements.

</details>


### [130] [Chaos-induced transport due to electrostatic-fluctuations in a toroidal plasma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23791)
*Andres E. Medrano-Albarran,Julio J. Martinell*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Electrostatic fluctuation-driven plasma transport is studied via a test particle model in toroidal geometry, comparing chaotic Hamiltonian dynamics and stochastic wave spectra, both yielding diffusive transport; zonal flows create transport barriers confirming chaos-driven transport.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the mechanisms by which electrostatic fluctuations drive plasma transport in tokamak-like toroidal geometry, distinguishing between transport arising from chaotic particle motion and from intrinsic stochasticity in the wave spectrum.

Method: A test particle model in toroidal geometry with a model wave spectrum that makes the Hamiltonian time-dependent, enabling chaotic particle motion. A second approach introduces a stochastic process within the wave spectrum. Lyapunov exponents are computed to confirm chaos, and zonal flow effects are added to test transport barrier formation.

Result: Both the chaotic Hamiltonian case and the stochastic wave spectrum case produce diffusive transport. Approximate Lyapunov exponents corroborate the presence of chaotic motion. Zonal flows suppress radial particle fluxes, forming a transport barrier in the chaotic transport cases.

Conclusion: Zonal flow-induced transport barriers reproduce the robust invariant torus resilience to chaos seen in Hamiltonian chaos models, supporting chaotic motion as the mechanism responsible for the observed transport.

Abstract: Plasma transport produced by electrostatic fluctuations is studied using a test particle model in a toroidal geometry. A model wave spectrum is assumed which introduces a time dependence in the Hamiltonian description of particle motion in the strong magnetic field. This allows for the existence of chaotic particle motion which gives rise to transport processes. Another way of producing transport is by introducing a stochastic process inside the wave spectrum. The two possibilities are explored and it is shown that both cases give rise to diffusive transport. The presence of chaotic motion is corroborated by computing approximate Lyapunov exponents. Additionally the presence of zonal flow is considered which can give rise to a transport barrier by suppressing radial particle fluxes, in the cases that show chaotic transport. This effect gives support to the existence of chaotic motion as responsible for transport since it reproduces the behavior of the robust invariant torus resilience to chaos, acting as a transport barrier, observed in Hamiltonian models of chaos.

</details>


### [131] [Physics-Informed Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks for Grad-Shafranov Tokamak Equilibria](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23846)
*D. A. Kaltsas,A. Kuiroukidis,J. Liu,L. Magafas,G. N. Throumoulopoulos*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper applies physics-informed Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) with guided training to solve the fixed-boundary Grad-Shafranov equilibrium problem, achieving stable and accurate nonlinear tokamak equilibria computation with simultaneous profile parameter identification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computing nonlinear Grad-Shafranov equilibria with standard MLPs under unguided physics-informed training is elusive or computationally inefficient, limiting the construction of tokamak-relevant axisymmetric MHD equilibria.

Method: Equation-driven, physics-constrained deep learning using Physics-Informed KANs to approximate GS solutions satisfying boundary conditions. Handles linear (Solov'ev) and nonlinear profiles, including a highly nonlinear pressure profile with H-mode features (pedestals, bootstrap current). Employs guided training: homotopy-based continuation curriculum learning and transfer learning via pretrained networks, combined with self-scaled Broyden optimization.

Result: The framework overcomes MLP limitations, enabling stable, efficient, and accurate equilibrium computation for nonlinear equilibria, with simultaneous profile parameter identification under equilibrium constraints.

Conclusion: The combination of KAN architecture, guided training schemes, and self-scaled Broyden optimization is key to successfully solving nonlinear GS equilibria and identifying profile parameters.

Abstract: We employ equation-driven, physics-constrained deep learning to solve the fixed-boundary Grad-Shafranov (GS) equilibrium problem, constructing axisymmetric magnetohydrodynamic equilibria with tokamak-relevant characteristics. Equilibria across linear (Solov'ev) and nonlinear profile functions are constructed using Physics-Informed Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) that approximate GS solutions while satisfying appropriate boundary conditions. A highly nonlinear pressure profile recreating high-confinement mode phenomenology, such as pressure pedestals and significant bootstrap current components, is also considered. To enable efficient convergence, guided training schemes are employed, specifically homotopy-based continuation curriculum learning and transfer learning via pretrained networks. While computing nonlinear equilibria employing standard Multi-Layer Perceptrons under unguided physics-informed training remains an elusive or computationally inefficient task, our framework overcomes this limitation. Specifically, we demonstrate that the combination of three key elements, namely KAN architecture, guided training schemes, and the self-scaled Broyden optimization method, enables stable, efficient, and accurate equilibrium computation with simultaneous profile parameter identification in view of equilibrium constraints.

</details>


### [132] [Geodesic Acoustic Modes in pair plasmas confined in tokamak magnetic fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24256)
*Alessandro Biancalani*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper derives the dispersion relation of the Geodesic Acoustic Mode (GAM) in pair plasmas, where ions and electrons share the same mass.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GAMs are plasma perturbations crucial for turbulence regulation and thus for plasma confinement in tokamaks. Pair plasmas (e.g., electron-positron) are experimentally proposed for studying fundamental plasma physics and early-universe formation, yet the GAM dispersion relation in this equal-mass regime had not been derived.

Method: Theoretical derivation of the GAM dispersion relation assuming equal ion and electron masses (pair plasma conditions).

Result: The dispersion relation for the Geodesic Acoustic Mode in pair plasmas is obtained. (Specific formula not stated in the abstract.)

Conclusion: The derived relation provides a theoretical basis for understanding GAM behavior in pair plasmas, supporting experimental efforts in electron-positron confinement and fundamental plasma/early-universe studies.

Abstract: This paper is devoted to the derivation of the dispersion relation of the Geodesic Acoustic Mode in pair plasmas, i.e. assuming that ions and electrons have the same mass. Geodesic Acoustic Modes are plasma perturbations playing a crucial role in turbulence regulation, and therefore in the determination of the plasma confinement in tokamaks. Experiments with pair plasmas, like electron-positron plasmas, have been proposed with different kinds of confinements, and aim to study fundamental processes in plasma physics and understanding the formation of the early universe.

</details>


### [133] [Horizon-Aware Early Event Prediction for Tokamak Disruption Alarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24443)
*Takeshi Koshizuka,Takaharu Yaguchi*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces Early Event Prediction (EEP) objectives into survival-based tokamak disruption prediction, proposing TLS and survTLS on top of a Deep Survival Machines baseline, evaluated on DIII-D, C-Mod, and EAST.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing full-distribution survival methods model the complete residual time-to-disruption distribution, but operational decisions depend on disruption risk within a finite prediction horizon, creating a mismatch.

Method: Use DSM as a full-distribution baseline and apply two EEP methods: Temporal Label Smoothing (TLS), which predicts disruption probability within a finite horizon, and survTLS, which also models within-horizon event-time distribution. A common causal encoder is used for comparison.

Result: TLS achieves the best mean alarm performance on DIII-D and EAST; all methods perform poorly on Alcator C-Mod. survTLS does not consistently beat DSM. Selected horizons and encoder-ablation results vary across devices.

Conclusion: Directly learning horizon-level event probability (TLS) is more effective than modeling detailed within-horizon event-time distributions in this setting; device-specific disruption characteristics drive differing optimal horizons and architectures.

Abstract: Reliable disruption prediction is essential for the safe operation of future tokamaks. Existing full-distribution survival methods model the complete residual time-to-disruption distribution, whereas operational decisions primarily depend on disruption risk within a finite prediction horizon. This mismatch motivates introducing Early Event Prediction (EEP) objectives into survival-based disruption prediction. We take Deep Survival Machines (DSM) as the full-distribution baseline and propose applying two established EEP methods to tokamak disruption prediction: Temporal Label Smoothing (TLS), which directly predicts disruption probability within a finite horizon, and survTLS, which additionally models the event-time distribution within that horizon. Using a common causal encoder, we compare these methods on DIII-D, Alcator C-Mod, and EAST. We distinguish threshold-free deadline ranking from validation-selected fixed-policy alarm performance and evaluate prediction horizons and encoder architectures. TLS achieves the best mean alarm performance on DIII-D and EAST, whereas all methods perform poorly on Alcator C-Mod. survTLS does not consistently outperform DSM, suggesting that directly learning horizon-level event probability is more effective than modeling detailed within-horizon event-time distributions in the present setting. Finally, the selected prediction horizons and encoder-ablation results vary across devices, reflecting differences in disruption characteristics.

</details>


<div id='cs.MS'></div>

# cs.MS [[Back]](#toc)

### [134] [AWE: Adaptive Weight Encoding for Exact Integer Matrix Products with Fewer GEMMs on FP4 Tensor Cores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24519)
*Shun-ichiro Hayashi,Daichi Mukunoki,Tetsuya Hoshino,Takahiro Katagiri*

Main category: cs.MS

TL;DR: This paper introduces Adaptive Weight Encoding (AWE) to reduce the number of FP4 Tensor Core GEMMs needed to emulate high-accuracy integer matrix multiplication, achieving INT8×INT8 in 6 products (vs. 9) and INT4×INT8 in 4.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FP4 Tensor Cores are fastest on NVIDIA B200/B300 but cannot hold INT8 operands, so Ozaki-style emulation splits inputs into FP4-storable base-13 digits. Prior work needs 9 FP4 GEMMs for INT8×INT8, and classical methods (Karatsuba, Toom–Cook) fail because limb sums reach ±24, leaving the FP4 storable set S. Reducing the number of FP4 GEMMs is thus the key efficiency question.

Method: AWE lets limbs use freely chosen integer weights, stores planes as linear combinations of limbs, and reconstructs the exact product by scaling and summing the FP4 GEMMs. For each input range, the authors search over these encoding choices to find configurations with fewer products. The formulation also works modulo m, covering the residue number system of Ozaki scheme II.

Result: Found INT8×INT8 in 6 products and INT4×INT8 in 4 products. For the FP64 significand (Ozaki scheme II), 75 products are reduced to 59. The boundary between encodings with and without residues lies near input width 15. The authors release the encodings found.

Conclusion: Adaptive Weight Encoding provides a general, search-based framework that lowers the FP4 GEMM count for high-accuracy matrix multiplication emulation, with published encodings and a modular variant extending applicability.

Abstract: Emulation of high-accuracy floating-point matrix multiplication, as in the Ozaki scheme, splits the inputs into low-precision components and multiplies them pairwise. These products must be error-free, and each is an integer matrix product times a scale factor. FP4 Tensor Cores are the fastest on the NVIDIA B200 and B300 but cannot hold INT8 operands. The FP4 values scaled by 2 form the set $S = \{0, \pm1, \pm2, \pm3, \pm4, \pm6, \pm8, \pm12\}$, which contains every residue modulo 13, so with carries any integer splits into base-13 digits that FP4 can store. Prior work splits each INT8 operand into 3 such digits (limbs) with weights $(1, 13, 169)$ and multiplies them pairwise, 9 FP4 matrix multiplications (GEMMs) for INT8$\times$INT8. The classical ways to reduce products, such as the Karatsuba and Toom--Cook methods, do not apply as they stand: sums of limbs reach $\pm 24$ and leave $S$. This paper asks how many FP4 GEMMs are needed for one integer matrix product. We propose Adaptive Weight Encoding (AWE): the limbs take freely chosen integer weights, the stored planes are linear combinations of limbs, and the exact product is the sum of the FP4 GEMMs scaled by reconstruction coefficients. For each input range, we searched these choices for encodings with fewer products and found INT8$\times$INT8 in 6 products and INT4$\times$INT8 in 4. The formulation also holds modulo $m$, which covers the residue number systems of Ozaki scheme II: for the FP64 significand, the 75 products of prior work are reduced to 59. The boundary in product count between encodings with and without residues lies near input width 15. We release the encodings found.

</details>


<div id='physics.optics'></div>

# physics.optics [[Back]](#toc)

### [135] [Nonlinear susceptibility for stimulated light scattering in plasmas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23912)
*Aleksei Zheltikov*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper proposes extending the concept of nonlinear-optical susceptibility to strongly coupled laser-plasma interactions, enabling a unified description of stimulated light scattering across optics and plasma physics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strong-field laser-plasma interactions lack a self-consistent framework that connects to established nonlinear-optics concepts, limiting insight into processes like stimulated-scattering-induced cross-beam energy transfer.

Method: A self-consistent treatment of strong-field laser-plasma interactions that heuristically generalizes nonlinear-optical susceptibility to perturbative regimes of stimulated light scattering in plasmas.

Result: The extended susceptibility yields powerful insights into stimulated-scattering-induced cross-beam energy transfer processes.

Conclusion: The findings provide grounds for a unified treatment of stimulated light scattering spanning fiber optics, neutral gases, and laser-plasma physics.

Abstract: A self-consistent treatment of strong-field laser - plasma interactions suggests a heuristically valuable extension of the notion of nonlinear-optical susceptibility to perturbative regimes of stimulated light scattering in plasmas, offering powerful insights into stimulated-scattering-induced cross-beam energy transfer processes. These findings lay grounds for a unified treatment of stimulated light scattering across a vast area of optical physics, spanning from fiber optics and neutral gases to laser - plasma physics.

</details>


<div id='physics.ins-det'></div>

# physics.ins-det [[Back]](#toc)

### [136] [Real-Time SiPM Pulse Deconvolution for a High-granularity Dual-readout Calorimeter with Neutral Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22269)
*Spencer Allen,Matteo Cremonesi,Jordan Damgov,Erdem Yigit Ertorer,Janna Goodman,Giuseppe Di Guglielmo,James Hirschauer,Shuichi Kunori,Peter Meiring*

Main category: physics.ins-det

TL;DR: This paper presents quantized 1D CNN models that localize pulse positions in HG-DREAM calorimeter waveforms and evaluates their FPGA/ASIC implementations for real-time readout.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The HG-DREAM calorimeter for FCC-ee requires longitudinal segmentation via timing to distinguish multiple energy deposits along optical fibers, needing fast, low-power electronics that can predict pulse locations from digitized waveforms.

Method: Three 1D CNN architectures (a compact baseline plus dilation/stride variants reaching a ~20-sample receptive field matching SiPM pulse width) predict pulse locations from 80-sample, 16 ns, 200 ps-digitized waveforms; fixed-point quantization to <16,8> baseline was applied, with FPGA (Xilinx XCVU13P, synthesized at 400 MHz) and TSMC 28 nm CMOS ASIC implementations.

Result: ROC AUC above 0.95 achieved with 4-8 filters per layer; quantization costs <10^-3 in AUC vs. floating-point. Smallest model separates two pulses with >95% probability at longitudinal separations of ≥20 cm. ASIC uses ~18500 μm^2 and sustains 4.8 MHz (exceeding the 0.5 MHz Z-pole requirement by 10x); FPGA uses 9663 LUT but fails the throughput requirement.

Conclusion: ASIC implementation meets FCC-ee triggered readout throughput requirements, but sustaining the 40 MHz triggerless Z-pole bunch-crossing frequency is not feasible with these models and targeted hardware.

Abstract: The High-Granularity Dual-Readout Calorimeter (HG-DREAM) designed for FCC-ee aims to achieve unprecedented energy resolution through fine three-dimensional shower imaging, simultaneous measurement of Cherenkov and scintillation signals, and machine learning capabilities. A key enabling technology is longitudinal segmentation via timing measurements, where multiple energy deposits along optical fibers are distinguished by the arrival times of Cherenkov photons. We present quantized one-dimensional convolutional neural networks that predict pulse locations directly from 80-sample, 16 ns waveforms digitized at 200 ps, and evaluate their implementation in front-end electronics. Three architectures, a compact baseline and two variants using dilation and stride to reach the $\sim$ 20-sample receptive field set by the SiPM pulse width, achieve ROC AUC above 0.95 with four to eight filters per layer. Fixed-point quantization to a $<$16,8$>$ baseline costs less than $10^{-3}$ in AUC relative to the floating-point reference. Applied to pairwise deposits, the smallest model separates two pulses with better than 95\% probability at longitudinal separations of 20 cm and above. Synthesized at 400 MHz, this model uses 9663 LUT on a Xilinx XCVU13P FPGA and occupies $\sim$ 18500 $μ$$m^2$ in TSMC 28 nm CMOS with reconfigurable weights. Assuming a triggered readout architecture for FCC-ee, the $\sim$ 500 kHz Z-pole trigger rate will be the most stringent throughput requirement across all FCC-ee operating modes. The ASIC implementation of the model sustains 4.8 MHz, exceeding the 0.5 MHz requirement by an order of magnitude, while the reconfigurable-weight FPGA implementation fails to do so. Sustaining the 40 MHz FCC-ee Z-pole bunch-crossing frequency in the triggerless architecture is not feasible with the models developed and the hardware targeted in this work.

</details>


<div id='quant-ph'></div>

# quant-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [137] [Resource and entanglement study of a hybrid qudit-qubit quantum algorithm for solving the integer programming problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23243)
*Kapil Goswami,Rick Mukherjee,Peter Schmelcher*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work analyzes a hybrid qudit-qubit integer programming algorithm, showing qudits reduce fault-tolerant resource requirements and that its entanglement structure hinders classical simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A hybrid qudit-qubit algorithm was recently proposed with polynomial quantum advantage for integer programming. The authors aim to understand the role of qudits and assess the algorithm's classical simulability.

Method: Comparative resource analysis (logical gate counts and fault-tolerant physical resources including non-Clifford gates) between the hybrid qudit-qubit and qubit-only implementations, plus an entanglement study examining entropy growth and multipartite entanglement.

Result: For qutrit (d=3) and ququint (d=5) examples versus qubit-only versions, total resource counts drop by ~180x and ~2220x respectively. Entanglement analysis reveals volume-law-like entropy growth, multipartite entanglement signatures, and synergistic tri-partite entanglement even for quadratic problems.

Conclusion: Qudits yield a more compact unitary structure with significant resource savings. The algorithm generates higher-order entanglement independent of the problem, making classical simulation increasingly difficult.

Abstract: Recently, a hybrid qudit-qubit algorithm [1] was presented for solving the integer programming problem with a polynomial quantum advantage. In this work, we investigate the algorithm [1] to understand the role of qudits ($d$-dimensional quantum system) by conducting a comparative resource analysis with its qubit-only implementation and the classical simulability of the algorithm by exploring the entanglement structure. The resource analysis part is performed in terms of logical gate counts, and fault-tolerant physical resources, including non-Clifford gates. The hybrid qudit-qubit implementation has a more compact structure of the unitary operators as opposed to its qubit-only simulation which reduces the logical and fault-tolerant resource requirements. Two example problems, one with qutrits ($d=3$) and the other with ququint ($d=5$), when contrasted with their qubit-only implementation, within a simplified fault-tolerant resource model, showed $\sim 180 \times $ and $\sim 2220\times$ fewer total resource count, respectively. For the entanglement study, volume-law-like entropy growth and signatures of multi-partite entanglement is observed leading to increasing difficulty in the classical simulation of the algorithm. The algorithm generates synergistic tri-partite entanglement even for a quadratic problem, revealing that the algorithmic structure itself can generate higher-order entanglement in the system independent of the problem.

</details>


<div id='astro-ph.SR'></div>
