### [138] [Oblique kink waves in solar coronal streamers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24495)
*Aleksandr V. Rubtsov,Valery M. Nakariakov,Dmitri Yu. Klimushkin,Ding Yuan*

Main category: astro-ph.SR

TL;DR: Coronal streamer kink waves excited by CME impacts are analyzed for oblique propagation along a plasma slab with a central current sheet, showing that obliquity reduces phase speed, shifts body to surface modes, and can bias Alfvén speed estimates by up to 1.5×.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Streamer kink waves are routinely used to estimate coronal Alfvén speed, but existing estimates assume radial, field-aligned propagation in the plane of the sky. CME impacts naturally produce oblique propagation, which may bias these seismological estimates.

Method: The authors model a plasma slab with a central current sheet representing a coronal streamer stalk. They examine cold and warm streamer plasmas and study how propagation obliquity affects wave dispersion, eigenfunctions, and seismological diagnostics.

Result: Increasing obliquity reduces phase speed via projection effects and intrinsic angle dependence. Waves transition from body to surface modes, producing stronger boundary perturbations and enhanced localization, yielding larger observable amplitudes for a given CME energy. Applying the theory to prior streamer waves gives Alfvén speeds of 50–500 km/s, and neglecting obliquity underestimates Alfvén speed by up to a factor of 1.5.

Conclusion: Wave propagation obliquity must be included in coronal seismology of streamer waves, and stereoscopic observations are needed to constrain the true propagation direction.

Abstract: Coronal streamer kink waves excited by an impact of coronal mass ejections (CMEs) are routinely used to estimate the coronal Alfvén speed. These estimates generally assume wave propagation parallel to the equilibrium magnetic field directed radially outward to the Sun, in the plane of the sky. However, the geometry of CME impacts naturally allows for oblique propagation. We investigate the properties of obliquely propagating kink waves guided by a plasma slab with a central current sheet representing a coronal streamer stalk. We consider both cold and warm streamer plasmas and analyse influence of propagation obliquity on wave dispersion, eigenfunctions, and seismological diagnostics. We find that increasing the propagation obliquity reduces the phase speed owing to both projection effects and the intrinsic dependence of the phase speed on the propagation angle. The wave structure also undergoes a gradual transition from a body mode to a surface mode, leading to stronger perturbations at the slab boundaries and enhanced wave localization. Consequently, for a given energy deposited by a CME impact, oblique kink waves are expected to produce larger observable amplitudes than field-aligned waves. Applying the developed theory to streamer waves reported previously, we infer Alfvén speeds in the range of 50-500 km/s. We show that neglecting wave propagation obliquity may underestimate the Alfvén speed by up to a factor of 1.5. These results demonstrate that wave propagation obliquity should be taken into account in coronal seismology of streamer waves and that stereoscopic observations will be required to constrain the true propagation direction.

</details>


<div id='physics.chem-ph'></div>

# physics.chem-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [139] [Benchmarking higher-ranking multipoles and polarizability tensors for small molecular systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24606)
*Bruno V. von Bruening,Alston J. Misquitta*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This work benchmarks CCSD(T) reference molecular dipole/quadrupole moments and dipole-dipole/quadrupole-quadrupole polarizabilities for 73 small molecules, evaluating HF, MP2, CCSD, and DFT methods plus basis-set requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate molecular properties are essential for building intermolecular interaction models, and high accuracy requires going beyond leading-order dipolar terms to include quadrupolar properties.

Method: CCSD(T) reference values were computed for 73 small non-spin-polarized molecules for four properties (dipole and quadrupole moments, dipole-dipole and quadrupole-quadrupole polarizabilities). A holistic performance analysis compared HF, MP2, CCSD, and many density functionals, along with a study of required basis-set levels.

Result: Asymptotically corrected hybrid GGAs B97-3-AC and PBE0-AC perform best across all four properties. Modern meta-GGAs and range-separated functionals perform inconsistently with large errors in quadrupolar properties. The Jensen aug-pcseg-2 basis set is computationally efficient for DFT; correlated references benefit from core polarization for third-row elements, and double augmentation is strictly necessary for quadrupole-quadrupole polarizabilities.

Conclusion: The findings have direct implications for intermolecular interaction methods (e.g., DFT-based SAPT) and for the quality of properties and interactions in machine-learning datasets.

Abstract: Molecular properties govern how molecules interact with one another or external fields. Accurate molecular properties are essential for constructing intermolecular interaction models, and to achieve high accuracy we need to go beyond leading-order (dipolar) terms.
  This work presents CCSD(T) references for 73 small non-spin-polarized molecules for molecular dipole and quadrupole moments, and dipole-dipole and quadrupole-quadrupole polarizabilities. Using these results, we provide a holistic performance analysis of molecular properties computed with HF, MP2, CCSD, and a wide range of density-functional methods. Additionally, we investigate in detail what levels of basis sets are required to simultaneously describe all properties in the data set.
  The best-performing density functionals for all four properties are the asymptotically cor- rected hybrid GGAs B97-3-AC, closely followed by PBE0-AC. Surprisingly, modern meta- GGAs and range-separated functionals perform inconsistently, with large errors in the quadrupo- lar properties. These results have direct implications for methods for intermolecular interac- tions, such as symmetry-adapted perturbation theory based on density functional theory, or for the quality of properties and interactions in machine-learning datasets.
  Finally, for DFT methods, the Jensen aug-pcseg-2 basis set is a computationally efficient alternative to more established basis sets, but for generating correlated references, third-row elements benefit from core polarization, and double augmentation is strictly necessary for quadrupole-quadrupole polarizabilities.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [140] [MGRD: Compact morphology-gated residual diffusion for variance-aware cross-domain neurite forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23990)
*Tsung Yeh Hsieh,Cosmin Anitescu,Chunghwan Kim,Victoria A. Webster-Wood,Yongjie Jessica Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MGRD is a compact stochastic diffusion surrogate that forecasts 20 future neurite-morphology frames from 10 observed frames, outperforming gSTA while using ~1% of its parameters and providing case-level variance scores for forecast-difficulty ranking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term time-lapse imaging of neurite morphology is resource-intensive and hard to scale, so forecasting future morphology could reduce this burden. However, existing digital-twin models like gSTA produce a single deterministic forecast and cannot represent variability among plausible futures.

Method: Morphology-Gated Residual Diffusion (MGRD): a compact stochastic surrogate that jointly forecasts 20 future neurite-morphology frames from 10 observed frames, conditioning on morphology features derived from the latest observation. It supports repeated sampling to yield a case-level variance score for ranking forecast difficulty, and uses cached morphology features for faster DDIM generation.

Result: On phase-field trajectories, MGRD cuts trajectory-wise mean MAE by 9.7% vs. a matched control while updating 4.46x fewer parameters. On human iPSC-derived neuron microscopy, it improves all four metrics over gSTA (39.6% lower mean MAE; 45.3% higher skeleton F1). Without mouse-domain retraining, it improves MAE and skeleton F1 on mouse cortical-neurosphere microscopy across 10-40-min intervals and horizons beyond 13 hours. Retaining ~60% of lowest-variance cases reduces mean MAE by 17.6% (iPSC) and 16.8% (simulation). It uses 1.01% of gSTA's parameters, <1/10 training-update time, and generates a 50-step DDIM trajectory 7.9% faster with cached features.

Conclusion: MGRD is established as a compact stochastic surrogate for neurite-morphology forecasting and case prioritization across simulation and microscopy datasets, offering strong accuracy, efficiency, and generalization benefits.

Abstract: Tracking neurite morphology over time helps characterize structural changes during neuronal development and deterioration, but long-term time-lapse imaging is resource-intensive and difficult to scale. Forecasting future morphology could reduce this burden. Existing neurite digital-twin models such as gated spatiotemporal attention (gSTA) produce a single deterministic forecast without representing variability among plausible futures. We introduce Morphology-Gated Residual Diffusion (MGRD), a compact stochastic surrogate that jointly forecasts twenty future neurite-morphology frames from ten observed frames while conditioning on morphology features derived from the latest observation. On controlled phase-field trajectories, MGRD reduces trajectory-wise mean MAE by 9.7% relative to a matched control while updating 4.46 times fewer parameters. On human iPSC-derived neuron microscopy, MGRD improves all four reported metrics over gSTA, including a 39.6% reduction in trajectory-wise mean MAE and a 45.3% increase in skeleton F1. Without mouse-domain retraining or fine-tuning, MGRD also improves MAE and skeleton F1 on mouse cortical-neurosphere microscopy across 10-40-min sampling intervals and forecast horizons beyond 13 hours. Repeated sampling provides a case-level variance score for ranking forecast difficulty. Retaining approximately 60% of the lowest-variance cases reduces mean MAE by 17.6% on iPSC microscopy and 16.8% on simulation data. MGRD uses 1.01% of gSTA's parameters, requires less than one tenth of its training-update time, and generates a 50-step DDIM trajectory 7.9% faster when morphology features are cached. These results establish MGRD as a compact stochastic surrogate for neurite-morphology forecasting and case prioritization across simulation and microscopy datasets.

</details>


<div id='math.SP'></div>

# math.SP [[Back]](#toc)

### [141] [Dirichlet--Neumann bracketing for nonlocal operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24892)
*Mateusz Kwaśnicki,Jacek Wszoła*

Main category: math.SP

TL;DR: Dirichlet–Neumann bracketing is established for Dirichlet eigenvalues of ψ(−Δ) on bounded Lipschitz domains for any complete Bernstein function ψ, yielding simplicity of interval eigenvalues and antisymmetry of second eigenfunctions in balls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Eigenvalue bracketing between Neumann and Dirichlet Laplacian eigenvalues was known for the classical Laplacian and fractional powers; the paper aims to unify and extend such spectral comparisons to a general class of nonlocal operators generated by complete Bernstein functions.

Method: The proof uses quadratic forms, operator monotonicity, and an analysis of equality in resolvent comparisons. Bracketing is then applied to intervals and balls.

Result: Dirichlet eigenvalues of ψ(−Δ) lie between ψ applied to Neumann and Dirichlet Laplacian eigenvalues; both inequalities are strict unless ψ has a meromorphic continuation to C\{0}. This gives simplicity of interval eigenvalues and antisymmetry of second ball eigenfunctions, recovering known fractional-power results and extending them to broader nonlocal operators.

Conclusion: A unified spectral bracketing framework extends known fractional Laplacian eigenvalue results to all complete Bernstein functions, covering a broader class of nonlocal operators.

Abstract: We establish Dirichlet--Neumann bracketing for the Dirichlet eigenvalues of $ψ(-Δ)$ on bounded Lipschitz domains, where $ψ$ is an arbitrary complete Bernstein function. The eigenvalues lie between $ψ$ applied to the corresponding Neumann and Dirichlet eigenvalues of the Laplacian. Both inequalities are strict whenever $ψ$ admits no meromorphic continuation to $\mathbb C \setminus \{0\}$. The proof uses quadratic forms, operator monotonicity, and an analysis of equality in resolvent comparisons. Applying the bracketing to intervals and balls gives a unified proof of simplicity of interval eigenvalues and antisymmetry of second eigenfunctions in balls under the same condition on $ψ$. For fractional powers, these recover results of Fall, Ghimenti, Micheletti and Pistoia for the interval, and of Fall, Feulefack, Temgoua and Weth and, independently, Benedikt, Bobkov, Dhara and Girg for the ball. The argument extends these conclusions to a broader class of nonlocal operators.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.dis-nn'></div>

# cond-mat.dis-nn [[Back]](#toc)

### [142] [Gradient-estimator design overcomes trainability barriers in neural-network-based variational optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22342)
*Yi-Ran Xue,Rui Wang,Baigeng Wang,Chenan Wei*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper introduces the adaptive minimum-variance phase (AMVP) estimator, an unbiased direct gradient estimator that boosts signal-to-noise ratio in weak-gradient regimes, enabling reliable and cheap neural-network variational optimization for quantum many-body problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Even sufficiently expressive neural networks fail to train in weak-gradient regimes, limiting their practical use in quantum many-body physics and ab initio quantum chemistry.

Method: Derive an unbiased direct gradient estimator and introduce the adaptive minimum-variance phase (AMVP) estimator to improve the signal-to-noise ratio of weak gradients.

Result: Compact networks outperform larger, fine-tuned standard-estimator models with over an order of magnitude less GPU time on correlated flux models, exceed DMRG accuracy, and reach chemical accuracy in N2 bond breaking and, for the first time, heavy-element I2 with explicit spin-orbit coupling.

Conclusion: Gradient-estimator design expands the capabilities of neural-network variational methods for accurate scientific computing.

Abstract: Neural networks provide expressive representations for scientific computing. However, even sufficiently expressive networks can suffer training failure in weak-gradient regimes, limiting their practical use in quantum many-body physics and ab initio quantum chemistry. Here we derive an unbiased direct gradient estimator and introduce the adaptive minimum-variance phase (AMVP) estimator for neural-network variational optimization. By improving the signal-to-noise ratio of weak gradients, these methods enable reliable scientific calculations where training previously failed, while substantially reducing computational cost. The framework enables compact networks to outperform larger and fine-tuned default standard-estimator models with over an order of magnitude less GPU time on correlated flux models, and ultimately exceed the density matrix renormalization group (DMRG) accuracy. It further achieves chemical accuracy in N$_2$ bond breaking and, for the first time, in heavy-element I$_2$ with explicit spin-orbit coupling. These results demonstrate that gradient-estimator design expands the capabilities of neural-network variational methods for accurate scientific computing.

</details>


<div id='astro-ph.HE'></div>

# astro-ph.HE [[Back]](#toc)

### [143] [A Linear Instability and Damping in the Acoustic Dispersion Relation of Fluids Subject to Inverse Compton Drag](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23824)
*Yiting Wang,Sebastian Heinz,Vladimir Zhdankin*

Main category: astro-ph.HE

TL;DR: Radiation drag from inverse-Compton scattering alters the acoustic dispersion relation of relativistic fluids, producing damping, instability, and dispersion of sound waves, with applications to relativistic astrophysical outflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: External radiation fields scatter off relativistic fluid electrons, and the resulting radiation drag is known to affect background flows. The paper investigates how this drag modifies the propagation of acoustic perturbations in such fluids.

Method: Derive the linear acoustic dispersion relation for a relativistic fluid including inverse-Compton radiation drag. Validate the derived growth/damping and dispersion rates with special-relativistic hydrodynamic simulations.

Result: Radiation drag either damps both acoustic modes or damps one while driving the other unstable. Instability is robust at short wavelengths but slow/overdamped at long wavelengths. A purely damped region exists for a wide parameter range. Waves become dispersive: phase speed deviates from sound speed at long wavelengths, approaching it at short wavelengths. Rates are of order the bulk radiative acceleration rate.

Conclusion: Radiation drag affects fluid perturbations whenever it matters for the background flow. The instability, damping, and dispersion are relevant to gamma-ray bursts, blazars in broad-line regions, and quasar jets through the CMB and galactic radiation fields.

Abstract: Radiation drag from inverse-Compton scattering of an external radiation field changes the acoustic dispersion relation of a relativistic fluid. We derive the linear dispersion relation and show that, depending on the electron distribution and fluid sound speed, radiation drag either damps both modes or damps one while driving the other unstable. The unstable growth is robust at short wavelengths but slow and overdamped at long wavelengths, where the linear analysis is limited by the background acceleration. The resulting growth and damping rates are of order the bulk radiative acceleration rate, so whenever radiation drag is important for the background flow, its effects on fluid perturbations are also important. A purely damped region exists for a wide range of parameters.
  Radiation drag also makes acoustic waves dispersive: The phase speed deviates from the ordinary sound speed at longer wavelengths, and approaches the sound speed at short wavelengths. We validate the derived rates using special-relativistic hydrodynamic simulations. We apply the derived relation to astrophysical jets such as gamma-ray bursts, blazars in the broad-line region of the host active galactic nucleus, and quasar jets traveling through the CMB and galactic radiation field. We find that the derived instability, damping and dispersion may be relevant across a range of relativistic outflow conditions.

</details>


### [144] [Particle Acceleration in Stratified 2D MRI Turbulence: From Reconnection to First- and Second-Order Fermi and Shear Acceleration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24735)
*Astor Sandoval,Mario Riquelme,Jorge Cuadra*

Main category: astro-ph.HE

TL;DR: This paper identifies and quantifies the particle acceleration mechanisms in 2D PIC simulations of the stratified MRI in pair plasmas, explaining the maximum-energy scaling with the scale-separation ratio ω_c,0/Ω_0 found by Sandoval et al. (2024).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-luminosity accretion disks around black holes are weakly collisional, potentially allowing nonthermal particle acceleration. Previous 2D PIC simulations (S24) showed nonthermal populations scaling with ω_c,0/Ω_0, but the underlying acceleration mechanisms and their scale-separation dependence were unclear.

Method: Analyze 2D PIC simulations of the stratified magnetorotational instability (MRI) in pair plasmas, identifying acceleration mechanisms and quantifying their scale-separation dependence. Three stages are distinguished: injection by non-ideal electric fields during plasmoid-driven reconnection, first-order Fermi-like acceleration in converging flows between separating plasmoids, and repeated plasmoid interactions with second-order Fermi and shear acceleration.

Result: Particles are injected with energies independent of scale separation. Then they undergo rapid first-order Fermi-like acceleration with rate proportional to the instantaneous non-relativistic cyclotron frequency (independent of ω_c,0/Ω_0), implying maximum energy ∝ ω_c,0/Ω_0. A slower second-order Fermi stage with subdominant shear acceleration increases energies only by a factor of a few over several orbits, regardless of ω_c,0/Ω_0.

Conclusion: The combination of the three stages yields maximum energy proportional to ω_c,0/Ω_0, explaining the S24 scaling and suggesting the MRI may accelerate particles to ultrarelativistic energies in low-luminosity disks. Testing in 3D is an important next step.

Abstract: Low-luminosity accretion disks around black holes are weakly collisional, potentially enabling nonthermal particle acceleration. Local two-dimensional particle-in-cell simulations of the stratified magnetorotational instability (MRI) in pair plasmas by Sandoval et al. (2024; hereafter S24) revealed nonthermal populations with maximum energies proportional to the scale-separation ratio $ω_{c,0}/Ω_0$, where $ω_{c,0}$ and $Ω_0$ are the initial particle cyclotron and Keplerian frequencies, respectively. We identify the acceleration mechanisms involved and quantify their scale-separation dependence. Acceleration occurs primarily within large-scale current sheets associated with an MRI dynamo. Particles are initially injected by non-ideal electric fields during reconnection driven by plasmoid splitting, attaining energies independently of scale separation. They subsequently undergo rapid first-order Fermi-like acceleration occurring within converging flows between newly formed, separating plasmoids. During this stage, energies grow at a rate proportional to the instantaneous non-relativistic cyclotron frequency, independently of $ω_{c,0}/Ω_0$. This fast acceleration rate implies a maximum energy proportional to $ω_{c,0}/Ω_0$. Particles then continue interacting repeatedly with plasmoids, undergoing slower, predominantly second-order Fermi acceleration, with a subdominant contribution from shear acceleration. This stage increases particle energies by only a factor of {\it a few} over several orbits, regardless of $ω_{c,0}/Ω_0$. The combination of these three stages implies a maximum energy proportional to $ω_{c,0}/Ω_0$, explaining the scaling found by S24 and suggesting that the MRI may accelerate particles to ultrarelativistic energies in low-luminosity disks. Testing these results in three dimensions remains an important next step.

</details>


<div id='math.MG'></div>

# math.MG [[Back]](#toc)

### [145] [The $L_p$ Minkowski problem for $C$-close sets: existence and continuity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23962)
*Wen Ai,Deping Ye,Baocheng Zhu*

Main category: math.MG

TL;DR: This paper solves the $L_p$ Minkowski problem for $C$-close sets in the range $p\in(0,1)$ for arbitrary nonzero finite Borel measures on $\Omega_{C^\circ}$, and establishes continuity of solutions for $p\in[0,1]$.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The $L_p$ Minkowski problem is a central problem in convex geometric analysis. Extending it to $C$-close sets and resolving the existence question for the previously open range $p\in(0,1)$ with general measures, as well as understanding how solutions depend on the data, motivates this work.

Method: The authors study the $L_p$ surface area measure of $C$-close sets for a pointed closed convex cone $C$ with nonempty interior, and solve the associated Minkowski problem via variational/measure-theoretic techniques for $p\in(0,1)$.

Result: Existence of a $C$-close set $\mathds{A}$ realizing any given nonzero finite Borel measure $\mu$ on $\Omega_{C^\circ}$ as its $L_p$ surface area measure is proven for $p\in(0,1)$; additionally, continuity of solutions in $p$ over $[0,1]$ is established in several settings.

Conclusion: The $L_p$ Minkowski problem for $C$-close sets is fully solved for $p\in(0,1)$ with general measures, and the solution map exhibits continuity for $p\in[0,1]$ under various conditions.

Abstract: Let $C$ be a pointed closed convex cone in $\mathbb{R}^n$ with nonempty interior, and let $S^{n-1}$ denote the unit sphere in $\mathbb{R}^n$. The $L_p$ Minkowski problem for $C$-close sets is to determine, for a real number $p$ and a nonzero finite Borel measure $μ$ defined on $Ω_{C^\circ}=S^{n-1}\cap \mathrm{int} C^{\circ}$, whether there exists a $C$-close set $\mathds{A}$ such that $μ$ is the $L_p$ surface area measure of $\mathds{A}$. In this paper, we will solve the problem for $p\in (0,1)$ and for $μ$ being a nonzero finite Borel measure on $Ω_{C^\circ}$. Moreover, we establish the continuity of solutions to the $L_p$ Minkowski problem for $p\in [0, 1]$ in several settings.

</details>

# math.OC [[Back]](#toc)

### [146] [Numerical approximation of distributed optimal control problems governed by linear thermo-poroelasticity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24448)
*Jeonghun J. Lee*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: The paper proposes and analyzes a three-field symmetric formulation for optimal control of linear thermo-poroelasticity, proving well-posedness, optimal control existence, optimality conditions, and deriving a priori error estimates for a fully discrete scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optimal control governed by linear thermo-poroelasticity, with solid displacement, fluid pressure, and temperature as state variables and both fluid and heat sources as controls, requires a rigorous and symmetric mathematical formulation and a reliable fully discrete numerical scheme with provable convergence, which is what this work aims to provide.

Method: The authors introduce a three-field symmetric formulation for the state (displacement, pressure, temperature) and controls (fluid and heat sources). They prove well-posedness of the symmetric differentiated formulation, existence and uniqueness of an optimal control pair, and derive first-order necessary optimality conditions via a coupled three-field adjoint system. For the fully discrete scheme using dG(0) time discretization, they apply variational discretization for the controls and derive a priori error estimates.

Result: Well-posedness of the symmetric differentiated formulation is established, existence and uniqueness of the optimal control pair are proved, first-order necessary optimality conditions are derived, and a priori error estimates of order O(h^s + Δt) are obtained for the state, adjoint, and control errors. Manufactured-solution experiments validate the fully discrete optimality system and illustrate the storage-degenerate regime.

Conclusion: The paper delivers a rigorous theoretical and numerical framework for optimal control of linear thermo-poroelasticity, providing well-posedness, optimality conditions, and optimal convergence rates, validated by numerical experiments.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose and analyze a novel three-field symmetric formulation for optimal control problems governed by linear thermo-poroelasticity. The state variables are the solid displacement $\boldsymbol{u}$, the fluid pressure $p$, and the temperature $θ$, and both the distributed fluid source $m_p$ and the distributed heat source $m_θ$ serve as control variables. We prove well-posedness of the symmetric differentiated formulation, establish the existence and uniqueness of an optimal control pair $(\bar{m}_p,\bar{m}_θ)$, and derive the first-order necessary optimality conditions via a coupled three-field adjoint system. For the fully discrete scheme based on dG(0) time discretization, we employ a variational discretization approach for both controls and derive a priori error estimates of order $\mathcal{O}(h^s + Δt)$ for the state, adjoint, and control errors. We also present manufactured-solution experiments that validate the fully discrete optimality system and illustrate the storage-degenerate regime covered by our analysis.

</details>


### [147] [Parallel Optimization: Theory, Algorithms, and Applications,](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24505)
*Yair Censor,Stavros A. Zenios*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: A 30-year-old book is offered as a free download courtesy of Oxford University Press for future generations of young scientists; a printed copy can be purchased from the publisher.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Oxford University Press granted permission to make this classic book freely accessible, aiming to benefit future generations of young scientists.

Method: Not applicable; this is an announcement rather than a research paper.

Result: The book won the 1999 INFORMS Computing Society Prize for Research Excellence at the interface between Operations Research and Computer Science, and was reviewed by Janos D. Pinter in the Journal of Global Optimization (16:107-108, 2000).

Conclusion: The book is freely available online via OUP's generosity, while a print version remains purchasable.

Abstract: This 30 years old book is posted for free download by the generosity of Oxford University Press that granted permission to allow access to future generations of young scientists. A printed copy can be purchased from the publisher. The authors received for this book "The 1999 INFORMS Computing Society Prize for Research Excellence in the Interface Between Operations Research and Computer Science" from the Institute for Operations Research and the Management Sciences (INFORMS). For a book review by Janos D. Pinter go to: Journal of Global Optimization, 16: 107-108, 2000.

</details>


<div id='math.PR'></div>

# math.PR [[Back]](#toc)

### [148] [Large Deviations for the Two-Dimensional Dean-Kawasaki Equation with Coulomb Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23730)
*Xiaohao Ji*

Main category: math.PR

TL;DR: The authors prove global well-posedness and a small-noise large deviation principle for the 2D Dean-Kawasaki equation with Coulomb interaction, with exact square-root noise coefficient, under a mass-interaction condition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Dean-Kawasaki equation with square-root noise is singular and its rigorous treatment (well-posedness and large deviations) in 2D with Coulomb-type interaction remains challenging.

Method: Works with finite-mode noise approximations ξ^K at each Fourier cutoff, establishes global pathwise well-posedness in the class of stochastic kinetic solutions for finite-entropy initial data, and adapts the Fehrman-Gess framework to derive the large deviation principle.

Result: Global pathwise well-posedness holds for every finite Fourier cutoff when λ_V max{cos(α_V),0} M < 8π. A large deviation principle on L^1((0,T)×T^2) is obtained under K(ε)→∞ with εK(ε)^4→0, with good rate function from the quadratic control problem of the skeleton equation.

Conclusion: The 2D Dean-Kawasaki equation with Coulomb interaction and exact square-root coefficient is globally well-posed and admits a small-noise large deviation principle within the stochastic kinetic solutions framework.

Abstract: We establish global well-posedness and a small-noise large deviation principle for the two-dimensional Dean-Kawasaki equation with Coulomb interaction $\partial_tρ^\varepsilon=Δρ^\varepsilon-\nabla\cdot(ρ^\varepsilon(V*ρ^\varepsilon))-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nabla\cdot(\sqrt{ρ^\varepsilon}\circξ^{K(\varepsilon)})$, where $V=λ_V(\cos(α_V)\nabla G+\sin(α_V)J\nabla G)$. Here $λ_V\geq0$ is the interaction strength, $α_V$ is the interaction angle, $G$ is the mean-zero Green function of $-Δ$ on $\mathbb{T}^2$, $J$ is rotation by $π/2$, and $ξ^K$ is a finite-mode approximation of space-time white noise. For finite-entropy initial data of mass $M$ satisfying $λ_V\max\{\cos(α_V),0\}M<8π$, the equation with the exact square-root coefficient is globally pathwise well posed at every finite Fourier cutoff in the class of stochastic kinetic solutions. Following Fehrman and Gess (arXiv:1910.11860), we prove a large deviation principle on $L^1((0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^2)$ under the scaling $K(\varepsilon)\to\infty$ and $\varepsilon K(\varepsilon)^4\to0$, with good rate function given by the quadratic control problem for the skeleton equation.

</details>


### [149] [A transform approach to the supercooled Stefan problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24297)
*Thomas Blore,Ben Hambly*

Main category: math.PR

TL;DR: This paper generalizes the probabilistic reformulation of the supercooled Stefan problem to allow non-integrable initial data, establishing existence, uniqueness, and asymptotic results via an Ito's formula-derived relationship.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The prior probabilistic reformulation (arXiv:1902.05174) of the supercooled Stefan problem assumed integrable initial data, limiting its applicability. This work extends the framework to non-integrable initial data to cover more general settings.

Method: Applying Ito's formula yields a relationship between the Laplace transform of the initial data and a transform of the solution, including its possibly discontinuous free boundary. This relationship is used to derive the main results.

Result: A sharp existence result for global solutions is obtained; previous uniqueness results are extended; and the asymptotic behaviour of solutions is determined for a certain class of initial data.

Conclusion: The generalized probabilistic framework handles non-integrable initial data, providing sharp global existence, extended uniqueness, and asymptotic characterizations of solutions.

Abstract: We consider a generalisation of the probabilistic reformulation of the supercooled Stefan problem from arXiv:1902.05174 allowing for non-integrable initial data. From an application of Ito's formula, we obtain a relationship between the Laplace transform of the initial data and a transform of the solution, together with its possibly discontinuous free boundary. Using this relationship we give a sharp existence result for global solutions, we extend previous results on the uniqueness of solutions, and we determine the asymptotic behaviour of solutions for a certain class of initial data.

</details>


<div id='physics.ao-ph'></div>

# physics.ao-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [150] [Conservation Constraints and Distributed Advective Memory in a Reduced Model of Atlantic Overturning Hysteresis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24000)
*Sandy Hardian Susanto Herho,Iwan Pramesti Anwar,Mutiara Rachmat Putri,Rusmawan Suwarman,Deny Juanda Puradimaja,Dasapta Erwin Irawan*

Main category: physics.ao-ph

TL;DR: A closed five-box thermohaline model with an exact salt invariant shows that Indo-Pacific gateway salt export to the Atlantic, driven solely by net Atlantic freshwater export, acts as purely additive forcing and cannot alter the equilibrium salt-advection feedback; gateways influence transient (oscillatory) rather than equilibrium dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interbasin exchange through the Indo-Pacific gateway supplies salt to the Atlantic and is widely invoked as a control on Atlantic overturning circulation stability, yet it is unclear whether this control can act on the equilibrium structure of a conceptual thermohaline model.

Method: A closed five-box model (North Atlantic, upper-limb, Indian, Pacific, deep reservoirs) with an exact salt invariant, where return flow splits between a warm Indian route and a cold route, plus an Indonesian Throughflow branch and Agulhas retroflection. Steady-state budgets of gateway reservoirs are added, and a discrete transit lag is replaced by a gamma memory kernel to derive a closed-form oscillatory instability threshold.

Result: Every internal exchange cancels, so gateway salt export is fixed by net Atlantic freshwater export alone, independent of warm-route fraction, throughflow, retroflection, and apportionment (max departure ~1e-11). The gateway is purely additive forcing that cannot renormalize the salt-advection feedback. Under gamma memory, equilibria are unchanged but a closed-form oscillatory instability threshold depends only on kernel shape; broad memory is strongly stabilizing and discrete lag is least stable. Oscillatory instability always precedes the saddle-node, with an interval widening >10-fold as memory sharpens.

Conclusion: Gateways act on transient rather than equilibrium dynamics of the Atlantic overturning circulation.

Abstract: Interbasin exchange through the Indo-Pacific gateway supplies salt to the Atlantic and is widely invoked as a control on the stability of the Atlantic overturning circulation. We ask whether that control can act on the equilibrium structure of a conceptual thermohaline model. A closed five-box model with an exact salt invariant is constructed, comprising North Atlantic, upper-limb, Indian, Pacific, and deep reservoirs, with the return flow split between a warm route through the Indian reservoir and a cold route, together with an Indonesian Throughflow branch and an Agulhas retroflection. Adding the steady-state budgets of the gateway reservoirs shows that every internal exchange cancels, so the salt they export to the Atlantic is fixed by the net Atlantic freshwater export alone. This holds independently of the warm-route fraction, the throughflow, the retroflection, and how the export is apportioned among gateway reservoirs; across a parameter sweep the largest departure is of order ten to the minus eleven. The gateway therefore enters as a purely additive forcing and cannot renormalize the salt-advection feedback. Replacing the discrete transit lag by a gamma memory kernel leaves the equilibria unchanged but yields a closed-form threshold for oscillatory instability depending only on kernel shape. Broad memory is strongly stabilizing, and a discrete lag is the least stable member of the family. Because the instantaneous feedback vanishes at the fold, oscillatory instability always precedes the saddle-node, over an interval widening more than tenfold as memory sharpens. Gateways therefore appear to act on transient rather than equilibrium dynamics

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.mes-hall'></div>

# cond-mat.mes-hall [[Back]](#toc)

### [151] [Collective depinning of dipolar skyrmion chains: thermal and athermal rounding of an elastic polymer in a quenched landscape](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23181)
*R. L. Silva,R. C. Silva,R. L. Stamps*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Numerical study of dipole skyrmion chains driven across pinning sites reveals length-independent depinning threshold, partly thermal transition rounding, and collective depinning via internal deformations, relevant for skyrmion-based devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Skyrmion chains, rather than isolated textures, may act as information carriers in spintronic devices, so understanding how interacting skyrmion chains depin in realistic weak-pinning landscapes is needed.

Method: Stochastic Thiele equation solved numerically for dipole skyrmion chains of varying lengths under periodic boundary conditions, driven across quenched attractive pinning sites at different magnetic fields.

Result: For chains of 50+ skyrmions, depinning threshold is length-independent and ~1/3 that of an isolated skyrmion. Transition width grows only partly thermally (0.334 to 0.633 decades, T=0 to 300 K); Larkin length stays ~2.6 bonds below kBT ~ V0/25. Width is nearly constant up to kBT ~ 0.14 V0 then rises ~fourfold by kBT ~ 0.23 V0 via creep-regime broadening, independent of chain length. Field increase (30 to 40 mT) widens the core by 26% and shortens creep tails. For ~200-skyrmion chains, depinning involves sequential internal deformations (Larkin length growth, roughness, transient bond stretching, mobility/Hall angle recovery).

Conclusion: Collective depinning behavior of skyrmion chains is governed by internal deformations rather than finite-size effects, with implications for skyrmion-based devices using chains as information carriers.

Abstract: The dynamics of interacting dipole skyrmion chains in a weak pinning landscape is studied through numerical solutions of the stochastic Thiele equation. Chains of different lengths with periodic boundary conditions are driven across quenched attractive pinning sites under different magnetic fields. A clear threshold spin current is identified as the critical depinning force. Three main results emerge: (i) for chains of 50 skyrmions or more, the depinning threshold is independent of chain length and remains close to one third of that of an isolated skyrmion. (ii) The transition rounding is only partly thermal: the width changes from w = 0.334 +/- 0.004 decades at T = 0 to 0.633 +/- 0.002 at 300 K, while the intrinsic Larkin correlation length remains about 2.6 bonds below kBT ~ V0/25. (iii) The width is nearly unchanged up to kBT ~ 0.14 V0, but increases almost fourfold by kBT ~ 0.23 V0, mainly through broadening of the creep regime. It is independent of chain length, ruling out finite-size rounding. Increasing the field from 30 to 40 mT changes the transition shape, widening its core by 26% while shortening the creep tails. For chains of about 200 skyrmions, depinning proceeds through a sequence of internal deformations - growth of the Larkin length and roughness, transient bond stretching, and subsequent recovery of mobility and Hall angle. These collective effects are relevant for skyrmion-based devices in which chains, rather than isolated textures, act as information carriers.

</details>


### [152] [Interplay of spin-orbit coupling and magnetic chirality: Multidirectional spin polarization in a helical antiferromagnet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23618)
*Kallol Mondal,Sudin Ganguly,Santanu K. Maiti*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This study explores spin-dependent transport in a non-collinear helical antiferromagnet with spin-orbit coupling, showing that multidirectional spin polarization can be generated along all three spatial axes due to symmetry breaking from the helical magnetic order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional spin-filtering systems typically produce spin polarization along a single axis. The authors seek to understand whether non-collinear magnetic textures combined with spin-orbit coupling can generate richer spin polarization, motivating investigation of helical antiferromagnets.

Method: A tight-binding framework is used to model the system, and spin-dependent transport is analyzed via the Landauer-Büttiker formalism, incorporating the helical magnetic texture and spin-orbit interaction.

Result: Finite spin polarization is generated simultaneously along the x, y, and z directions, unlike conventional single-axis spin filters. The polarization is strongly modified by spin-orbit coupling and depends on helical system parameters; notably, long-range hopping significantly enhances spin filtering, yielding large polarization in all three directions.

Conclusion: Non-collinear helical antiferromagnets with spin-orbit coupling enable multidirectional spin polarization by breaking symmetry, offering a route to enhanced and tunable spin-filtering effects.

Abstract: We investigate spin-dependent transport in a non-collinear helical antiferromagnet in the presence of spin-orbit coupling within a tight-binding framework. Using the Landauer-Büttiker formalism, we analyze the generation of spin polarization arising from the combined effects of the helical magnetic texture and spin-orbit interaction. We find that finite spin polarization can be generated simultaneously along the $\hat{x}$, $\hat{y}$, and $\hat{z}$-directions, in contrast to the predominantly single-axis spin polarization commonly observed in conventional spin-filtering systems. The multidirectional spin polarization originates from the symmetry breaking introduced by the non-collinear magnetic order. We further show that the spin polarization is modified significantly in the presence of spin-orbit coupling and it has strong dependence on the parameters associated with the helical system. In particular, long-range hopping significantly enhances the spin-filtering effect, leading to large spin polarization components along all three spatial directions.

</details>


<div id='math.DG'></div>

# math.DG [[Back]](#toc)

### [153] [Sharp harmonic-mean inequalities for Neumann and Aharonov--Bohm spectra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24488)
*Daguang Chen,Chengxi Yang*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: Sharp two-eigenvalue isoperimetric inequalities are proved for Neumann and Aharonov-Bohm Neumann spectra on surfaces, with equality for disks, using a reciprocal Rayleigh-Ritz principle, Green-level comparison, and a magnetic spectral ordering theorem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to establish sharp isoperimetric inequalities involving the second and third (or first two magnetic) Neumann eigenvalues on surfaces, extending classical eigenvalue optimization to the Aharonov-Bohm setting.

Method: Combines a two-dimensional reciprocal Rayleigh-Ritz principle with Green-level comparison. Uses a spectral ordering theorem for magnetic spherical caps via the factorization L0=T*T, L1=TT* and an exact Neumann-Dirichlet spectral shift.

Result: For smooth, simply connected proper domains in S^2, the harmonic mean of mu_2 and mu_3 is bounded by the first positive Neumann eigenvalue of the equal-area geodesic disk, with equality only for disks. Analogous magnetic bounds hold for surfaces with bounded Gaussian curvature. Sharp full-sphere, closed-surface, and annular inequalities (in terms of conformal modulus and flux) are also obtained.

Conclusion: Sharp two-eigenvalue isoperimetric inequalities hold for both Neumann and Aharonov-Bohm Neumann spectra, with disks as unique extremizers, and magnetic spectral ordering provides a key tool.

Abstract: We prove sharp two-eigenvalue isoperimetric inequalities for Neumann and Aharonov--Bohm Neumann spectra on surfaces. If $Ω\subset\mathbb S^2$ is smooth, simply connected and proper, then the harmonic mean of $μ_2(Ω)$ and $μ_3(Ω)$ is bounded above by the first positive Neumann eigenvalue of the equal-area geodesic disk, with equality only for disks. For simply connected surfaces with Gaussian curvature bounded above, we obtain the magnetic analogue for the first two Aharonov--Bohm eigenvalues. The proof combines a two-dimensional reciprocal Rayleigh--Ritz principle with Green-level comparison. A key additional ingredient is a spectral ordering theorem for magnetic spherical caps: for $0<ν<1/2$, the first two eigenvalues lie in the angular sectors of effective orders $ν$ and $1-ν$. We prove this by the factorization $L_0=T^*T$, $L_1=TT^*$ and an exact Neumann--Dirichlet spectral shift. We also obtain sharp full-sphere and closed-surface bounds, and an annular inequality in terms of conformal modulus and flux.

</details>


### [154] [Shrinking Kähler-Ricci solitons on toric fano fibrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24884)
*Tristan C. Collins,Genggeng Huang,Freid Tong,Yulun Xu*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: A smooth toric Fano fibration admits a complete gradient shrinking Kähler-Ricci soliton.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract provides no explicit motivation statement.

Method: The result is established via a proof (details not specified in the abstract).

Result: Every smooth toric Fano fibration admits a complete gradient shrinking Kähler-Ricci soliton.

Conclusion: Existence of complete gradient shrinking Kähler-Ricci solitons is guaranteed for smooth toric Fano fibrations.

Abstract: We prove that a smooth toric Fano fibration admits a complete gradient shrinking Kähler-Ricci soliton.

</details>


### [155] [A quantitative inequality for general area-minimizing hypercones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24958)
*Gongping Niu*

Main category: math.DG

TL;DR: The paper proves a quantitative isoperimetric-type inequality for general area-minimizing hypercones (possibly with nonisolated singularities), extending the unweighted inequality from regular cones. The proof uses calibration with vector fields built via minimal foliations and projection kernels weighted by a positive Jacobi field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior quantitative stability inequalities for area-minimizing hypercones were only known for regular (smooth) cones. This work aims to extend them to general area-minimizing hypercones, which may have nonisolated singularities, broadening the applicability of the quantitative inequality.

Method: The proof follows a calibration argument. The key difficulty is constructing the vector fields, since pointwise asymptotic estimates for positive Jacobi fields used in the regular case do not apply directly. Instead, the author uses Zhihan Wang's minimal foliations on the two sides of the cone, integrating projection kernels with a positive Jacobi field as weight on each leaf. The kernel construction provides the needed divergence bound, and Wang's weak Harnack inequality and growth estimates yield the integral bounds that establish linear growth of the vector fields.

Result: There exists a constant c_C > 0 such that for every R > 0 and every set F of locally finite perimeter with F Δ E ⋐ B_R, the normalized perimeter excess satisfies (Per(F;B_R) − Per(E;B_R))/R^n ≥ c_C (|F Δ E|/R^{n+1})^2. Thus the unweighted quantitative inequality is extended from regular to general area-minimizing hypercones.

Conclusion: The unweighted quantitative inequality for area-minimizing hypercones is extended from the regular case to general hypercones with possibly nonisolated singularities. The calibration framework carries over, with the main innovation being the vector field construction via minimal foliations and weighted projection kernels.

Abstract: Let $\mathbf{C}=\partial E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$ be an area-minimizing hypercone. The cone may have nonisolated singularities. We prove that there is a constant $c_{\mathbf{C}}>0$ such that \[
  \frac{\operatorname{Per}(F;B_R)-\operatorname{Per}(E;B_R)}{R^n}
  \geq c_{\mathbf{C}}
  \left(\frac{|F\triangle E|}{R^{n+1}}\right)^2 \] for every $R>0$ and every set $F$ of locally finite perimeter with $F\triangle E\Subset B_R$. This extends the unweighted quantitative inequality from regular area-minimizing hypercones to general area-minimizing hypercones.
  We follow the calibration argument in the author's earlier work on regular cones. The main change is the construction of the vector fields. The pointwise asymptotic estimates for positive Jacobi fields used in the regular case do not directly apply here. We use the minimal foliations constructed by Zhihan Wang on the two sides of the cone. On each leaf, we integrate projection kernels with a positive Jacobi field as the weight. The kernel construction gives the required divergence bound. Wang's weak Harnack inequality and growth estimates give the integral bounds needed to prove linear growth of the vector fields.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.stat-mech'></div>

# cond-mat.stat-mech [[Back]](#toc)

### [156] [Lowest-known Energy Configuration of $N=100\,000$ Coulomb Charges in a Disk: Breaking the $10^{5}$ Barrier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24722)
*Georgiy K. Lavrov,Eduard G. Nikonov*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: First computation of the lowest-known energy configuration for N=100,000 classical point charges confined to a disk interacting via 1/r Coulomb potential, more than doubling previous record (N=40,886).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To push the Thomson problem in a disk to unprecedented system sizes and test the extrapolation of asymptotic energy expansions to extreme scales.

Method: An adaptive defect-targeting subdomain optimization strategy, yielding approximately linear growth of wall-clock time per cycle with N; N=100,000 took ~31 hours on a single 24-core CPU without GPU.

Result: Energy E_min=7.80466624157×10^9, deviating only 2.525×10^-7 from the asymptotic expansion fitted to 100≤N≤5000. Bond-orientational order analysis shows a polycrystalline bulk threaded by radial grain boundaries.

Conclusion: Establishes a new benchmark for two-dimensional Coulomb systems and validates asymptotic expansion extrapolation at extreme scales.

Abstract: We report the first calculation of the lowest-known energy configuration for $N=100\,000$ classical point charges confined to a disk and interacting via the $1/r$ Coulomb potential - a system size never before achieved for the Thomson problem in a disk, more than doubling the previous record of $N=40\,886$ reported by Amore and Zarate. An adaptive defect-targeting subdomain optimization strategy yields an approximately linear growth of the wall-clock time per cycle with $N$; for $N=100\,000$ a single $24$-core CPU workstation without GPU acceleration required $\approx31$ hours. The energy $E_{\min}=7.80466624157\times10^{9}$ deviates from the asymptotic expansion fitted to $100\le N\le5000$ data by only $2.525\times10^{-7}$, providing a stringent test of its extrapolation to unprecedented scales. Bond-orientational order analysis reveals a polycrystalline bulk threaded by radial grain boundaries, establishing a new benchmark for two-dimensional Coulomb systems.

</details>

# hep-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [157] [Multitask Jet Analysis with Vision-Language Models: A Physics-Informed Four-Panel Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24334)
*Lu Zhang,Rachik Soualah,Abbes Amira*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: Vision-Language Models adapted with LoRA can perform jet tagging and structured jet analysis from a single physics-informed RGB image, achieving strong performance across classification, attribute prediction, and consistency tasks, with transfer to broader and real data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate identification of physics objects is a bottleneck in disentangling event topologies at high-energy physics colliders, where enormous recorded data make jet tagging challenging. The paper explores whether VLMs can serve as a common interface for structured jet analysis.

Method: Each JetClass jet is encoded as a 224x224 RGB image with four panels: p_T flow of constituents; charged/neutral-hadron and electromagnetic composition; p_T-weighted impact-parameter significances with displaced-track multiplicity; and signed-track p_T densities with jet-charge asymmetry. Four open VLMs are adapted via LoRA for class classification (QCD, Higgs, W/Z, top), six-field attribute prediction, and cross-panel consistency with replaced-panel localization, tested on 24000 balanced JetClass jets.

Result: Ablations show impact-parameter lifetime signature dominates heavy-flavor tagging, with jet charge separating W and Z. Zero-shot recall is near chance (3.33%-10.57%). After adaptation, Macro recall grows with training size; Gemma4-E4B reaches 75.05% (74.87% F_1), 93.31% Task 2 field-mean, and 99.88%/99.75% binary/localization recall. Budget reallocation raises Zqq recall by up to 9.83 points. Transfer to JetClass-II and real Aspen Open Jets is retained (recall >70% and Task 2/3 >92% with 3000 target jets).

Conclusion: Rasterizing energy flow, species, displacement, and jet charge with parameter-efficient VLM adaptation enables jet analysis through a single instruction-conditioned interface for future collider data.

Abstract: The enormous recorded data at high-energy physics (HEP) colliders make the accurate identification of physics objects a bottleneck in disentangling event topologies, where machine learning has become a standard tool for jet tagging. In this work, we examine whether Vision-Language Models (VLMs) can provide a common interface for structured jet analysis from a single physics-informed image. Each JetClass jet becomes a 224x224 RGB image with four panels encoding p_T flow of all constituents; charged-hadron, neutral-hadron, and electromagnetic composition; p_T-weighted impact-parameter significances with displaced-track multiplicity; and signed-track p_T densities with local p_T^k-weighted jet-charge asymmetry. Four open VLMs are adapted with low-rank adaptation (LoRA) for class classification across QCD, Higgs, W/Z, and top jets, six-field attribute prediction, and cross-panel consistency with replaced-panel localization, evaluated on 24000 balanced JetClass test jets. Ablations identify the impact-parameter lifetime signature of heavy-flavor tagging as dominant, with jet charge separating the nearly mass-degenerate hadronic W and Z. Zero-shot recall stays near chance (3.33%-10.57%), once adapted, Task 1 Macro recall grows monotonically with training size, and Gemma4-E4B reaches 75.05% (74.87% F_1), 93.31% Task 2 field-mean, and 99.88%/99.75% binary/localization recall. Reallocating budget toward W/Z jets raises Zqq recall by up to 9.83 points at near-constant Macro recall. Transfer to broader-topology JetClass-II and real-data Aspen Open Jets (probing the simulation-to-data gap) is retained: with 3000 target jets, Task 1 recall exceeds 70% on JetClass-II and every Task 2/3 metric exceeds 92%. Thus rasterizing energy flow, species, displacement, and jet charge, with parameter-efficient adaptation, supports jet analysis through one instruction-conditioned interface for future collider data.

</details>

### [158] [LiNiO2/NiO Phase Prediction Using Artificial Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22604)
*Jonas Scheunert,Shamail Ahmed,Thomas Demuth,Andreas Beyer,Kerstin Volz*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Neural networks (CNNs and ViTs) are trained on synthetic 4DSTEM diffraction patterns to distinguish LiNiO2 from NiO, then tested on experimental data, with GradCAM used to interpret decisions; results are robust even on highly varying data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identifying material phases is key in material analysis. Neural networks offer fast, cheap analysis of large 4DSTEM image datasets. Distinguishing LiNiO2 from NiO is important and challenging in the lithium-ion battery community because NiO formation limits battery capacity.

Method: Train multiple network architectures (classical CNNs and different forms of vision transformers, ViTs) on synthetic diffraction-pattern images, test them on both synthetic and experimentally recorded 4DSTEM diffraction patterns, and interpret network decision-making using the GradCAM method.

Result: The networks show very robust results, especially when dealing with highly varying data, a regime where traditional template-matching methods typically struggle.

Conclusion: Neural-network approaches (CNNs and ViTs) provide a robust, fast method for distinguishing LiNiO2 and NiO phases in 4DSTEM data, outperforming traditional template matching on highly varying data.

Abstract: In the realm of material analysis, identifying different material phases is of key importance. Artificial intelligence in the form of neural networks provides a very fast and, once trained, computationally inexpensive method for analysing large amounts of image data, like the sets of diffraction patterns generated during four-dimensional scanning transmission electron microscopy (4DSTEM) dataset acquisition. In this work, we train multiple network architectures on images of this type to distinguish between the phases of LiNiO2 and NiO, an important and challenging distinction in the lithium-ion battery community, since the formation of NiO limits battery capacity. We test both classical convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and different forms of vision transformers (ViTs). Our networks are trained on synthetic images and tested on experimentally recorded diffraction patterns. Additionally, we also investigate the decision-making of our networks using the GradCAM method. We test our networks on both synthetic as well as experimental diffraction patterns. Our networks exhibit very robust results, especially when dealing with highly varying data, an area where traditional template-matching methods typically struggle.

</details>


### [159] [Electronic-Entropy-Driven Phase Transitions in Compressed Iron Oxides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24281)
*S. Azadi,S. M. Vinko,C. Crepisson,A. Principi,T. D. Kuehne,M. S. Bahramy*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Electronic entropy, normally a secondary correction, becomes a primary thermodynamic driver under strong electronic excitation, inducing polymorphic and stoichiometric phase transformations in compressed iron oxides.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electronic entropy is usually treated as a minor correction to structural stability. This work challenges that assumption by examining whether strong electronic excitation can make electronic entropy a dominant driving force for phase transformations.

Method: Finite-temperature density functional theory is used to compute electronic-temperature-dependent Gibbs free energies of Fe2O, FeO, Fe4O5, Fe3O4, and several Fe2O3 polymorphs (α, ι, ζ, η, θ) across 60–260 GPa.

Result: At 60–140 GPa, excitation reorders Fe2O3 polymorph stability (ι → η). At 180 GPa, FeO is stabilized over an intermediate electronic temperature range while η-Fe2O3 wins at higher T. At 220–260 GPa, Fe2O is lowest at low T but FeO stabilizes as electronic temperature rises.

Conclusion: Electronic entropy controls both crystal-structure stability at fixed composition and competition between iron-oxide stoichiometries. The predicted electronic-entropy-driven phase boundaries offer a route to nonthermal structural transformations in ultrafast and high-energy-density experiments.

Abstract: Electronic entropy is usually treated as a secondary correction to structural stability, but under strong electronic excitation it can become a primary thermodynamic driving force. Here we show that electronic entropy can drive both polymorphic and stoichiometric phase transformations in compressed iron oxides. Using finite-temperature density functional theory, we calculate the electronic-temperature-dependent Gibbs free energies of Fe$_2$O, FeO, Fe$_4$O$_5$, Fe$_3$O$_4$, and multiple Fe$_2$O$_3$ polymorphs, including $α$-, $ι$-, $ζ$-, $η$-, and $θ$-Fe$_2$O$_3$, over the pressure range 60--260 GPa. At 60-140 GPa, electronic excitation mainly reorganizes the relative stability of Fe$_2$O$_3$ polymorphs, driving transitions from $ι$-Fe$_2$O$_3$ to $η$-Fe$_2$O$_3$. At 180 GPa, the free-energy landscape becomes strongly competitive as FeO is stabilized over an intermediate range of electronic temperature, while $η$-Fe$_2$O$_3$ becomes favourable at higher T. At 220-260 GPa, the lowest-free-energy phase at low T is the Fe-rich compound Fe$_2$O, but increasing electronic temperature stabilizes FeO. These results demonstrate that electronic entropy can control not only the relative stability of crystal structures at fixed composition, but also the competition between different iron-oxide stoichiometries. The predicted electronic-entropy-driven phase boundaries provide a route to nonthermal structural transformations in ultrafast and high-energy-density experiments.

</details>


### [160] [Experimentally validated process-microstructure-property relations of bainitic steels derived from phase-field simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23737)
*Dhanunjaya Kumar Nerella,Muhammad Adil Ali,Oguz Gulbay,Oleg Shchyglo,Ingo Steinbach*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study links processing conditions to bainitic steel microstructure and mechanical properties using 3D phase-field simulations, correlating simulated microstructures with yield surface changes under multiaxial loading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how thermal processing affects microstructure and mechanical properties of bainitic steels, and to develop predictive process-structure-property models that support material design and application.

Method: Three-dimensional phase-field simulations are used to capture microstructural features including bainite morphology, phase distribution, and retained austenite content, which are then correlated with yield strength changes under multiaxial load via the yield surface.

Result: Optimized processing routes refine the microstructure, enhance mechanical properties, and significantly alter yield surface characteristics.

Conclusion: The findings offer valuable insights for designing and applying bainitic steels and for developing predictive models linking process-structure-property relationships.

Abstract: This study examines the impact of processing conditions, such as thermalprocessing, on the resulting microstructure and mechanical properties. Spe-cial emphasis is placed on microstructural features obtained from three-dimensional phase-field simulations, which provide detailed insights into bai-nite morphology, phase distribution and retained austenite content. Thesesimulated microstructures are correlated with changes in yield strength undermultiaxial load, as represented in the yield surface of the material. The re-sults demonstrate that optimized processing routes can refine the microstruc-ture, enhance mechanical properties and significantly alter the yield surfacecharacteristics. These findings provide valuable insights for the design andapplication of bainitic steels, as well as for the development of predictivemodels linking process-structure-property relationships.

</details>


### [161] [Transitions between bulk and interfacial fracture in diamond/$c$BN heterostructures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23963)
*Wei Qiu,Feiyu Zhou,Xiaonan Wang,Feng Xie,Yan Chen,Shengying Yue,Yilun Liu,Penghua Ying*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A DFT plus fine-tuned atomistic foundation model reveals how interfacial termination, crystallographic orientation, and diffusion-induced intermixing control tensile fracture strength and fracture-plane selection in diamond/cBN heterostructures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whether a crack-free heterostructure fails at its interface or within an adjoining phase depends on the relative cohesion of competing atomic planes, yet how interfacial chemistry, orientation, and intermixing reshape this competition was unclear.

Method: Combined density functional theory (DFT) with a fine-tuned atomistic foundation model to resolve tensile fracture in coherent diamond/cubic boron nitride (cBN) heterostructures; the potential reproduces independent DFT tensile responses, including an unseen (001) interface. Atom-resolved stress fields, DFT separation energetics, and electron localization analysis were used.

Result: The potential reproduces independent DFT tensile responses including an unseen (001) orientation. Termination, orientation, and intermixing jointly determine fracture resistance and plane selection: C-N-bonded (111) and C-B-bonded (001) stay interface-controlled across the intermixing range, while pristine C-B-bonded (111) fractures at a neighboring B-N plane inside cBN. Increasing diffusion from 0 to 50.0% lowers fracture strength nonlinearly from 50.3 to 15.8 GPa and drives a bulk-to-interface transition through three regimes (cBN fracture up to 4.86%, config-dependent competition at 7.6-10.1%, interfacial fracture at >=12.5%). The transition involves stress relocation and reversal of relative cohesion of competing planes.

Conclusion: The findings establish an atomistic framework for controlling fracture resistance and fracture pathways in strongly bonded heterostructures.

Abstract: Whether an initially crack-free heterostructure fails at its interface or within an adjoining phase is controlled by the relative cohesion of competing atomic planes, but how interfacial chemistry, crystallographic orientation, and intermixing reshape this competition remains unclear. Here, we combine density functional theory (DFT) with a fine-tuned atomistic foundation model to resolve tensile fracture in coherent diamond/cubic boron nitride (cBN) heterostructures. The resulting potential reproduces independent DFT tensile responses, including an unseen (001) interface orientation. Interfacial termination, orientation, and diffusion-induced intermixing jointly determine fracture resistance and fracture-plane selection. C-N-bonded (111) and C-B-bonded (001) remain interface-controlled throughout the investigated intermixing range, whereas pristine C-B-bonded (111) fractures at a neighboring B-N plane inside cBN because the interface is more strongly bound. Increasing the diffusion fraction from 0 to 50.0% causes a nonlinear decrease in fracture strength from 50.3 to 15.8 GPa and drives a bulk-to-interface transition through three regimes: cBN fracture up to 4.86%, configuration-dependent competition between 7.6 and 10.1%, and interfacial fracture at 12.5% and above. Atom-resolved stress fields and DFT separation energetics show that this transition is associated with stress relocation and a reversal in the relative cohesion of competing planes, while electron localization analysis connects the cohesion hierarchy to termination- and orientation-dependent bonding. These results establish an atomistic framework for controlling fracture resistance and fracture pathways in strongly bonded heterostructures.

</details>


### [162] [Polar vibrational excitations and glass-like response in relaxor PMN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24100)
*Kehan Cai,Pinchen Xie,Yifan Li,Roberto Car*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using machine-learned atomistic models trained on first-principles data, the authors show that compositional disorder in the relaxor PMN creates a continuum of polar excitations and a boson peak whose excess quasi-localized modes explain its anomalous dielectric, optical, and thermal behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relaxor ferroelectrics such as PMN retain crystalline order yet display glass-like dielectric and thermal responses. The microscopic origin linking these dielectric, optical, and thermal anomalies remains unclear.

Method: Atomistic models trained on first-principles data are used to simulate PMN. The authors analyze how compositional disorder couples elastic motion to electric polarization, compute polar vibrational excitations, low-frequency susceptibility, far-infrared spectra, and identify a boson peak with excess phonon-like and quasi-localized modes.

Result: Compositional disorder produces a continuum of polar vibrational excitations, unlike the discrete polar optical modes of conventional ferroelectric PbTiO3. Simulations reproduce the temperature dependence of the low-frequency susceptibility, and far-infrared spectra agree with experiments. A disorder-induced boson peak comprises quasi-localized excitations with quartic frequency-dependent density of states and strong anharmonicity, explaining anomalous specific heat above 5 K.

Conclusion: The dielectric, optical, and thermal anomalies of the crystalline relaxor PMN are connected through its disorder-induced polar excitation spectrum, providing a unified microscopic picture analogous to structural glasses.

Abstract: Relaxor ferroelectrics retain crystalline order while exhibiting glass-like dielectric and thermal responses. Using atomistic models trained on first-principles data, we investigate these responses in the prototypical relaxor Pb(Mg_{1/3}Nb_{2/3})O_3 (PMN). Compositional disorder couples elastic motion to electric polarization, producing a continuum of polar vibrational excitations, unlike the discrete polar optical modes of conventional ferroelectric PbTiO_3. The simulations qualitatively reproduce the temperature dependence of the low-frequency susceptibility, while the calculated far-infrared spectra agree closely with experiments. At low frequencies, disorder in PMN generates a boson peak comprising excess phonon-like modes and quasi-localized excitations. The latter exhibit a quartic frequency dependence of their density of states and pronounced anharmonicity, as in structural glasses. These excess modes account for PMN's anomalous behavior in the specific heat for temperature higher than 5 K. These results connect the dielectric, optical, and thermal anomalies of a crystalline relaxor through its disorder-induced polar excitation spectrum.

</details>
