### [163] [Element-dependent buckling loads of stiffened panels under cantilevered shear](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22656)
*Lifeng Wang,Zhongli Qiu,Xuanhao Cheng,Run Du,Wenming Cheng,Min Xie,Xiong Rao*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: Linearized buckling loads in stiffened panels depend on the stress stiffness assembled by shell elements; this work evaluates how different element formulations and their second-variation truncations affect predicted buckling loads across various cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The linearized buckling load of a stiffened panel depends on the stress stiffness assembled by its shell element. Different element implementations and truncations of the second variation may yield inconsistent results, so the paper aims to quantify which formulation best matches the complete second variation and reference solutions.

Method: Exported operators were compared against three truncations of one second variation. ANSYS SHELL181 (with and without a rotation-rotation drilling/bending block), SHELL281 (with block and couplings in both passes), and Abaqus S4/S8R were tested against a 20-node continuum. Tests covered optimized panels under cantilevered shear, conventionally stiffened panels, and cases under shear flow, cylinders, open beams, and axial compression.

Result: Removing the rotation-rotation block recovers the SHELL181 perturbation load factor to 0.02%. Abaqus S4 matches the complete second variation's critical mode to 1.0000 on translations and load factor to 1.7%, versus 17% and 34% for the other two forms. On an optimized shear panel, the 20-node continuum lies 3-6% above S4/SHELL281, which are 11% and 23% below both SHELL181 passes and 25% above S8R. On a conventionally stiffened panel, SHELL181 passes stand 1.0% and 3.5% above the complete form, 9% and 21% at half rib pitch. Under shear flow, cylinders, open beams, and axial compression, the three forms coincide within 0.3%.

Conclusion: Striping the rotation-rotation drilling block aligns SHELL181 perturbation with the classic pass. S4 and SHELL281 best match the complete second variation, while SHELL181 passes and S8R deviate significantly on shear-dominated stiffened panels. All three forms converge on shear flow, cylinder, open beam, and axial compression cases.

Abstract: The linearized buckling load of a stiffened panel depends on the stress stiffness its shell element assembles. We read it from exported operators against three truncations of one second variation. The classic pass of ANSYS SHELL181 carries a rotation-rotation block pairing the drilling freedom with the bending rotations and its perturbation pass does not; removing the block recovers the perturbation load factor to 0.02%. SHELL281 carries block and couplings in both passes. Abaqus S4 matches the critical mode of the complete second variation to 1.0000 on the translations and its load factor to 1.7%, against 17% and 34% for the other two forms. On an optimized panel under cantilevered shear a 20-node continuum lies 3% to 6% above that form, S4 and SHELL281, 11% and 23% below both SHELL181 passes and 25% above Abaqus S8R, at the finest meshes. On a conventionally stiffened panel the SHELL181 passes stand 1.0% and 3.5% above the complete form, 9% and 21% at half the rib pitch; under a shear flow, on cylinders, open beams and under axial compression the three forms coincide and no pass parts by more than 0.3%.

</details>


<div id='math.FA'></div>

# math.FA [[Back]](#toc)

### [164] [Rayleigh-Residual Flow I: Normal Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24901)
*Eric Shen*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: Studies the squared Rayleigh-residual functional on complex projective space and its negative gradient flow, characterizing critical points, eigenvalues, and dynamics via a replicator equation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand the geometry and dynamics of the Rayleigh-residual gradient flow, whose local minima are the eigenlines, and analyze how polynomial reweighting and Haar-random initialization affect spectral exploration.

Method: Analyze critical points of the squared Rayleigh-residual functional; show positive-residual critical points have descending tangent directions; describe the critical set for normal matrices via circles through spectral points; map amplitude dynamics to a rank-≤4 replicator equation; compute logarithmic first integrals and reduced interior trajectories, transverse escape rates, reweighting effects, and filtered-ensemble density.

Result: Local minima are exactly the eigenlines; critical set for normal matrices described by circles; replicator equation yields explicit log first integrals and reduced trajectories; escape rates computed; distinction between local saddle trapping and Haar-initial-state concentration; density of filtered ensemble derived; numerical examples show convergence, nonmonotone Rayleigh motion, and improved finite-time spectral exploration via low-degree random polynomial filters.

Conclusion: Provides a geometric-dynamical characterization of Rayleigh-residual flow, linking eigenline minima, replicator dynamics, and polynomial filtering to spectral exploration, supported by numerical experiments.

Abstract: We study the squared Rayleigh-residual functional on complex projective space and its negative gradient flow. For an arbitrary complex matrix, every positive-residual critical point has a strictly descending tangent direction; consequently, the local minima are precisely the eigenlines. For normal matrices, we describe the critical set in terms of circles through spectral points and identify the amplitude dynamics with a replicator equation whose payoff matrix has rank at most four. This gives explicit logarithmic first integrals and a reduced representation of interior trajectories. We compute transverse escape rates near critical components and analyze how polynomial reweighting changes unstable coordinates. Finally, we distinguish local saddle trapping from concentration of Haar-distributed initial states and derive the density of the filtered ensemble. Numerical examples with random spectra and a Jordan block illustrate convergence, nonmonotone Rayleigh motion, and the improvement of finite-time spectral exploration by low-degree random polynomial filters.

</details>


### [165] [A purely metric characterization of supercritical Sobolev spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24520)
*Ryan Alvarado,Efstathios Konstantinos Chrontsios Garitsis*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: The paper gives a metric, derivative-free characterization of local supercritical Sobolev mappings: for p>n (or p>Q), the local Sobolev space coincides with a "compactly Hölder" class CH^{p,1-n/p}, with quantitative seminorm comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sobolev spaces are usually defined via weak derivatives (or, on metric spaces, via Hajłasz/Newtonian upper gradients). A purely metric, derivative-free characterization in the supercritical regime p>n or p>Q is desirable, extending the known supercritical case where Sobolev maps are Hölder-continuous.

Method: Introduce and use the compactly Hölder class CH^{p,1-Q/p} on a complete Q-Ahlfors regular metric space X supporting a p-Poincaré inequality with p>Q, and prove it equals the continuous representatives of the local Hajłasz space M^{1,p}_loc and Newtonian space N^{1,p}_loc. Also derive quantitative comparisons of the corresponding seminorms.

Result: Shows f: R^n -> R^m continuous lies in W^{1,p}_loc(R^n:R^m) iff f is (p,1-n/p)-compactly Hölder, for p>n; more generally CH^{p,1-Q/p}(X:V) equals the continuous representatives of M^{1,p}_loc(X:V) and N^{1,p}_loc(X:V) for Banach-space targets V.

Conclusion: Provides a metric, derivative-free characterization of supercritical local Sobolev spaces on Euclidean and metric measure spaces, with equivalent seminorms, unifying Hajłasz and Newtonian descriptions.

Abstract: In this paper we provide a purely metric and derivative-free characterization of continuous mappings in the local supercritical Sobolev space. More specifically, we show that a continuous mapping $f\colon\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^m$ lies in the local Sobolev space $W^{1,p}_\text{loc}(\mathbb{R}^n\colon\mathbb{R}^m)$ if and only if $f$ is a $(p,1-n/p)$-compactly Hölder mapping, where $p>n$. In fact, we prove the following more general result: whenever $X$ is a complete $Q$-Ahlfors regular metric space supporting a $p$-Poincaré inequality with $p>Q$, and $V$ is any Banach space, the compactly Hölder class $CH^{p,1-Q/p}(X\colon V)$ consists precisely of the continuous representatives of the local Hajłasz and Newtonian Sobolev spaces $M^{1,p}_\text{loc} (X \colon V)$ and $N^{1,p}_\text{loc}(X\colon V)$. We also establish quantitative comparisons between the corresponding seminorms.

</details>


<div id='math-ph'></div>

# math-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [166] [Routing and memory in the three-dimensional mirror model on K-lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23244)
*Jian Gu,Paul Schaeffer,Qin Hao*

Main category: math-ph

TL;DR: Numerical study of delocalization observables in the 3D Manhattan mirror model (K-lattice), separating transmission channel locations from routing between them. Finds a resolved deficit of physical transmission relative to an exact conditional comparison, suppression of nearby transmitting channels, inverse-square-root decay of old-memory remainder, and finite routing cycles despite a connected auxiliary contact graph.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand delocalization in the 3D Manhattan mirror model (K-lattice) by separating the locations of transmitting boundary channels from the routing between them, aiming to identify estimates that may assist a rigorous proof of delocalization.

Method: Principal experiment separates transmitting boundary channel locations from routing between them; derives an exact conditional comparison preserving all transmission and reflection aperture sets. A second experiment resolves repeated-vertex contributions by return age (squared norm of signed old-memory remainder). Annular experiments quantify continuation losses; history dependence tested for replication. A periodic configuration's auxiliary contact graph is also analyzed.

Result: Statistically resolved deficit of physical transmission relative to the conditional comparison; nearby transmitting channels suppressed relative to a uniform subset of same cardinality; squared norm of signed old-memory remainder consistent with inverse-square-root decay; decreasing continuation losses in annular experiments; initially apparent history dependence fails independent replication; periodic configuration has connected auxiliary contact graph but only finite routing cycles.

Conclusion: Results identify estimates on reflection routing and adaptively selected revisit charges that may assist a rigorous proof, but do not establish an infinite-volume delocalized phase.

Abstract: We study numerical observables relevant to delocalization in the three-dimensional Manhattan mirror model, also known as the K-lattice. Our principal experiment separates the locations of transmitting boundary channels from the routing between them. We derive an exact conditional comparison that preserves all transmission and reflection aperture sets, and find a statistically resolved deficit of physical transmission relative to this comparison. Nearby transmitting channels are themselves suppressed relative to a uniform subset with the same cardinality. A second experiment resolves the contribution of repeated vertices by their return age. The squared norm of the signed old-memory remainder is consistent with an inverse-square-root decay over the measured range. Annular experiments quantify decreasing continuation losses, while an initially apparent history dependence fails independent replication. Finally, a periodic configuration has a connected auxiliary contact graph but only finite routing cycles. These results identify estimates on reflection routing and adaptively selected revisit charges that may assist a rigorous proof. They do not establish an infinite-volume delocalized phase.

</details>


### [167] [Local conservation laws and symmetries of three-dimensional incompressible Euler flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23772)
*Alexey Shevyakov*

Main category: math-ph

TL;DR: This paper classifies all smooth local conservation laws, symmetry characteristics, and adjoint symmetries of 3D homogeneous incompressible Euler flow, showing classical balances plus generalized time-dependent momentum exhaust the classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding local conservation laws and symmetries of the 3D incompressible Euler equations is fundamental to fluid dynamics, yet a complete classification accounting for explicit time/position dependence and arbitrary finite-order derivatives was lacking.

Method: The proof proceeds in three stages: classifying adjoint symmetries via their determining equations, verifying explicit multiplier identities, and establishing the correspondence between multiplier restrictions and current classes. A shared highest-derivative response analysis reduces both symmetry problems to order at most one.

Result: Modulo trivial currents, the classification reduces to classical mass, momentum, angular-momentum, kinetic-energy, and helicity balances plus generalized time-dependent momentum. Every symmetry characteristic is equivalent on solutions to one from a point transformation; every adjoint symmetry has a multiplier representative whose product with the Euler equations is a divergence.

Conclusion: The classical conservation laws, extended by generalized time-dependent momentum, exhaust all smooth local conservation laws of 3D homogeneous incompressible Euler flow. Symmetry characteristics and adjoint symmetries admit simple point-transformation and multiplier representatives, respectively.

Abstract: We classify all smooth local conservation laws of three-dimensional homogeneous incompressible Euler flow in velocity and pressure, allowing explicit time and position and derivatives of arbitrary finite order. Modulo currents vanishing on solutions and currents with identically zero divergence, the classical mass, momentum, angular-momentum, kinetic-energy and helicity balances, together with generalised time-dependent momentum, exhaust the classification. We also classify all smooth finite-order local symmetry characteristics and adjoint symmetries: differential expressions satisfying the linearised Euler equations and their formal adjoint on solutions, respectively. Every symmetry characteristic is equivalent on solutions to one induced by a point transformation. Every adjoint symmetry has a multiplier representative, whose product with the Euler equations is identically a divergence. These representatives exhaust the restrictions of all local conservation-law multipliers to solutions. The conservation-law proof has three stages: classification of adjoint symmetries using their determining equations, verification of explicit multiplier identities, and the correspondence between multiplier restrictions and current classes. A common analysis of highest-derivative responses reduces both symmetry problems to order at most one.

</details>


### [168] [The Hadamard parametrix on globally hyperbolic spacetimes with Robin boundary conditions: Fundamental solutions and Hadamard states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24465)
*Beatrice Costeri,Claudio Dappiaggi,Benito Alberto Juárez-Aubry*

Main category: math-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies the Klein-Gordon equation with Robin boundary conditions on globally hyperbolic spacetimes with a timelike boundary. It proves existence/uniqueness of advanced and retarded fundamental solutions, characterizes their wavefront set, defines Robin-Hadamard two-point correlation functions (with a local Hadamard form including reflected null rays), proves equivalence to the wavefront set (microlocal) condition—a Radzikowski-type theorem—and establishes existence of such states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum field theory on curved spacetimes with a timelike boundary requires a well-defined propagator theory and a suitable notion of Hadamard states. Robin boundary conditions generalize Dirichlet/Neumann conditions, but the associated microlocal analysis—especially with reflections of null geodesics at the boundary—had not been fully developed.

Method: Proves existence and uniqueness and studies structural properties of the advanced/retarded fundamental solutions; characterizes their wavefront set in the interior under infinitesimal convexity of the boundary and local finiteness of boundary reflections, using the Melrose–Sjöstrand propagation of singularities theorem. Defines the Robin-Hadamard two-point function as a positive distribution with prescribed antisymmetric part and wavefront set, and gives a local Hadamard parametrix form with reflected-ray contributions whose coefficients solve transport equations with Robin matching conditions at the boundary. Proves equivalence between this local form and the wavefront set condition (Radzikowski analogue), and uses a deformation argument starting from the ground state on static backgrounds to prove existence.

Result: Existence and uniqueness of advanced/retarded fundamental solutions; wavefront set characterization for broken null bicharacteristics involving boundary reflections; a local Hadamard parametrix with reflected-ray contributions valid for any finite number of reflections; equivalence of the local Hadamard form with the wavefront set condition (counterpart of Radzikowski's theorem); and existence of Robin-Hadamard two-point correlation functions on the considered class of spacetimes.

Conclusion: The work extends Hadamard states and Radzikowski's microlocal characterization to spacetimes with a timelike boundary under Robin conditions, providing the propagator theory, the wavefront set analysis including reflected rays, and the existence of Robin-Hadamard states, thereby laying foundations for QFT with boundary conditions.

Abstract: On a $d$-dimensional, $d\geq 2$, globally hyperbolic spacetime with a timelike boundary $(\mathcal{M},g)$, we investigate the Klein-Gordon equation with Robin boundary conditions. First of all, we prove existence and uniqueness of the advanced and retarded fundamental solutions, discussing in addition their structural properties. Secondly, under the hypothesis of infinitesimal convexity of $\partial\mathcal{M}$ and of local finiteness of boundary reflections for broken null bicharacteristics, we characterize their wavefront set in the interior $\mathring{\mathcal{M}}$, using the propagation of singularities theorem of Melrose and Sjöstrand.
  In the second part of the paper, we introduce a notion of Robin-Hadamard two-point correlation function as a positive distribution on $\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}$ with prescribed antisymmetric part and singular structure, the latter being characterized in terms of its wavefront set. We complement this definition with a local Hadamard form. Up to a smooth remainder, this consists of the standard directed Hadamard parametrix together with additional contributions associated with the reflected null rays. Their coefficients are determined by transport equations supplemented by Robin matching conditions at the boundary. The construction applies to an arbitrary, but finite number of reflections. We prove that this local formulation is equivalent to the wavefront set condition, thereby establishing a counterpart of Radzikowski's theorem in the presence of a timelike boundary. Finally, starting from the ground state on suitable static backgrounds and using a deformation argument, we prove existence of Robin-Hadamard two-point correlation functions on the class of spacetimes considered.

</details>

### [169] [Scale-Vector Alignment: A Scale-Aware Framework for Spatially Resolved Morphological Similarity in Astronomical Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24304)
*Mengke Zhao,Guang-Xing Li,Keping Qiu,Shanghuo Li*

Main category: astro-ph.IM

TL;DR: The paper introduces scale-vector alignment, a scale-aware method based on Constrained Diffusion Decomposition (CDD) to compare the multiscale morphology of astronomical maps from different tracers, defining pixel-wise and scale-wise similarities and demonstrating applications in Orion A, NGC 6334I(N), and NGC 3627.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Astronomical maps made with different tracers are not expected to have identical morphology because excitation, optical depth, chemistry, radiation, and ISM phase alter tracer response, and these differences depend on both position and spatial scale. Existing methods lack a scale-aware way to quantify such tracer differences.

Method: CDD decomposes an image into localized scale components, whose amplitudes define a per-position scale vector describing intensity distribution over spatial scale. A pixel-wise similarity $\Spix$ is defined as normalized alignment of two local scale vectors, removing overall amplitude to compare relative scale composition. A scale-wise similarity $\Sscale(l)$ compares the two CDD component maps at each scale. Spatial shifts build an empirical shifted reference distribution for $\Spix$.

Result: In Orion A, the tracer most similar to the dust-derived column-density map shifts from $^{12}$CO to $^{13}$CO to C$^{18}$O toward higher column density. In NGC 6334I(N), line-continuum similarity decreases locally around the brightest compact structures due to radiative-transfer effects. In NGC 3627, CO is most similar to 21 $\mu$m emission, with $\Sscale$ peaking at an intermediate sub-kpc scale.

Conclusion: Scale-vector alignment measures where two tracers share similar multiscale structure and at which scales their spatial distributions agree. The implementation is publicly available at https://github.com/meng-ke/Scale-Vector-Alignment.

Abstract: Astronomical maps made with different tracers are not expected to have identical morphology. Excitation, optical depth, chemistry, radiation, and ISM phase alter the response of a tracer, and the resulting differences can depend on both position and spatial scale. We propose scale-vector alignment, a scale-aware method based on Constrained Diffusion Decomposition (CDD). CDD decomposes an image into localized scale components; at each position, their amplitudes define a scale vector that describes how the measured intensity is distributed over spatial scale. We define the pixel-wise similarity $\Spix(x,y)$ as the normalized alignment of two local scale vectors. The normalization removes the overall amplitude, so $\Spix$ compares relative scale composition rather than absolute flux. We also define the scale-wise similarity $\Sscale(l)$ by comparing the two CDD component maps at each spatial scale. Spatial shifts are used to construct an empirical shifted reference distribution for $\Spix$. In Orion~A, the tracer with the highest similarity to the dust-derived column-density map changes from $^{12}$CO to $^{13}$CO to C$^{18}$O toward higher column density. In NGC~6334I(N), the line--continuum similarity decreases locally around the brightest compact structures, where radiative-transfer effects can alter the observed line morphology. In NGC~3627, CO is most similar to 21~$μ$m emission, and $\Sscale$ reaches its maximum at an intermediate sub-kpc scale. The method measures where two tracers have similar multiscale structure and at which scales their spatial distributions agree. The implementation is publicly available at https://github.com/meng-ke/Scale-Vector-Alignment.

</details>

### [170] [Equivariant Neural Prediction of the Stokes Resistance Tensors for Arbitrary Microparticle Shapes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23105)
*Sanjay Pradeep,David Dandy,Candace S. J. Tsai,Jeff D. Eldredge*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: An SO(3)-equivariant neural network predicts the full 6x6 grand resistance matrix (A and C blocks) of irregular microparticles from spherical-harmonic surface representations, achieving ~1-3% error while running 4-5 orders of magnitude faster than a regularised-Stokeslet solver.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Empirical drag correlations compress the resistance tensors into a single scalar, discarding orientation dependence of drag and the rotational response. A faithful, fast tensor surrogate is needed for applications like atmospheric dust transport, microplastic fate, and colloidal Brownian dynamics.

Method: An SO(3)-equivariant neural network takes spherical-harmonic surface representations and predicts the full symmetric positive-definite A (translational) and C (rotational) blocks. Equivariance holds to floating-point precision. Trained on 1.1x10^5 random shapes (near-spherical to very rough), with a sealed test set of 18,000; labels generated by a regularised-Stokeslet solver. A spectral-convergence study validates the degree-15 spherical-harmonic representation.

Result: Achieves 1.4% and 2.7% mean relative error on the two blocks, evaluating 4-5 orders of magnitude faster than the solver. Over 1.16x10^6 orientation-sampled events, it reveals lateral drift up to 11 deg, rotational misalignment up to 46 deg, and shape-induced diffusion spreads of 30% (translational) and 2.4x (rotational), all zero under scalar or spheroid reductions. The orientation-averaged scalar friction is accurate to 1.7-2.8% (median), while the surrogate reproduces that scalar to ~1% and supplies full anisotropic tensors.

Conclusion: A fast, equivariant tensor surrogate can replace both the numerical solver and scalar approximations in transport, microplastic fate, and colloidal Brownian dynamics applications.

Abstract: The Stokes-flow hydrodynamics of an irregular microparticle is encoded by its grand resistance matrix, a 6x6 tensor whose translational and rotational blocks (A and C) govern settling, diffusion, and orientational transport. Empirical drag correlations compress these tensors to a single scalar, discarding drag's orientation dependence and the rotational response. We present an SO(3)-equivariant neural network that predicts the full symmetric positive-definite A and C blocks from a particle's spherical-harmonic surface representation, equivariant by construction to floating-point precision. Trained on 1.1x10^5 random shapes from near-spherical to very rough, with a sealed test set of 18,000, it achieves 1.4% and 2.7% mean relative error on the two blocks while evaluating each shape 4-5 orders of magnitude faster than the regularised-Stokeslet solver that generated its labels. A spectral-convergence study confirms the representation is faithful: truncating a shape at spherical-harmonic degree 15 changes its resistance by a median of 0.14% (translation) and 0.38% (rotation), while the training shapes, generated band-limited at degree 15, carry no truncation error. Across 1.16x10^6 orientation-sampled settling, rotation and diffusion events, the surrogate reveals lateral drift up to 11 deg, rotational misalignment up to 46 deg, and shape-induced diffusion spreads of 30% (translational) and 2.4x (rotational), all identically zero under any scalar or spheroid reduction. Even the orientation-averaged scalar friction the correlations target, accurate to 1.7-2.8% (median; 2.2-3.2% mean), carries no tensor orientation, whereas the surrogate reproduces that scalar to ~1% while supplying the full anisotropic tensors. A fast, equivariant tensor surrogate can replace both solver and scalar approximation in atmospheric dust transport, microplastic fate and colloidal Brownian dynamics.

</details>


### [171] [Wall-modelled large-eddy simulation of turbulent channel flow with unstable stratification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23311)
*Li-Sheng Jiang,Ao Xu,Heng-Dong Xi*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A buoyancy-modified logarithmic-quadratic wall model is developed for turbulent mixed convection (Poiseuille–Rayleigh–Bénard), validated via WMLES up to Re_τ≈6000 and Ra=10^10, achieving low velocity/temperature errors and large mesh savings over DNS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unstable thermal stratification alters near-wall momentum and heat transport, pushing the mean velocity profile away from the classical log law and making wall modelling difficult for turbulent mixed convection.

Method: The model couples an approximately linear near-wall mean-temperature/streamwise-velocity relation with a thermally modified mean-gradient representation inspired by mixing-length scaling. It is calibrated a priori against DNS wall quantities, then implemented in wall-modelled large-eddy simulations.

Result: WMLES (Re_τ up to ~6000, Ra up to 10^10) matches DNS at Ra=10^8,10^9 with max errors of 3.6% (velocity) and 1.9% (temperature). Global quantities deviate up to 11.7% (Nu) and 15.9% (C_f). Mesh counts drop by ~195–542× versus DNS; at Ra=10^10, Ri_b=0.1 DNS extrapolation needs ~10^11 cells, ~3 orders more than WMLES. Flow analysis reveals coexistence of VLSM-like streamwise-elongated motions and buoyancy-associated streamwise rolls.

Conclusion: The buoyancy-modified wall law enables accurate, highly efficient WMLES of turbulent mixed convection and clarifies how shear–buoyancy balance reorganises near-wall flow structures.

Abstract: Unstable thermal stratification modifies near-wall momentum and heat transport, causing the mean velocity profile to depart from the classical logarithmic law and complicating wall modelling for turbulent mixed convection. We develop a buoyancy-modified logarithmic-quadratic wall model for incompressible Poiseuille--Rayleigh--Bénard flow. The model combines an approximately linear relation between the near-wall mean temperature and mean streamwise velocity with a thermally modified mean-gradient representation inspired by mixing-length scaling. A priori assessments using wall quantities from the direct numerical simulation (DNS) database show that the calibrated wall law reconstructs near-wall velocity and wall-function-equivalent eddy-viscosity profiles. We implement the wall model in wall-modelled large-eddy simulations (WMLES) at friction Reynolds numbers up to $Re_τ\approx6000$ and Rayleigh numbers up to $Ra=10^{10}$. For cases with DNS reference profiles at $Ra=10^8$ and $10^9$, the maximum pointwise absolute relative errors are $3.6\%$ for the mean velocity and $1.9\%$ for the mean temperature. For cases with available DNS global-transport data, the maximum relative deviations in the Nusselt number $Nu$ and skin-friction coefficient $C_f$ are $11.7\%$ and $15.9\%$, respectively. The WMLES reduces the mesh count by factors of approximately $195$--$542$ relative to the corresponding DNS meshes. For the $Ra=10^{10}$ and $Ri_b=0.1$ case, extrapolation of reference DNS resolution strategies gives a mesh count of order $10^{11}$, approximately three orders of magnitude larger than the present WMLES mesh count. We also examine how the balance between shear and buoyancy reorganises flow structure, and we identify signatures consistent with the coexistence of streamwise-elongated motions resembling very-large-scale motions and buoyancy-associated streamwise rolls.

</details>


### [172] [Multiscale Kinetic Methods for Nonequilibrium Flow and Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24183)
*Zhaoli Guo,Kun Xu*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A review of multiscale kinetic methods that bridge rarefied, transitional, and continuum regimes, organized through four interacting layers: numerical methods, computational strategies, asymptotic properties, and framework-level formulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonequilibrium flow and transport are inherently multiscale. Continuum descriptions break down in parts of the domain, while fully resolved kinetic descriptions are prohibitively expensive because numerical resolution stays tied to the smallest collision scales. A unifying perspective is needed beyond existing reviews.

Method: Reviews the field through four interacting layers: numerical methods, computational strategies, asymptotic properties, and framework-level formulations. Emphasizes common principles: transport-interaction coupling, asymptotic consistency, scale-adaptive representation, and scale-dependent physical description.

Result: Surveys the main developments along these four layers, showing how they connect under shared principles. Positions multiscale kinetic computation as evolving into a broader transport methodology.

Conclusion: Multiscale kinetic computation has become a broader transport methodology for nonequilibrium systems across scales, with common principles linking numerical methods, computational strategies, asymptotic properties, and framework formulations.

Abstract: Nonequilibrium flow and transport problems are inherently multiscale. Kinetic theory provides a fundamental physical basis for describing such phenomena, since it connects microscopic transport and interaction processes with emergent macroscopic behavior across regimes. In many situations, however, continuum descriptions lose validity in parts of the domain, whereas fully resolved kinetic descriptions become prohibitively expensive when numerical resolution remains tied to the smallest collision scales. Over the past two decades, a broad class of multiscale kinetic methods has therefore been developed to bridge rarefied, transitional, and continuum regimes in gas dynamics and other carrier-based transport systems. Existing reviews have clarified important parts of this field, including general numerical methods for kinetic equations, asymptotic-preserving methodology, and specific method families. This review adopts a different perspective by examining the subject through four interacting layers: numerical methods, computational strategies, asymptotic properties, and framework-level formulations. It surveys the main developments along these lines and emphasizes the common principles that connect them, including transport-interaction coupling, asymptotic consistency, scale-adaptive representation, and scale-dependent physical description. From this perspective, multiscale kinetic computation has evolved into a broader transport methodology for nonequilibrium systems across scales.

</details>


### [173] [A high-order ghost-point immersed boundary method coupled with auxiliary differential equations for time-domain impedance walls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24785)
*Abdoulaye Ouattara*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A ghost-point immersed boundary method coupled with auxiliary differential equations imposes a locally-reacting impedance boundary condition on non-conforming walls within the linearized Euler equations, achieving high-order accuracy and improved stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imposing impedance (locally-reacting) boundary conditions on walls that do not conform to a Cartesian grid is challenging; existing immersed boundary methods struggle to combine high-order accuracy with time-domain impedance modeling and numerical stability.

Method: A ghost-point immersed boundary method coupled with auxiliary differential equations. Ghost-point reconstruction uses a normal-stencil approach: an elliptical least-squares cloud of fluid nodes builds a high-order polynomial along the local wall normal, extrapolated onto ghost points via 1D Lagrange interpolation. The impedance (mass-spring-damper oscillator model) is advanced in time using two auxiliary state variables per ghost point, coupled to the reconstruction at every Runge-Kutta stage.

Result: Validated against exact and semi-analytical references for a flat immersed wall, a cylindrical wall reflecting an acoustic pulse, and a cylinder scattering a harmonic monopole. Convergence orders ~3 on the curved wall and ~4.5 on the flat wall. A cross-check against an exact harmonic reference shows a persistent offset is a property of the semi-analytical reference, not the solver. Spectral stability analysis shows the normal-stencil reconstruction reduces, but does not eliminate, a known instability when the immersed wall becomes tangent to the grid.

Conclusion: The coupled ghost-point/auxiliary-equation approach provides a high-order, accurate, and relatively stable framework for locally-reacting impedance conditions on non-conforming walls in linearized Euler equations, though the tangency instability is only mitigated, not removed.

Abstract: A ghost-point immersed boundary method is coupled with auxiliary differential equations to impose a locally-reacting impedance condition on a wall that does not conform to a Cartesian grid, in the framework of the linearized Euler equations. The ghost-point reconstruction follows the normal-stencil approach: an elliptical least squares cloud of fluid nodes is used to build a high-order polynomial along the local wall normal, which is then extrapolated onto the ghost points by one-dimensional La grange interpolation. The impedance, modeled as a massspringdamper oscillator, is advanced in time through two auxiliary state variables per ghost point, coupled to the reconstruction at every RungeKutta stage. The method is validated against exact and semi-analytical references on a flat immersed wall, a cylindrical wall reflecting an acoustic pulse, and a cylinder scattering a harmonic monopole. Convergence orders ranging from about three on the curved wall to about four and a half on the flat wall are measured, and a cross-check against an exact harmonic reference shows that a persistent offset observed against a semi-analytical reference is a property of that ref erence rather than of the coupled solver. A spectral stability analysis of the linearized semi-discrete operator further shows that the normal-stencil reconstruction reduces, without eliminating, a known instability that occurs when the immersed wall becomes tangent to the grid.

</details>
